\frontmattersection{Review preview status}

This is a review preview, not a finished grammar. It is an assembled
draft of the current Tedim grammar sections.

Several domains remain incomplete, and end-of-section caveats remain
visible while the draft is still under review.

\subsection*{Tedim Chin-Specific Conventions}
\begin{tabular}{@{}ll@{}}
\textsc{1sg$\rightarrow$3} & first singular acting on third person \\
\textsc{2$\rightarrow$1} & second person acting on first person \\
\textsc{3$\rightarrow$1} & third person acting on first person \\
\textsc{ag} & agent (nominalizer) \\
\textsc{aug} & augmentative (big/great) \\
\textsc{dist} & distal/anaphoric demonstrative \\
\textsc{down} & downward directional \\
\textsc{hab.cont} & habitual continuative \\
\textsc{i} & verb form I (citation form) \\
\textsc{ii} & verb form II (dependent form) \\
\textsc{more} & comparative (more X) \\
\textsc{neg.abil} & negative abilitative (cannot) \\
\textsc{prox} & proximal demonstrative \\
\textsc{toward} & goal directional \\
\textsc{up} & upward directional \\
\end{tabular}

\vspace{1em}
\noindent\textbf{Verb Forms:} Tedim Chin verbs have two conjugation forms.
\textsc{i} (Form I) is the citation/independent form; \textsc{ii} (Form II)
appears in dependent clauses and certain constructions.

\reviewchapterbreak

\section{Phonology and tone}\label{phonology-and-tone}

\subsection{Phonology and tone}\label{phonology-and-tone-1}

The current phonology and tone section is deliberately cautious: the
literature supports a modest segmental summary, practical spelling is
only indirect evidence, and the tone-sensitive \tdim{-a} distinction
remains blocked.

\subsubsection{Overview of phonology and tone in
Tedim}\label{overview-of-phonology-and-tone-in-tedim}

Tedim phonology can be printed cautiously from the literature as a
modest segmental summary, an orthography note, and a three-tone system
that is not marked in ordinary spelling. This section stays narrow: it
prints what the literature supports now, keeps orthography in its proper
place as indirect evidence, and leaves the tone-sensitive \tdim{-a}
distinction unresolved
\citetext{\citealp[pp.~9-13]{henderson1965}; \citealp[pp.~25-26, 37,
57-60]{zamngaihcing2017}}.

The Bible orthography is useful for locating lexical items in context,
but the tone contrast itself is taken from the phonological literature,
since tone is not consistently represented in ordinary printed spelling.

\paragraph{Orientation table}\label{orientation-table}

\paragraph{Segmental phonology}\label{segmental-phonology}

The segmental literature converges on a conservative summary rather than
a maximal phoneme chart. Henderson describes a relatively small
initial-consonant system and a limited final set, while ZNC gives a
modernized IPA-based account with a conservative vowel inventory and a
simple syllable template
\citetext{\citealp[pp.~9-13]{henderson1965}; \citealp[pp.~25-26,
37]{zamngaihcing2017}}. That is enough for a grammar-facing orientation,
but not enough to pretend the segment inventory is fully settled in the
present section.

\paragraph{Orthography and syllable
shape}\label{orthography-and-syllable-shape}

Practical Tedim spelling is useful because it reflects familiar
segmental contrasts, yet it remains a spelling system. Henderson
explicitly notes that tone is not marked in orthography, and vowel
length is only occasionally shown by doubling. The safest print claim is
therefore that orthography helps orient the reader to segments and
syllable shape, but cannot by itself prove a phonological analysis
\citetext{\citealp[pp.~9-11,
13]{henderson1965}; \citealp[pp.~25-26]{zamngaihcing2017}}.

\paragraph{Tone status}\label{tone-status}

The tone literature supports a three-tone analysis, with tone
contrastive in lexical items and with analytical transcription marking
tone more explicitly than practical orthography does. The present
section can therefore print a short tone-status summary: Tedim has three
tones, tone is not marked in ordinary spelling, and tone interacts with
morphology in ways that belong partly in other sections
\citetext{\citealp[p.~13]{henderson1965}; \citealp[pp.~57-60]{zamngaihcing2017}}.

\paragraph{Bible-attested minimal and near-minimal
sets}\label{bible-attested-minimal-and-near-minimal-sets}

True orthography-identical tone minimal pairs are limited in the current
source-resolved Bible layer, so near-minimal sets are used where needed
and labeled explicitly. The \texttt{ta\ /\ -ta} row is kept as
supporting Bible attestation rather than strict minimal-pair evidence.

\paragraph{Short Bible examples}\label{short-bible-examples}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:phon-thei-abil-matt7}
\gll \tdimword{lutthei} \tdimword{ding} \tdimword{uh} \tdimword{hi} \\
     enter-\textsc{abil} \textsc{irr} 2/3PL \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{they will be able to enter} (Matthew 7:21)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:phon-hi-lex-gram-gen16}
\gll \tdimword{na} \tdimword{hi} \tdimword{hi} \\
     \textsc{2sg} \textsc{decl} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{you are} (Genesis 16:13)
\end{exe}

These examples show Bible attestation of the forms in context. The tone
assignment remains literature-backed, because ordinary Bible spelling
does not mark tone contrast reliably. Short Bible examples are printed
in ordinary Bible spelling, and tone-marked forms are restricted to the
literature column in the table above.

\paragraph{The blocked -a issue}\label{the-blocked--a-issue}

The main unresolved issue is the tone-sensitive \tdim{-a} distinction.
The literature distinguishes high-tone and low-tone values, but the
current corpus spelling does not preserve tone, so this contrast cannot
be recovered safely. For present purposes, the right grammar-facing
decision is to keep \tdim{-a} blocked and describe the issue explicitly
as unresolved rather than flattening it into a single case marker
\citep[pp.~57-60]{zamngaihcing2017}.

\paragraph{\texorpdfstring{Boundaries with stem alternation, TAM,
\tdim{-pih}, and verb
paradigms}{Boundaries with stem alternation, TAM, , and verb paradigms}}\label{boundaries-with-stem-alternation-tam-and-verb-paradigms}

Tone interacts with stem alternation, TAM / aspect / modal, \tdim{-pih}
\glossquote{with / accompanying}, and verb paradigms, but those are
better printed in their own sections. This section therefore only marks
the boundary: it does not attempt to sort out the full tone-sensitive
behavior of suffixes or other unresolved tone-sensitive material that
stays outside this section.

\paragraph{Deferred material}\label{deferred-material}

A full phoneme table, a full tone-sandhi account, and a complete tone
analysis remain deferred. The \texttt{ta\ /\ -ta} contrast remains
caveated as supporting evidence rather than as a strict minimal pair.

\paragraph{What can be printed now}\label{what-can-be-printed-now}

What can be printed now is a short segmental orientation, an orthography
caveat, a three-tone status summary, two or three Bible-attested minimal
or near-minimal sets with literature-backed tone interpretation, and a
blocked -a warning.

\reviewchapterbreak

\section{Deixis, pronouns, and nominal
domain}\label{deixis-pronouns-and-nominal-domain}

\subsection{Demonstratives / deixis}\label{demonstratives-deixis}

The current demonstrative evidence is strongest for core \tdim{hih}
\glossquote{this} and \tdim{tua} \glossquote{that / the aforementioned},
plus plural \tdim{hihte} \glossquote{these} and \tdim{tuate}
\glossquote{those}, while \tdim{hi} and exact \tdim{hih ciangin}
\glossquote{when this / when doing thus} remain boundary material.

\subsubsection{Overview of demonstratives and deixis in this
section}\label{overview-of-demonstratives-and-deixis-in-this-section}

This section treats the demonstrative core \tdim{hih} \glossquote{this}
and \tdim{tua} \glossquote{that / the aforementioned}, their plural
forms, and a small set of constructional extensions. It leaves copular
and sentence-final material around \tdim{hi}
\glossquote{be / DECL / copula-like}, and broader discourse-structure
analysis, to other sections.

The basic two-way contrast is stable in both corpus and literature
\citep{henderson1965, zamngaihcing2017}. A standing caveat is that Bible
usage is dominated by \tdim{tua} \glossquote{that / the aforementioned},
while the literature often cites \tdim{huā}
\glossquote{distal demonstrative}; the relation is plausible but not
settled here.

Raw occurrence counts are not treated as grammar facts in this section.

\paragraph{Current demonstrative
inventory}\label{current-demonstrative-inventory}

\tdim{hih} & proximal demonstrative & identificational and adnominal &

& stable & treated as DEM + \tdim{-te} \\
\tdim{tuate} & distal plural demonstrative & pronominal and NP-linked &
stable & treated as DEM + \tdim{-te} \\
\tdim{hih bangin} & manner/deictic construction & instruction or quoted
manner & stable with caveat & interface with clause-linkage uses of
\tdim{bangin} \\
\tdim{tua bangin} & manner/discourse resumptive construction & anaphoric
summary or continuation & stable with caveat & interface with
clause-linkage uses of \tdim{bangin} \\
\tdim{tua ciangin} & temporal/discourse linker & event sequencing
(\glossquote{then}) & stable & boundary with clause-linkage chapter \\

clause-linkage and discourse domains \\

\paragraph{Core demonstratives: hih and
tua}\label{core-demonstratives-hih-and-tua}

The core contrast is between \tdim{hih} \glossquote{this} and \tdim{tua}
\glossquote{that / the aforementioned}. In controlled examples,
\tdim{hih} is robust in proximal or presentational contexts, while
\tdim{tua} regularly tracks already activated or discourse-linked
referents.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:dem-hih}
\gll \tdimword{Híh} \tdimword{pèn} \tdimword{Adam'} \tdimword{suanlekhakte'} \tdimword{laibu} \tdimword{àhì} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{prox} \textsc{top} \textsc{adam}.\textsc{poss} genealogy-\textsc{pl}.\textsc{poss} book be.\textsc{3sg} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{This is the book of the generations of Adam.} (Genesis 5:1)
\end{exe}

Matthew 1:21 provides a Gospel comparandum with adnominal \tdim{tua}
\glossquote{that / the aforementioned}.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:dem-tua}
\gll \tdimword{túa} \tdimword{tá-pà} \tdimword{ìn} \tdimword{àmà} \tdimword{mì-tè} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{màwh-ná} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{pàn-ìn} \tdimword{honkhia} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{àhìh} \tdimword{màn-ìn} \\
     \textsc{dist} child-male \textsc{erg} \textsc{3sg}.\textsc{poss} person-\textsc{pl} \textsc{3sg} err-\textsc{nmlz} 2/3PL \textsc{abl}-\textsc{erg} deliver \textsc{irr} be.\textsc{3sg}.\textsc{rel} reason-\textsc{erg} \\
\glt \glossquote{Because that child will save his people from their sins.} (Matthew 1:21)
\end{exe}

\paragraph{Plural demonstratives}\label{plural-demonstratives}

Plural forms are straightforwardly built on demonstrative stems plus
\tdim{-te} \glossquote{plural}. The controlled evidence supports both
\tdim{hihte} \glossquote{these} and \tdim{tuate} \glossquote{those} as
regular plural demonstrative forms.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:dem-hihte}
\gll \tdimword{Híh-tè} \tdimword{pèn} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{ínn-kúan,} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{kam} \tdimword{pau,} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{léi-tāng,} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{mì-nām-ìn} \tdimword{Ham'} \tdimword{suanlekhak-tè} \tdimword{àhì} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{prox}-\textsc{pl} \textsc{top} \textsc{3sg} house-family \textsc{3sg} mouth speak \textsc{3sg} land-earth \textsc{3sg} person-tribe-\textsc{erg} \textsc{ham}.\textsc{poss} genealogy-\textsc{pl} be.\textsc{3sg} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{These are the descendants of Ham by their families, languages, lands, and nations.} (Genesis 10:20)
\end{exe}

Luke 6:14 gives a Gospel comparandum with distal plural \tdim{tuate}
\glossquote{those}.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:dem-tuate}
\gll \tdimword{Túa-tè} \tdimword{ìn:} \tdimword{Simon} \tdimword{peter} \tdimword{cí-ìn} \tdimword{mīn} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{phuah} \tdimword{pà.} \\
     \textsc{dist}-\textsc{pl} \textsc{erg} \textsc{simon} \textsc{peter} say-\textsc{quot} name \textsc{3sg} thunder father \\
\glt \glossquote{Those were: Simon (called Peter)...} (Luke 6:14)
\end{exe}

\paragraph{Adnominal and pronominal
uses}\label{adnominal-and-pronominal-uses}

The same demonstrative forms can modify nouns or stand independently.
Adnominal use is clear in forms such as \tdim{hih mite}
\glossquote{these people}, while pronominal use is clear in standalone
predicate frames.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:dem-adnominal-hih}
\gll \tdimword{Híh} \tdimword{mì-tè} \tdimword{kà} \tdimword{mú} \tdimword{zò} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{prox} person-\textsc{pl} \textsc{1sg} see.\textsc{i} south \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{I have seen these people.} (Exodus 32:9)
\end{exe}

Matthew 12:49 gives a Gospel pronominal comparandum.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:dem-pronominal-hihte}
\gll \tdimword{Híh-tè} \tdimword{pèn} \tdimword{kà} \tdimword{nù} \tdimword{lè} \tdimword{kà} \tdimword{sanggam-tè} \tdimword{àhì} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{prox}-\textsc{pl} \textsc{top} \textsc{1sg} female and \textsc{1sg} brother-\textsc{pl} be.\textsc{3sg} 2/3PL \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{These are my mother and my brothers.} (Matthew 12:49)
\end{exe}

\paragraph{Discourse and temporal
deixis}\label{discourse-and-temporal-deixis}

Discourse-temporal uses are centered on \tdim{tua ciangin}
\glossquote{then / at that time} and \tdim{tua ahih ciangin}
\glossquote{when that was so / then}. These constructions are treated
here as demonstrative-linked discourse frames, while fuller
subordination analysis is left to clause linkage.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:dem-tua-ciangin}
\gll \tdimword{túa} \tdimword{cìang-ìn} \tdimword{khuavak} \tdimword{ōm} \tdimword{pah} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{dist} then-\textsc{erg} light exist do.so \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{Then light appeared at once.} (Genesis 1:3)
\end{exe}

Matthew 28:19 gives a Gospel comparandum for \tdim{tua ahih ciangin}.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:dem-tua-ahih-ciangin}
\gll \tdimword{Túa} \tdimword{àhìh} \tdimword{cìang-ìn} \tdimword{nòtè} \tdimword{pái} \tdimword{ùn-lá.} \\
     \textsc{dist} be.\textsc{3sg}.\textsc{rel} then-\textsc{erg} \textsc{2pl}.\textsc{pro} go \textsc{pl}.\textsc{imp}-and \\
\glt \glossquote{When that is so, you must go...} (Matthew 28:19)
\end{exe}

\paragraph{Manner constructions with
bangin}\label{manner-constructions-with-bangin}

Manner constructions with \tdim{bangin} \glossquote{like / as} are
productive with demonstrative bases: \tdim{hih bangin}
\glossquote{like this / thus} and \tdim{tua bangin}
\glossquote{like that / thus}. These are kept in this section because
the demonstrative base remains constructionally active.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:dem-hih-bangin}
\gll \tdimword{híh} \tdimword{bàng-ìn} \tdimword{nà} \tdimword{ngen} \tdimword{ùn} \\
     \textsc{prox} like-\textsc{erg} \textsc{2sg} pray \textsc{imp}.\textsc{pl} \\
\glt \glossquote{Pray like this.} (Matthew 6:9)
\end{exe}

Exodus 14:30 gives a controlled Old Testament comparandum with
\tdim{tua bangin}.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:dem-tua-bangin}
\reviewwarninginline{[REVIEW PREVIEW WARNING: ex:dem-tua-bangin: Bible reference mapped, but slice example is not a direct verse span; analyzed slice wording]}
\gll \tdimword{Topa} \tdimword{ìn} \tdimword{túa} \tdimword{bàng-ìn} \tdimword{Egypt} \tdimword{mite'} \tdimword{khutsung} \tdimword{pàn-ìn} \tdimword{Israel-té} \tdimword{honkhia} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     Lord \textsc{erg} \textsc{dist} like-\textsc{erg} \textsc{egypt} person-\textsc{pl}.\textsc{poss} palm \textsc{abl}-\textsc{erg} \textsc{israel}-\textsc{te} deliver \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{Thus the Lord saved Israel from the hand of the Egyptians.} (Exodus 14:30)
\end{exe}

\paragraph{\texorpdfstring{Deferred forms: \tdim{hi}
\glossquote{be / DECL / copula-like} and \tdim{hih ciangin}
\glossquote{when this / when doing thus}}{Deferred forms:   and  }}\label{deferred-forms-and}

\tdim{Hi} is kept as deferred boundary material. In current evidence it
overlaps heavily with copular, auxiliary, and sentence-final-particle
behavior, so it is not promoted as a stable demonstrative row here.

\tdim{Hih ciangin} \glossquote{when this / when doing thus} is also
deferred. Current controlled evidence does not support it as a stable
temporal demonstrative counterpart to \tdim{tua ciangin}, and many
apparent hits overlap with non-demonstrative verbal material.

\paragraph{Boundary with interrogatives and
quantifiers}\label{boundary-with-interrogatives-and-quantifiers}

Forms such as \tdim{kua} \glossquote{who}, \tdim{bang}
\glossquote{what}, \tdim{kuamah} \glossquote{nobody}, and \tdim{bangmah}
\glossquote{nothing} belong primarily to interrogative and
quantifier/negative-indefinite domains, so they are not expanded in this
section.

\paragraph{Deferred material}\label{deferred-material-1}

Several issues remain outside the present account. These include the
full \tdim{hi} system across copular and sentence-final uses, full
discourse-structural deixis, and the unresolved scope of Bible
\tdim{tua} versus literature \tdim{huā}.

\subsection{Pronouns / clusivity}\label{pronouns-clusivity}

The current pronoun evidence is strongest for independent forms such as
\tdim{kei} \glossquote{I / me}, \tdim{nang} \glossquote{you.SG}, and
\tdim{amah} \glossquote{he / she / it}, while first-plural clusivity
(\tdim{eite} \glossquote{we} / \tdim{kote} \glossquote{we.EXCL}) and
participant-oriented prefixes (\tdim{hong-}
\glossquote{participant-oriented venitive-like marker}, \tdim{kong-}
\glossquote{participant-oriented directive-like marker}) remain tightly
controlled boundary material.

\subsubsection{Overview of pronouns and pronominal marking in this
section}\label{overview-of-pronouns-and-pronominal-marking-in-this-section}

This section describes independent personal pronouns and closely related
pronominal marking. It keeps demonstratives, interrogatives,
quantifiers, and the wider verbal agreement system in their own
sections.

The stable independent forms include \tdim{kei} \glossquote{I / me},
\tdim{nang} \glossquote{you.SG}, \tdim{amah} \glossquote{he / she / it},
\tdim{eite} \glossquote{we}, \tdim{kote} \glossquote{we}, \tdim{note}
\glossquote{you.PL}, and \tdim{amaute} \glossquote{they}. The treatment
of first-person plural distinctions follows cautious grammar-facing
practice: \tdim{kote} can be treated as an exclusive form in the
controlled evidence, while \tdim{eite} shows inclusive uses but is not
assigned one global label here \citep{henderson1965, zamngaihcing2017}.

\paragraph{Current personal-pronoun
inventory}\label{current-personal-pronoun-inventory}

{\def\LTcaptype{none} % do not increment counter
\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Person
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Singular
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Plural
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Note or caveat
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
First & kei & eite, kote & \tdim{eite} is often inclusive; \tdim{kote}
is often exclusive in controlled examples \\
Second & nang & note & Number contrast is stable in the controlled
examples \\
Third & amah & amaute & Third-person contrast is stable for independent
forms \\
\end{longtable}
}

\paragraph{Independent personal
pronouns}\label{independent-personal-pronouns}

Independent pronouns occur freely as noun-phrase heads. The third-person
form \tdim{amah} \glossquote{he / she / it} and the second-person plural
form \tdim{note} \glossquote{you.PL} illustrate ordinary clause-level
use.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:pro-amah}
\gll \tdimword{àmàh} \tdimword{pèn} \tdimword{mì-hìng} \tdimword{khèmpeuh'} \tdimword{nù} \tdimword{àhì} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{3sg}.\textsc{pro} \textsc{top} person-kind all.\textsc{poss} female be.\textsc{3sg} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{She was the mother of all living people.} (Genesis 3:20)
\end{exe}

Matthew 5:13 supplies a Gospel comparandum with second-person plural
\tdim{note} \glossquote{you.PL}.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:pro-note}
\gll \tdimword{Nòtè} \tdimword{pèn} \tdimword{léi-tùng} \tdimword{mì-tè} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{dìng-ìn} \tdimword{cí} \tdimword{tàwh} \tdimword{nà} \tdimword{kī-bàng} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{2pl}.\textsc{pro} \textsc{top} land-on person-\textsc{pl} \textsc{3sg} \textsc{irr}-\textsc{erg} say \textsc{com} \textsc{2sg} \textsc{refl}-same 2/3PL \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{You are like salt for the people of the world.} (Matthew 5:13)
\end{exe}

\paragraph{First-person plural forms and
clusivity}\label{first-person-plural-forms-and-clusivity}

The controlled evidence supports a cautious contrast between \tdim{eite}
\glossquote{we} and \tdim{kote} \glossquote{we.EXCL}. In these data,
\tdim{kote} behaves like an exclusive first-person plural, while
\tdim{eite} has inclusive uses but is not forced into a single global
value for all contexts.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:pro-eite}
\gll \tdimword{Bàng} \tdimword{hàng} \tdimword{hìam} \tdimword{cìh} \tdimword{lèh} \tdimword{eite} \tdimword{beh} \tdimword{khàt} \tdimword{ì-hí} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     like reason \textsc{q} say.\textsc{nom} and \textsc{1pl}.\textsc{pro} tribe one \textsc{1pl}.\textsc{incl}-be \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{Because we are one kin-group.} (Genesis 13:8)
\end{exe}

Genesis 34:9 gives the complementary row with \tdim{kote}
\glossquote{we.EXCL}.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:pro-kote}
\gll \tdimword{Kòtè} \tdimword{tàwh} \tdimword{kī-ten-ná} \tdimword{hòng} \tdimword{bāwl} \tdimword{ùn.} \\
     \textsc{1pl}.\textsc{pro} \textsc{com} \textsc{refl}-marry-\textsc{nmlz} \textsc{3→1} make \textsc{imp}.\textsc{pl} \\
\glt \glossquote{Arrange intermarriage with us.} (Genesis 34:9)
\end{exe}

No equally clean Gospel example is currently used for this construction
because no Gospel token of \tdim{eite} or \tdim{kote} matches the same
clusivity contrast at comparable quality.

\paragraph{Possessive prefixes as pronominal
material}\label{possessive-prefixes-as-pronominal-material}

Possessive prefixes are directly related to person marking and belong in
a compact pronoun-facing treatment: \tdim{ka-}
\glossquote{first-person singular possessive}, \tdim{na-}
\glossquote{second-person singular possessive}, \tdim{a-}
\glossquote{third-person possessive}, and \tdim{i-}
\glossquote{first-person plural possessive}. Full paradigm structure and
verbal agreement behavior are handled in the separate agreement and
NP-possession sections.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:poss-na}
\gll \tdimword{Nà} \tdimword{pa'} \tdimword{ínn-àh} \tdimword{kòtè'} \tdimword{giah} \tdimword{nàdìng} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{áwng} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{hìam?} \\
     \textsc{2sg} male.\textsc{poss} house-\textsc{loc} \textsc{1pl}.\textsc{pro}.\textsc{poss} camp \textsc{purp} \textsc{3sg} open \textsc{irr} \textsc{q} \\
\glt \glossquote{Is there room in your father's house for us to stay?} (Genesis 24:23)
\end{exe}

Luke 2:49 supplies a Gospel row with \tdim{ka-}
\glossquote{first-person singular possessive} in a nominal host phrase.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:poss-ka-pa-inn}
\gll \tdimword{Kà} \tdimword{Pa'} \tdimword{ínn} \tdimword{sùng-àh} \tdimword{kà} \tdimword{ōm} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{lamtak} \tdimword{théi} \tdimword{lò} \tdimword{nà} \tdimword{hì} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{hìam?} \\
     \textsc{1sg} male.\textsc{poss} house inside-\textsc{loc} \textsc{1sg} exist \textsc{irr} know know \textsc{neg} \textsc{2sg} \textsc{decl} 2/3PL \textsc{q} \\
\glt \glossquote{Did you not know that I should be in my Father's house?} (Luke 2:49)
\end{exe}

Genesis 3:20 also gives a third-person possessive prefix with \tdim{a-}
\glossquote{third-person possessive}.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:poss-a}
\gll \tdimword{Mipa} \tdimword{ìn} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{zi'} \tdimword{mīn} \tdimword{pèn} \tdimword{Eve,} \tdimword{cí} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     man \textsc{erg} \textsc{3sg} wife.\textsc{poss} name \textsc{top} \textsc{eve} say \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{The man called his wife's name Eve.} (Genesis 3:20)
\end{exe}

\paragraph{Emphatic pronouns in -mah}\label{emphatic-pronouns-in--mah}

An emphatic element \tdim{-mah} \glossquote{self / emphatic} combines
with pronoun bases, including \tdim{keimah} \glossquote{I myself} and
\tdim{nangmah} \glossquote{you yourself}. Forms such as \tdim{kuamah}
\glossquote{nobody} and \tdim{bangmah} \glossquote{nothing} are treated
primarily in quantifier and negative-indefinite domains.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:emph-keimah}
\gll \tdimword{Kèimah} \tdimword{gìm} \tdimword{hòng} \tdimword{kī-pìak-ná,} \tdimword{kà} \tdimword{thùak} \tdimword{zàwh} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{hì} \tdimword{lò} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{1sg}.\textsc{pro} suffering \textsc{3→1} \textsc{refl}-give.to-\textsc{nmlz} \textsc{1sg} suffer able \textsc{irr} \textsc{decl} \textsc{neg} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{My punishment is more than I can bear.} (Genesis 4:13)
\end{exe}

Matthew 8:22 supplies a Gospel emphatic comparandum with \tdim{nangmah}
\glossquote{you yourself}.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:emph-nangmah}
\gll \tdimword{Nangmah} \tdimword{ìn} \tdimword{kèi} \tdimword{hòng} \tdimword{zúi} \tdimword{ìn.} \\
     \textsc{2sg}.\textsc{pro} \textsc{erg} \textsc{neg} \textsc{3→1} follow \textsc{erg} \\
\glt \glossquote{You yourself, follow me.} (Matthew 8:22)
\end{exe}

\paragraph{Reflexive and reciprocal ki-
boundary}\label{reflexive-and-reciprocal-ki--boundary}

The prefix \tdim{ki-} \glossquote{reflexive / reciprocal / middle-like}
is a boundary topic between pronoun-sensitive participant structure and
broader valency behavior. A clear reciprocal predicate is \tdim{ki-gawm}
\glossquote{join together}.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:refl-ki}
\gll \tdimword{à} \tdimword{zi} \tdimword{tàwh} \tdimword{kī-gawm} \tdimword{à,} \tdimword{àmau} \tdimword{tegel} \tdimword{pum} \tdimword{khàt} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{bàng} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{3sg} wife \textsc{com} \textsc{refl}-seize \textsc{3sg} \textsc{3pl}.\textsc{pro} both body one \textsc{3sg} like 2/3PL \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{He is joined with his wife, and the two become one body.} (Genesis 2:24)
\end{exe}

Matthew 19:5 supplies a Gospel row with the same reciprocal predicate.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:refl-ki-matt19}
\gll \tdimword{à} \tdimword{zi} \tdimword{tàwh} \tdimword{kī-gawm} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{à,} \tdimword{àmau} \tdimword{tegel} \tdimword{pumkhat} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{bàng} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{3sg} wife \textsc{com} \textsc{refl}-seize \textsc{irr} \textsc{3sg} \textsc{3pl}.\textsc{pro} both body \textsc{3sg} like \textsc{irr} 2/3PL \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{He will be joined with his wife, and the two will become one body.} (Matthew 19:5)
\end{exe}

\paragraph{Participant-oriented hong- and kong-
boundary}\label{participant-oriented-hong--and-kong--boundary}

The prefixes \tdim{hong-}
\glossquote{participant-oriented venitive-like marker} and \tdim{kong-}
\glossquote{participant-oriented directive-like marker} are included
here only as boundary material. Their full status in inverse,
directionality, and agreement-related analyses is left for dedicated
sections.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:hong-prefix}
\reviewwarninginline{[REVIEW PREVIEW WARNING: ex:hong-prefix: Bible reference mapped, but slice example is not a direct verse span; analyzed slice wording]}
\gll \tdimword{Topa} \tdimword{àw,} \tdimword{cí-kin} \tdimword{nà} \tdimword{gil-kial} \tdimword{hòng} \tdimword{mú-ìn} \tdimword{àn} \tdimword{hòng} \tdimword{pía} \tdimword{kà} \tdimword{hì} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{hìam?} \\
     Lord voice say-half \textsc{2sg} bowels \textsc{3→1} see-\textsc{erg} \textsc{3pl}.\textsc{poss} \textsc{3→1} give \textsc{1sg} \textsc{decl} 2/3PL \textsc{q} \\
\glt \glossquote{Lord, when did we see you hungry and give you food?} (Matthew 25:37)
\end{exe}

Genesis 41:41 supplies an Old Testament comparandum for \tdim{kong-}
\glossquote{participant-oriented directive-like marker}.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:kong-prefix}
\gll \tdimword{Egypt} \tdimword{gàm} \tdimword{khèmpeuh} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{ùk} \tdimword{dìng-ìn} \tdimword{nàng} \tdimword{kòng} \tdimword{kòih} \tdimword{khin-zò} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{egypt} land all \textsc{3sg} rule \textsc{irr}-\textsc{erg} \textsc{2sg}.\textsc{pro} \textsc{1sg→3} put \textsc{compl}-\textsc{compl} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{I have placed you over all the land of Egypt.} (Genesis 41:41)
\end{exe}

\paragraph{Boundary with demonstratives, interrogatives, quantifiers,
and wider
agreement}\label{boundary-with-demonstratives-interrogatives-quantifiers-and-wider-agreement}

This section does not treat demonstratives such as \tdim{hih}
\glossquote{this} and \tdim{tua} \glossquote{that}, interrogatives such
as \tdim{kua} \glossquote{who}, quantifier-like negatives such as
\tdim{kuamah} \glossquote{nobody} and \tdim{bangmah}
\glossquote{nothing}, or the full prefixal agreement system.

\paragraph{Deferred material}\label{deferred-material-2}

Several issues remain outside the present account. These include full
clusivity-system stabilization, the full scope of \tdim{ki-} across
valency domains, and full analysis of \tdim{hong-} and \tdim{kong-}
across agreement and directional systems.

\subsection{NP structure / possession}\label{np-structure-possession}

The current evidence supports demonstrative-before-noun order alongside
noun-plus-postnominal numeral and quantifier patterns, with possession
kept as a cautious boundary subsection.

\subsubsection{Overview of noun phrase
structure}\label{overview-of-noun-phrase-structure}

The clearest current evidence supports a small but coherent set of
NP-order observations. Demonstrative-related material is safest where a
proximal form precedes the noun, as in \tdim{hih mite}
\glossquote{these people}, with \tdim{hih} \glossquote{this} introducing
the phrase. Numerals and several quantifier-like forms follow the noun
in the cleanest current examples, as seen in \tdim{mi khat}
\glossquote{one person / a person}, \tdim{ni li} \glossquote{four days},
\tdim{mi khempeuh} \glossquote{all people}, \tdim{mi pawlkhat}
\glossquote{some people}, and \tdim{mi tampi} \glossquote{many people}.
The present section therefore supports a modest contrast between
demonstrative-before-noun structure and noun-plus-postnominal numeral or
quantifier structure, while leaving broader NP templating for later
review.

Possession is now visible enough to include as part of the same section,
but it remains less normalized than the modifier-order material. The
safest checked rows show possessor-before-possessed structure in compact
noun phrases such as \tdim{na pa' inn-ah}
\glossquote{in your father's house} and \tdim{a zi' min}
\glossquote{his wife's name}, while the analysis of possessive prefixes,
apostrophe marking, genitive structure, and relator or case overlap
still requires explicit caveats.

Gospel searches produced clean noun-plus-numeral and
noun-plus-quantifier examples, which helps keep the section from
becoming Genesis-only. Demonstratives and possession remain more OT-led
in the current stage because the Gospel comparanda were less clean under
present careful evidence control.

\subsubsection{Current NP pattern
inventory}\label{current-np-pattern-inventory}

{\def\LTcaptype{none} % do not increment counter
\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Pattern
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Example form
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Rough function
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Status in this draft
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Main boundary issue
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
demonstrative + noun & \tdim{hih mite} & proximal demonstrative before a
plural noun & well-supported & Gospel demonstrative comparanda are less
clean than the OT anchor \\
noun + numeral / indefinite boundary & \tdim{mi khat} & noun followed by
\tdim{khat} as numeral or indefinite-like modifier & supported with
caveat & overlaps with the numerals and indefinites boundary \\
noun + numeral & \tdim{ni li} & counted noun followed by a simple
numeral & well-supported & numeral formation belongs to the numerals
section \\
noun + numeral & \tdim{kum sawm le nih} & counted noun followed by a
compound numeral & well-supported & compound-ten analysis belongs to the
numerals section \\
noun + quantifier & \tdim{mi khempeuh} & noun followed by a total
quantifier & well-supported & full quantifier semantics belong to the
quantifiers section \\
noun + quantifier & \tdim{mi pawlkhat} & noun followed by an existential
or partitive-like quantifier & supported with caveat & grouping
semantics remain caveated \\
noun + quantifier & \tdim{mi tampi} & noun followed by a quantity
modifier & supported with caveat & borders on broader degree or
adjective-like modification \\
possessor + possessed NP & \tdim{na pa' inn-ah} & pronominal possessor
with possessed noun phrase & supported with caveat & overlaps with
possessive prefixes, apostrophe analysis, and case closure \\
possessed NP chain & \tdim{a zi' min} & possessor-marked noun phrase
containing a possessed noun & supported with caveat & does not yet
justify a full possession paradigm \\
\end{longtable}
}

\subsubsection{Demonstratives and nouns}\label{demonstratives-and-nouns}

The clearest checked demonstrative-related anchor remains
\tdim{hih mite}, which safely supports a demonstrative-before-noun
description for the current evidence. That is a narrower and safer claim
than a full theory of determiner position. The demonstratives section
remains the right place for the broader deictic inventory; the present
section only uses the NP row to show that demonstrative-related
modification does not pattern like the postnominal numeral and
quantifier rows.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:np-hih-mite}
\gll \tdimword{híh} \tdimword{mì-tè} \\
     \textsc{prox} person-\textsc{pl} \\
\glt \glossquote{these people} (Exodus 5:5)
\end{exe}

Matthew 6:2 gives a Gospel comparandum in \tdim{a hih mite}, but that
row is embedded in a larger relative-like phrase and is therefore less
clean as a simple adnominal demonstrative anchor. For present purposes,
the OT phrase above remains the better controlled example.

No equally clean Gospel demonstrative example is currently used here, so
the subsection stays with the compact OT anchor while the embedded
Gospel comparandum remains only a supporting note.

\subsubsection{Numerals and nouns}\label{numerals-and-nouns}

The normalized numerals section already carries the fuller description
of the numeral system, including compound tens and larger-number
expressions. The current NP section uses numeral material only to
support noun-plus-numeral order: the cleanest checked rows place the
noun before the numeral rather than the numeral before the noun.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:np-ni-li}
\gll \tdimword{nì} \tdimword{li} \\
     day four \\
\glt \glossquote{four days} (John 11:39)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:np-kum-sawm-le-nih}
\gll \tdimword{kúm} \tdimword{sàwm} \tdimword{lè} \tdimword{nìh} \\
     year ten and two \\
\glt \glossquote{twelve years} (Matthew 9:20)
\end{exe}

These rows show that the pattern is not tied to a single lexical head.
\tdim{Ni li} \glossquote{four days} and \tdim{kum sawm le nih}
\glossquote{twelve years} both keep the noun before the numeral, even
when the numeral itself is compound. At the same time, \tdim{mi khat}
\glossquote{one person / a person} remains an explicit boundary row,
since the present section uses it as NP-order evidence without resolving
the full numeral versus indefinite analysis that belongs to the numerals
and quantifier-adjacent discussion.

No equally clean Old Testament example is currently used for this
construction, so the section keeps the compact Gospel anchors.

\subsubsection{Quantifiers and nouns}\label{quantifiers-and-nouns}

The quantifiers section already carries the fuller discussion of total,
existential-like, and negative-licensed quantifier material. Here the
relevant point is more limited: the cleanest checked quantifier rows
also place the noun before the quantifier-like item.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:np-mi-khempeuh}
\gll \tdimword{mì} \tdimword{khèmpeuh} \\
     person all \\
\glt \glossquote{all people} (Luke 2:1)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:np-mi-pawlkhat}
\gll \tdimword{mì} \tdimword{pàwl-khàt} \\
     person some-one \\
\glt \glossquote{some people} (Matthew 2:1)
\end{exe}

\tdim{Mi pawlkhat} and \tdim{mi tampi} (Mark 6:34) support the same
general noun-plus-quantifier profile, although each keeps its own
semantic caveats from the normalized quantifiers section. The current
section therefore uses them as modest NP-order evidence rather than as a
reopened quantifier chapter.

No equally clean Old Testament example is currently used for this
construction, so the section keeps the compact Gospel anchors.

\subsubsection{Possession}\label{possession}

Possession is now printable in a cautious way, but it remains less
normalized than the modifier-order material above. The safest current
claim is that the checked possession rows show
possessor-before-possessed order inside the noun phrase. They do not yet
justify a full paradigm of possessive prefixes, a settled treatment of
apostrophe marking, or a broad theory of alienable versus inalienable
possession.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:np-poss-na-pa-inn}
\gll \tdimword{nà} \tdimword{pa'} \tdimword{ínn-àh} \\
     \textsc{2sg} male.\textsc{poss} house-\textsc{loc} \\
\glt \glossquote{in thy father's house} (Genesis 24:23)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:np-poss-a-zi-min}
\gll \tdimword{à} \tdimword{zi'} \tdimword{mīn} \\
     \textsc{3sg} wife.\textsc{poss} name \\
\glt \glossquote{his wife's name} (Genesis 3:20)
\end{exe}

These rows are enough for a cautious description of
possessor-before-possessed structure, especially when read together with
the main anchors \tdim{ka pa}, \tdim{Topa' inn}, and \tdim{a pa' inn}.
They are not enough for a full possession paradigm, because the current
evidence still overlaps with pronouns and possessive prefixes, genitive
or apostrophe analysis, relator and postposition structure, and broader
questions about head-dependent order.

No equally clean Gospel possession row was found for here under current
careful evidence control. The possession subsection therefore remains
OT-led even though the section as a whole includes Gospel-balanced
NP-order examples.

\subsubsection{Deferred and boundary
material}\label{deferred-and-boundary-material}

Several important possession and NP topics remain explicitly deferred.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  A full possession paradigm is still deferred: the present section does
  not normalize every possessive prefix or every nominal host.
\item
  Kinship possession remains under-supported beyond a few controlled
  anchors such as \tdim{ka pa}.
\item
  Pronominal possession and agreement overlap remains shared with the
  pronoun and prefix/agreement material rather than settled here.
\item
  Apostrophe-marked relations such as \tdim{Topa' inn} remain promising,
  but their exact relation to genitive marking and title-like noun
  combinations still needs fuller normalization.
\item
  \tdim{Topa' tungah} remains a relator or case-boundary row rather than
  a clean possession example.
\item
  \tdim{ka suahna leitang} remains a nominalization boundary row rather
  than a core possession anchor.
\item
  Demonstrative, numeral, and quantifier rows are used here for NP-order
  support only; their full systems remain in the demonstratives,
  numerals, and quantifiers sections.
\item
  Analyzer-noisy or report-only material, including isolated prefix
  surfaces and broad recursive-possession claims, is not promoted into
  the present discussion here.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Summary}\label{summary}

The NP structure / possession section can now support a modest
description of noun phrase order: the cleanest current evidence points
to demonstrative-before-noun structure alongside noun-plus-postnominal
numeral and quantifier patterns. Possession is now visible enough to
include with formal examples, but it remains a cautious subsection whose
broader paradigm, marking analysis, and interface with pronouns,
genitive structure, and relators are still explicitly deferred.

\subsection{Noun domain}\label{noun-domain}

The noun-domain evidence is strongest for simple stems such as
\tdim{gam} \glossquote{land / country} and \tdim{aksi}
\glossquote{star}, for plural \tdim{-te}, and for nouns that remain
visible as heads inside larger phrases.

\subsubsection{Overview of the noun
domain}\label{overview-of-the-noun-domain}

The clearest current evidence supports a modest but coherent noun-domain
description. Simple lexical nouns such as \tdim{gam}
\glossquote{land / country} and \tdim{aksi} \glossquote{star} are safely
attested, and plural marking with \tdim{-te} is printable for controlled
anchors such as \tdim{aksi-te} \glossquote{stars} and \tdim{mite}
\glossquote{people}. Human noun \tdim{mi} \glossquote{person} is also a
stable lexical head in larger noun phrases, as seen in rows such as
\tdim{mi khat} \glossquote{one person / a person}, \tdim{mi khempeuh}
\glossquote{all people}, \tdim{mi pawlkhat} \glossquote{some people},
and \tdim{mi tampi} \glossquote{many people}, while nonhuman nouns such
as \tdim{ni} \glossquote{day} and \tdim{kum} \glossquote{year} behave
similarly in counted phrases.

This is enough to describe a small noun inventory, to note a cautious
\tdim{-te} plural pattern, and to show that lexical nouns remain visible
as heads inside demonstrative, numeral, and quantifier phrases. It is
not yet enough for a full noun-domain chapter, because compounds, proper
names, classifier-like nouns, and nominalized nouns still require more
boundary control.

Gospel searches produced usable noun-domain evidence for \tdim{aksi}
\glossquote{star}, \tdim{mi khempeuh} \glossquote{all people},
\tdim{ni li} \glossquote{four days}, and proper-name material such as
\tdim{Abraham' suan David} \glossquote{David, descendant of Abraham},
which helps keep the section from becoming Genesis-only. The cleanest
\tdim{gam} \glossquote{land / country} and \tdim{-te} plural anchors,
however, remain OT-led in the current stage, while compound material
such as \tdim{minam} \glossquote{nation / people-group} still needs
explicit caveats.

\subsubsection{Current noun-domain
inventory}\label{current-noun-domain-inventory}

\subsubsection{Simple noun stems}\label{simple-noun-stems}

The safest simple-stem anchors remain \tdim{gam} and \tdim{aksi}.
\tdim{gam} is a robust nonhuman common noun in the noun reports, while
\tdim{aksi} is visible both in older OT material and in a clean Gospel
phrase that keeps the stem separate from plural marking. These rows are
enough to show that the noun section is not limited to derived forms or
larger noun phrases: basic lexical stems are directly attested.

At the same time, the current section keeps the claim narrow. It does
not attempt to classify all noun semantics or all stem alternations; it
only records that ordinary lexical nouns can be cited safely before the
analysis moves on to plural marking and larger NP environments.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:noun-gam}
\gll \tdimword{gàm} \tdimword{sùng} \\
     land inside \\
\glt \glossquote{in the land} (Genesis 2:5)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:noun-aksi}
\gll \tdimword{àmà} \tdimword{aksi} \\
     \textsc{3sg}.\textsc{poss} star \\
\glt \glossquote{his star} (Matthew 2:2)
\end{exe}

\subsubsection{Plural marking with -te}\label{plural-marking-with--te}

Plural marking with \tdim{-te} is now safe to discuss in a limited way.
The clearest noun-domain anchors are nonhuman \tdim{aksi-te} and human
\tdim{mi-te} in the fused form \tdim{mite}. Taken together, they support
a modest claim that \tdim{-te} marks plurality on nouns in examples.

The present section does not claim that every plural or collective
pattern has been normalized. Broader questions about distributive
readings, plurality outside simple noun stems, and the interaction of
plural marking with quantification are still deferred.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:noun-aksi-te}
\reviewwarninginline{[REVIEW PREVIEW WARNING: ex:noun-aksi-te: Bible reference mapped, but slice example is not a direct verse span; analyzed slice wording]}
\gll \tdimword{aksi-tè} \\
     star-\textsc{pl} \\
\glt \glossquote{stars} (Genesis 1:16)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:noun-hih-mite}
\gll \tdimword{híh} \tdimword{mì-tè} \\
     \textsc{prox} person-\textsc{pl} \\
\glt \glossquote{these people} (Exodus 5:5)
\end{exe}

No equally clean Gospel plural example is currently used for this
construction, so the section keeps the compact Old Testament anchors.

\subsubsection{Human nouns and common
nouns}\label{human-nouns-and-common-nouns}

Human noun \tdim{mi} is now one of the clearest lexical noun anchors in
the nominal domain. It appears as a simple noun stem and as the head of
several already-normalized noun phrases, including \tdim{mi khat},
\tdim{mi khempeuh}, \tdim{mi pawlkhat}, and \tdim{mi tampi}. Its plural
form \tdim{mite} is equally secure in the current evidence.

This matters for the noun domain because it shows that the normalized
NP, numeral, and quantifier sections are reusing genuine noun heads
rather than unanalyzed filler forms. The NP structure section remains
the right place for broader ordering claims, but the noun domain can now
safely say that \tdim{mi} and \tdim{mite} are stable human common nouns.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:noun-mi-khat}
\gll \tdimword{mì} \tdimword{khàt} \\
     person one \\
\glt \glossquote{a man / one person} (Genesis 32:24)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:noun-mi-khempeuh}
\gll \tdimword{mì} \tdimword{khèmpeuh} \\
     person all \\
\glt \glossquote{all people} (Luke 2:1)
\end{exe}

\subsubsection{Nouns in larger phrases}\label{nouns-in-larger-phrases}

The normalized numerals, quantifiers, and NP structure / possession
sections already show that nouns remain visible as heads in larger
phrases. In the current noun-domain pass, that point can be stated
modestly: noun heads such as \tdim{mi}, \tdim{ni}, and \tdim{kum} stay
lexically identifiable inside counted and quantified expressions rather
than disappearing into unanalyzed templates.

\tdim{Ni li} \glossquote{four days} and \tdim{kum sawm le nih}
\glossquote{twelve years} are the clearest counted anchors for this
purpose. Together they show that lexical noun heads remain visible
inside larger numeral phrases. The noun-domain claim is therefore
limited to lexical headedness; it does not reopen the fuller NP-order or
numeral/quantifier analyses.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:noun-ni-li}
\gll \tdimword{nì} \tdimword{li} \\
     day four \\
\glt \glossquote{four days} (John 11:39)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:noun-kum-sawm-le-nih}
\gll \tdimword{kúm} \tdimword{sàwm} \tdimword{lè} \tdimword{nìh} \\
     year ten and two \\
\glt \glossquote{twelve years} (Matthew 9:20)
\end{exe}

No equally clean Old Testament example is currently used for this
construction, so the section keeps the compact Gospel anchors.

\subsubsection{Compounds and proper
nouns}\label{compounds-and-proper-nouns}

Compounds and proper nouns are now visible enough for a cautious note,
but they remain less normalized than simple stems and plural-marked
nouns. The clearest compound-like anchor is \tdim{minam}, which is still
transparent enough to support a controlled print comment about noun-noun
composition. Proper names such as \tdim{Abraham} are also clearly
nominal, but the current evidence is not yet strong enough to generalize
over Bible names, place names, or analyzer-noisy multiword name strings.

The current section therefore treats compounds and proper nouns as
boundary material with one safe compound example and one cautious
proper-name example. \tdim{Abraham' suan David} is a useful comparandum,
but it does not yet justify a full proper-name subsection or a complete
account of name-internal structure.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:noun-minam-khat}
\gll \tdimword{mì-nam} \tdimword{khàt} \\
     person-kind one \\
\glt \glossquote{one nation} (Genesis 11:6)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:noun-abraham-suan-david}
\gll \tdimword{Abraham'} \tdimword{suan} \tdimword{David} \\
     \textsc{abraham}.\textsc{poss} plant \textsc{david} \\
\glt \glossquote{David, descendant of Abraham} (Matthew 1:1)
\end{exe}

\subsubsection{Nominalization boundary}\label{nominalization-boundary}

Derived nouns and nominalized forms remain shared with the
nominalization section. Items such as \tdim{kholhna} and broader
\tdim{-na} material are important for the nominal domain, but the
present section does not absorb them into the lexical noun inventory. It
only notes that lexical nouns, plural-marked nouns, and larger noun
phrases have now been normalized far enough that nominalized nouns can
be kept as an explicit boundary rather than being mixed into the basic
noun list.

\subsubsection{Deferred and boundary
material}\label{deferred-and-boundary-material-1}

Several noun-domain topics remain explicitly deferred.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Transparent compounds remain only partially normalized: \tdim{minam}
  is useful, but broader compound classes still need more evidence.
\item
  Lexicalized compounds remain deferred because current report evidence
  does not cleanly separate synchronically analyzable forms from frozen
  lexemes.
\item
  Proper nouns remain boundary-heavy: \tdim{Abraham} is usable as a
  controlled anchor, but Bible person and place names are not yet
  normalized as a full subsystem.
\item
  Classifier-like nouns and measure-like nominal heads remain shared
  with numeral and quantifier work rather than settled here.
\item
  Kinship nouns remain partly shared with possession, especially where
  forms such as \tdim{pa}, \tdim{inn}, and \tdim{min} enter possessor
  constructions.
\item
  Nominalized nouns in \tdim{-na} remain with the nominalization section
  rather than being promoted as basic noun stems here.
\item
  Plural and number patterns beyond straightforward \tdim{-te} marking
  remain deferred.
\item
  Analyzer-noisy noun labels and multiword proper-name strings are not
  promoted into the present discussion.
\item
  Raw report-only noun lists are not treated as grammar facts without
  checked careful evidence control.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Summary}\label{summary-1}

The noun domain can now support a fuller section than the earlier narrow
section allowed. Current evidence safely identifies simple noun stems
such as \tdim{gam} and \tdim{aksi}, a cautious \tdim{-te} plural pattern
visible in \tdim{aksi-te} and \tdim{mite}, stable human noun heads such
as \tdim{mi}, and lexical nouns that remain visible inside counted and
quantified noun phrases. Compounds, proper nouns, and nominalized nouns
are now better framed as explicit boundary material rather than being
mixed into an under-controlled noun list
\citep{henderson1965, zamngaihcing2017}.

\subsection{Case marking}\label{case-marking}

The current case evidence centers on \tdim{-ah}
\glossquote{locative / goal-like} and \tdim{-in}
\glossquote{ergative / agentive}, while \tdim{-pan}
\glossquote{source / ablative}, \tdim{-panin}
\glossquote{source / departure}, and \tdim{-tawh} \glossquote{with}
remain more cautious oblique extensions.

\subsubsection{Overview of case-like
marking}\label{overview-of-case-like-marking}

The current evidence supports a modest but coherent picture of
postnominal case-like marking in Tedim. The clearest claims concern
locative or goal-like \tdim{-ah} \glossquote{locative / goal-like} and
clause-level agentive or ergative \tdim{-in}
\glossquote{ergative / agentive}. The same section also supports more
cautious discussion of source marking with \tdim{-pan}
\glossquote{source / ablative} and \tdim{-panin}
\glossquote{source / departure}, plus accompaniment or
material-extension uses of \tdim{-tawh} \glossquote{with}, but those
markers remain more boundary-heavy because they overlap with relator
nouns, postpositions, or broader semantic extension.

The safest current prose therefore treats case marking as an NP-final
domain rather than as a fully settled paradigm of discrete suffixes.
Plain noun-plus-case rows such as \tdim{khua-ah}
\glossquote{in the town} and clause-level agent rows such as
\tdim{Kain in} \glossquote{Cain as agent} are secure enough to print.
Relator-hosted forms such as \tdim{lakpan} \glossquote{from among} and
\tdim{David khuapi sungah} \glossquote{in the city of David} are also
real, but they should not be collapsed into a bare suffix inventory.
Apostrophe-marked possession can host the same NP-final marking, yet the
apostrophe itself remains a boundary issue rather than a settled
genitive case marker in this section.

The Gospel search was productive enough to keep the section from
becoming OT-only. The current manually reviewed supplement adds usable
Gospel support for \tdim{Herod in} \glossquote{Herod as agent},
\tdim{keima inn-ah} \glossquote{into my house},
\tdim{David khuapi sungah} \glossquote{in the city of David}, and
\tdim{lakpan} \glossquote{from among}. The possessive-boundary example
remains OT-led, because no equally compact Gospel row was as clean under
the current control standard.

\subsubsection{Current case-marking
inventory}\label{current-case-marking-inventory}

\tdim{-ah} & locative or goal-like NP-final marking & \tdim{khua-ah},

\tdim{-in} & clause-level ergative or agentive marking & \tdim{Kain in},

marking & \tdim{na pa' inn-ah} & supported with caveat & does not settle
the apostrophe or genitive analysis \\
\tdim{-pan} & source marking, often on a relator host & \tdim{lakpan} &
supported with caveat & current clean evidence is relator-hosted rather
than a broad bare-suffix paradigm \\
\tdim{-panin} & source or departure marking & \tdim{inn panin} &
supported with caveat & internal structure remains under review \\
\tdim{-tawh} & accompaniment, with material or instrumental extension &
\tdim{kei tawh}, \tdim{leivui tawh} & supported with caveat &
accompaniment should not be flattened together with material-extension
use \\
relator noun plus case & spatial relational phrase closed by case-like
marking & \tdim{David khuapi sungah}, \tdim{tungah}, \tdim{kiangah} &
supported with caveat & belongs jointly with the relators /
postpositions section rather than a suffix-only list \\
\end{longtable}
}

\subsubsection{Locative and goal marking with
-ah}\label{locative-and-goal-marking-with--ah}

The clearest present claim is that \tdim{-ah} is a locative or goal-like
case marker at the right edge of the noun phrase. The primary checked
control is plain noun-plus-locative \tdim{khua-ah}, while the new
source-balance supplement adds a compact Gospel goal-like example with
\tdim{keima inn-ah}. This is enough to describe \tdim{-ah} as marking
location and destination-like relations without forcing a sharper split
among locative, allative, and general oblique labels than the current
evidence can support.

The section also keeps one important negative control explicit. The
current evidence does \textbf{not} justify collapsing deferred \tdim{-a}
material into \tdim{-ah}. The source inventory and case background notes
both keep tone-sensitive or otherwise ambiguous \tdim{-a} questions
unresolved, so the description remains narrower than a full locative or
allative chapter.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:case-ah-khua}
\gll \tdimword{khùa-àh} \\
     town-\textsc{loc} \\
\glt \glossquote{in the town} (Genesis 11:28)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:case-ah-inn}
\gll \tdimword{kèima} \tdimword{ínn-àh} \\
     \textsc{1sg}.self house-\textsc{loc} \\
\glt \glossquote{into my house} (Matthew 8:8)
\end{exe}

These two rows together support the safest present wording.
\tdim{khua-ah} keeps the simple noun-plus-case pattern visible, while
\tdim{keima inn-ah} shows that the same marker naturally appears in a
Gospel clause with motion toward a location. That is enough for cautious
the present discussion, but not yet enough to decide whether every
\tdim{-ah} token should be described as specifically locative, allative,
or more generally oblique.

\subsubsection{Agentive, ergative, or instrumental marking with
-in}\label{agentive-ergative-or-instrumental-marking-with--in}

The present section supports an agentive or ergative analysis of
\tdim{-in} more clearly than it supports any broader instrumental
analysis. Genesis 4:3 remains the primary checked anchor because
\tdim{Kain in} is the cleanest controlled row. The new supplement adds
Matthew 2:4 \tdim{Herod in} as a manually reviewed Gospel support
example, which helps show that the pattern is not confined to one OT
narrative window.

At the same time, this section does not claim a complete ergative
system. It also does not promote a general instrumental \tdim{-in}
category. The most important control here is still the homograph
problem: raw searches for \tdim{-in} overgenerate forms such as
\tdim{ciangin} \glossquote{when}, so only checked clause-level noun
phrase rows are promoted into the grammar prose.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:case-in-kain}
\gll \tdimword{Kain} \tdimword{ìn} \\
     \textsc{kain} \textsc{erg} \\
\glt \glossquote{Cain as transitive subject} (Genesis 4:3)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:case-in-herod}
\gll \tdimword{Herod} \tdimword{ìn} \\
     \textsc{herod} \textsc{erg} \\
\glt \glossquote{Herod as transitive subject} (Matthew 2:4)
\end{exe}

These examples are intentionally compact. They are not meant to reopen
the full alignment chapter. They only show that a noun phrase can bear
checked \tdim{-in} marking in a clause with a transitive predicate, and
that this is stronger evidence than any uncontrolled \tdim{-in} string
would be.

\subsubsection{Other controlled oblique
markers}\label{other-controlled-oblique-markers}

The section still retains a small set of other controlled case-like
markers, but they are best treated more cautiously than \tdim{-ah} and
\tdim{-in}. Source marking with \tdim{-pan} and \tdim{-panin} is real
enough to describe, especially in rows such as \tdim{lakpan}
\glossquote{from among} and \tdim{inn panin}
\glossquote{from the house}, yet the cleanest \tdim{-pan} example is
relator-hosted and the internal structure of \tdim{-panin} remains under
review. Likewise, \tdim{-tawh} remains part of the section because
\tdim{kei tawh} \glossquote{with me} is a clean accompaniment row and
\tdim{leivui tawh} \glossquote{with dust} shows a material or
instrument-like extension, but that semantic split needs to stay
explicit.

This means the section can already mention \tdim{-pan}, \tdim{-panin},
and \tdim{-tawh} in the current inventory, but it should not yet present
them as a fully normalized oblique subsystem. For the present pass, they
remain controlled supporting material rather than the center of the
case-marking chapter.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:case-panin-inn}
\gll \tdimword{ínn} \tdimword{pàn-ìn} \\
     house \textsc{abl}-\textsc{erg} \\
\glt \glossquote{from the house} (Genesis 12:1)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:case-tawh-kei}
\gll \tdimword{kèi} \tdimword{tàwh} \\
     \textsc{1sg}.\textsc{pro} \textsc{com} \\
\glt \glossquote{with me} (Genesis 14:24)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:case-tawh-leivui}
\gll \tdimword{leivui} \tdimword{tàwh} \\
     dust \textsc{com} \\
\glt \glossquote{with dust} (Genesis 2:7)
\end{exe}

These rows keep the oblique material concrete. \tdim{Inn panin}
\glossquote{from the house} shows source marking without a relator host,
while \tdim{kei tawh} \glossquote{with me} and \tdim{leivui tawh}
\glossquote{with dust} show that \tdim{-tawh} covers both accompaniment
and material-extension uses.

No equally clean Gospel source or accompaniment row is currently used
here, so the subsection stays OT-led while the broader oblique inventory
remains cautiously delimited.

\subsubsection{Genitive / possessive
boundary}\label{genitive-possessive-boundary}

The clearest way to discuss genitive or possessive boundary material at
present is to show that an already possessed noun phrase can host
case-like marking at its right edge. Genesis 24:23 gives the compact row
\tdim{na pa' inn-ah} \glossquote{in your father's house}, which is
already reused in the normalized NP structure / possession section. Here
it serves a narrower purpose: it shows that case-like marking closes the
larger noun phrase rather than attaching only to a simple underived noun
stem.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:case-poss-na-pa-inn}
\gll \tdimword{nà} \tdimword{pa'} \tdimword{ínn-àh} \\
     \textsc{2sg} male.\textsc{poss} house-\textsc{loc} \\
\glt \glossquote{in thy father's house} (Genesis 24:23)
\end{exe}

This is enough to justify a boundary note, but not enough to settle the
apostrophe analysis. The present section therefore does not settle
whether the apostrophe material should be treated as a genitive suffix,
a possessive linker, or an orthographic boundary convention. It only
records that possessed noun phrases can participate in the same
case-closing pattern that appears elsewhere in the nominal domain. The
fuller discussion of possessive structure remains with the normalized NP
structure / possession section.

No equally good Gospel example of a possessed noun phrase with case-like
closure is currently used here, so the subsection stays with the compact
OT example while the wider possession discussion remains elsewhere.

\subsubsection{Case marking and
relators/postpositions}\label{case-marking-and-relatorspostpositions}

The boundary with relators and postpositions is now one of the main
editorial points of the section. Forms such as \tdim{sungah}
\glossquote{inside / in}, \tdim{tungah} \glossquote{on / upon},
\tdim{kiangah} \glossquote{beside / near}, and \tdim{lakpan}
\glossquote{from among} are not noise, but neither are they simple proof
that Tedim can be described by a suffix-only case list. The relational
host matters: \tdim{sung} \glossquote{inside}, \tdim{tung}
\glossquote{on top}, \tdim{kiang} \glossquote{side / vicinity}, and
\tdim{lak} \glossquote{among} contribute spatial content before locative
or source marking is added.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:case-relator-david-khuapi-sungah}
\gll \tdimword{David} \tdimword{khùa-pì} \tdimword{sùng-àh} \\
     \textsc{david} village-big inside-\textsc{loc} \\
\glt \glossquote{in the city of David} (Luke 2:11)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:case-relator-lakpan}
\gll \tdimword{lak-pàn} \\
     midst-\textsc{abl} \\
\glt \glossquote{from among} (Matthew 5:19)
\end{exe}

These rows belong here because they are case-like and NP-final. They
also belong with the relators / postpositions section because the
relational noun is part of the grammar, not just a carrier for a suffix.
That is why forms such as \tdim{tungah} or \tdim{lakpan} should not be
collapsed into case marking without careful evidence control. The best
current solution is to let the two sections meet at the boundary rather
than pretending the boundary does not exist.

No equally clean Old Testament example is currently used for this
construction, so the section keeps the compact Gospel anchors.

\subsubsection{Case marking and argument
structure}\label{case-marking-and-argument-structure}

The normalized case section can now say one modest thing about argument
structure: checked \tdim{-in} rows such as \tdim{Kain in}
\glossquote{Cain as agent} and \tdim{Herod in}
\glossquote{Herod as agent} show case-marked noun phrases functioning as
clause-level agents, while locative or goal-like rows such as
\tdim{inn-ah} \glossquote{in / into the house} show noun phrases
entering the clause as spatial arguments or adjuncts. That is enough to
connect the nominal domain to clause structure without reopening the
full alignment or valency chapter.

The present section therefore cross-references the transitivity section
rather than replacing it. The transitivity section remains the place for
fuller discussion of argument frames, lexical valency, and broader
alignment questions. Case marking only contributes the controlled
NP-final marking side of that picture here.

\subsubsection{Deferred and boundary
material}\label{deferred-and-boundary-material-2}

Several important topics remain explicitly deferred.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  A full case paradigm is still deferred; the present evidence is not
  yet enough for a full case paradigm.
\item
  The ergative versus instrumental distinction for \tdim{-in} is not
  settled here.
\item
  The finer split among locative, dative, allative, and general oblique
  functions is still under review.
\item
  Tone-sensitive \tdim{-a} material remains blocked rather than being
  collapsed into \tdim{-ah}.
\item
  Apostrophe or genitive analysis remains unresolved and stays shared
  with the NP structure / possession section.
\item
  Possessive noun phrase overlap remains explicit, especially where
  possessed NPs then take \tdim{-ah}.
\item
  Relator-noun and postposition structure remains shared with the
  relators / postpositions section.
\item
  Full alignment and argument-structure claims remain shared with the
  transitivity section.
\item
  \tdim{-panin} remains a controlled source-marking row without a fully
  settled internal analysis.
\item
  \tdim{-tawh} remains split between accompaniment and material or
  instrumental extension.
\item
  Analyzer-noisy or raw report-only marker rows are not promoted into
  the present discussion.
\item
  Raw generated-report counts and broad analyzer output are not treated
  as grammar facts in this section.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Summary}\label{summary-2}

The case-marking section can now support a fuller description than the
earlier narrow section allowed. Current evidence safely supports
clause-level \tdim{-in}, locative or goal-like \tdim{-ah}, a boundary
note on possessed noun phrases such as \tdim{na pa' inn-ah}, and a
controlled interface with relator-hosted forms such as
\tdim{David khuapi sungah} and \tdim{lakpan}. Source marking with
\tdim{-pan} and \tdim{-panin}, along with \tdim{-tawh}, remains usable
but secondary and caveated. The result is a real grammar section with
explicit limits, not a claim that the full Tedim case system has already
been finished \citep{henderson1965, zamngaihcing2017}.

\subsection{Relators / postpositions}\label{relators-postpositions}

The current evidence supports relational nouns such as \tdim{sung}
\glossquote{inside}, \tdim{tung} \glossquote{on / upon}, \tdim{kiang}
\glossquote{beside / near}, and \tdim{lak} \glossquote{among / midst},
together with postpositional source patterns that close the larger
phrase.

\subsubsection{Overview of relators and
postpositions}\label{overview-of-relators-and-postpositions}

Tedim uses relational nouns and postpositional expressions to encode
spatial and other relations, and case-like marking often appears at the
right edge of the resulting phrase. The clearest current relator stems
are \tdim{sung} \glossquote{inside}, \tdim{tung} \glossquote{on / upon},
\tdim{kiang} \glossquote{beside / near}, \tdim{lak}
\glossquote{among / midst}, and the more cautious \tdim{pualam}
\glossquote{outside}. Related forms such as \tdim{laizang}
\glossquote{middle / midst} point to the same semantic field, but the
present section keeps its main discussion on the better-controlled
relator stems \citep{henderson1965, zamngaihcing2017}.

The main descriptive point is therefore structural rather than
terminological. A form such as \tdim{sung} \glossquote{inside} or
\tdim{kiang} \glossquote{beside / near} contributes spatial content of
its own, while the phrase may still close with case-like material such
as \tdim{-ah} \glossquote{locative / goal-like} or with source-marking
\tdim{pan} \glossquote{from} and \tdim{panin} \glossquote{from}. This is
why the relator section and the case-marking section overlap without
collapsing into each other.

\subsubsection{Current relator / postposition
inventory}\label{current-relator-postposition-inventory}

  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}

\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright

\endlastfoot
\tdim{sung} & interior relation & \tdim{David khuapi sungah} & well

\tdim{tung} & surface or superposed relation & \tdim{tua tui tungah} &

\tdim{kiang} & side, vicinity, or adjacency & \tdim{mipa kiangah} &

\tdim{tungah} & surface relation closed by \tdim{-ah} &
\tdim{tua tui tungah} & well supported & the relator stem and the edge
marker both contribute meaning \\
\tdim{kiangah} & adjacency relation closed by \tdim{-ah} &
\tdim{mipa kiangah} & supported with caveat & also touches
recipient-like use in larger clause structure \\
\tdim{lakpan} / \tdim{lak panin} & source from an interior set &
\tdim{lakpan}, \tdim{numeite lak panin} & well supported & fused and
separated spellings both belong at the relator or case boundary \\
\tdim{NP + tawh} & accompaniment or associative postposition &
\tdim{kei tawh} & supporting boundary pattern & shared with
accompaniment and material-extension uses discussed under case
marking \\
\end{longtable}
}

\subsubsection{Spatial relator nouns}\label{spatial-relator-nouns}

The clearest first point is that Tedim has spatial relator nouns, not
only bare suffixes. In the current evidence, bare spatial use is
especially easy to see with \tdim{pualam} \glossquote{outside}, while
the other stems frequently occur with additional edge marking. That
asymmetry is itself informative: the relational noun remains visible
even when another marker closes the phrase.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:rel-pualam-kongkhak}
\gll \tdimword{kòng-khàk} \tdimword{pualam} \\
     \textsc{1sg→3}-limit outside \\
\glt \glossquote{outside the door} (Mark 2:2)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:rel-pualam-khuapi}
\gll \tdimword{khùa-pì} \tdimword{pualam} \\
     village-big outside \\
\glt \glossquote{outside the city} (Genesis 24:11)
\end{exe}

These examples keep the relator stem visible without forcing a larger
claim about every spatial noun in the language. The same evidence domain
also supports discussion of \tdim{sung} \glossquote{inside}, \tdim{tung}
\glossquote{on / upon}, \tdim{kiang} \glossquote{beside / near}, and
\tdim{lak} \glossquote{among / midst}, but those stems are most
naturally illustrated below in phrases where a case-like or
source-marking element closes the larger expression.

\subsubsection{Relator plus case-like
marking}\label{relator-plus-case-like-marking}

When case-like marking is added, forms such as \tdim{sungah}
\glossquote{inside / in}, \tdim{tungah} \glossquote{on / upon},
\tdim{kiangah} \glossquote{beside / near}, and \tdim{lakpan}
\glossquote{from among} show that the relational noun and the final
marker both contribute to the construction. The resulting forms are real
grammatical units, but the present evidence does not support treating
them as if the relator had disappeared and only a suffix remained.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:rel-sungah-david-khuapi}
\gll \tdimword{David} \tdimword{khùa-pì} \tdimword{sùng-àh} \\
     \textsc{david} village-big inside-\textsc{loc} \\
\glt \glossquote{in the city of David} (Luke 2:11)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:rel-tungah-tua-tui}
\gll \tdimword{túa} \tdimword{tùi} \tdimword{tùngàh} \\
     \textsc{dist} water on-\textsc{loc} \\
\glt \glossquote{upon the water} (Genesis 1:2)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:rel-kiangah-mipa}
\gll \tdimword{mipa} \tdimword{kìang-àh} \\
     man beside-\textsc{loc} \\
\glt \glossquote{to the man / beside the man} (Genesis 2:19)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:rel-lakpan}
\gll \tdimword{lak-pàn} \\
     midst-\textsc{abl} \\
\glt \glossquote{from among} (Matthew 5:19)
\end{exe}

The examples are deliberately compact, because the descriptive issue is
compact as well. \tdim{David khuapi sungah} and \tdim{tua tui tungah}
show interior and surface relations closed by \tdim{-ah}, while
\tdim{mipa kiangah} shows that adjacency expressions behave in the same
way. \tdim{Lakpan} then shows that source marking can be hosted by the
relator itself rather than by a simple noun stem.

\subsubsection{Postpositional phrase
structure}\label{postpositional-phrase-structure}

The current evidence also supports a modest structural claim about
postpositional phrases. A relator or postposition follows the noun
phrase that supplies its semantic anchor, and the whole sequence can
then carry the source-like meaning of \tdim{pan} \glossquote{from} or
\tdim{panin} \glossquote{from}. This is clearest when the noun phrase
before the relator is larger than a single bare noun.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:rel-kiang-panin-pa}
\gll \tdimword{Pà} \tdimword{kìang} \tdimword{pàn-ìn} \\
     father beside \textsc{abl}-\textsc{erg} \\
\glt \glossquote{from the Father} (John 6:45)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:rel-lak-panin-numeite}
\gll \tdimword{numei-tè} \tdimword{lāk} \tdimword{pàn-ìn} \\
     woman-\textsc{pl} among \textsc{abl}-\textsc{erg} \\
\glt \glossquote{from among the women} (Exodus 2:7)
\end{exe}

These examples keep the structural claim modest. The section does not
claim a single uniform analysis for every spelling alternation or every
source-marking form. It only shows that the relational element is
phrase-medial rather than invisible, because the noun phrase comes first
and the postpositional or case-like material closes the expression
afterward.

\subsubsection{Case-marking boundary}\label{case-marking-boundary}

The boundary with case marking remains explicit. Forms such as
\tdim{sungah}, \tdim{tungah}, \tdim{kiangah}, and \tdim{lakpan} are
shared with the case-marking section because both pieces of the
construction matter: the relator contributes the spatial relation, and
the final marker contributes locative or source-like closure. The
case-marking section therefore remains responsible for the edge-marking
side of the analysis, while the present section explains why the
relational host is still grammatically visible.

This division of labor matters especially for \tdim{-ah}, \tdim{pan},
and \tdim{panin}. A phrase like \tdim{David khuapi sungah} is not just a
bare locative suffix attached to \tdim{khuapi}, and \tdim{lakpan} is not
just a bare ablative suffix detached from \tdim{lak}. The relator and
the phrase-edge marker work together.

\subsubsection{Possession and NP-structure
boundary}\label{possession-and-np-structure-boundary}

The relator or postposition can follow a larger noun phrase rather than
a simple stem. This is already visible in \tdim{David khuapi sungah}
\glossquote{in the city of David}, in kin-based phrases such as
\tdim{Pa kiang panin} \glossquote{from the Father}, and in possessed
noun phrases such as \tdim{Faro' inn sungah}
\glossquote{in Pharaoh's house}. The structure is therefore
phrase-closing rather than stem-bound.

The present section stops there. Fuller discussion of possessive marking
and noun-phrase architecture remains with the NP structure / possession
section, but that section and the present one now meet at a clear
grammatical boundary.

\subsubsection{Deferred and boundary
material}\label{deferred-and-boundary-material-3}

Several issues remain outside the present account.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \tdim{nuai} and \tdim{mai} remain possible spatial nouns, but the
  current section does not promote them as core relator anchors.
\item
  \tdim{tawhin} is still too sparse and too instrument-like to stand
  beside \tdim{pan}, \tdim{panin}, or \tdim{tawh} in the present
  account.
\item
  The exact relation between tighter spellings such as \tdim{lakpan} and
  separated spellings such as \tdim{lak panin} remains open.
\item
  Wider recipient-like uses around \tdim{kiang} still belong partly with
  clause structure and partly with case marking.
\item
  Compound spatial nouns such as \tdim{laizang} remain relevant
  background, but they are not the center of the present section.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Summary}\label{summary-3}

The current evidence supports a grammar-facing account of Tedim relators
and postpositions. Spatial relations are carried by visible relational
nouns such as \tdim{sung} \glossquote{inside}, \tdim{tung}
\glossquote{on / upon}, \tdim{kiang} \glossquote{beside / near},
\tdim{lak} \glossquote{among / midst}, and cautiously \tdim{pualam}
\glossquote{outside}, while postpositional and case-like material closes
the larger phrase. The result is a relational system whose structure is
clearer once the relator and the phrase-edge marker are described
together rather than forced into a suffix-only inventory
\citep{henderson1965, zamngaihcing2017}.

\subsection{Numerals}\label{numerals}

The current numeral evidence supports a decimal system with basic
cardinals, counted noun phrases, and \tdim{-na} ordinals, while
larger-number and classifier-like material remain explicit boundary
notes.

\subsubsection{Overview of the numeral
system}\label{overview-of-the-numeral-system}

Tedim numerals are organized around a decimal structure in which
\tdim{khat} \glossquote{one}, \tdim{nih} \glossquote{two}, and
\tdim{kua} \glossquote{nine} combine with base terms such as \tdim{sawm}
\glossquote{ten} and \tdim{za} \glossquote{hundred}
\citep{zamngaihcing2017, henderson1965}. Larger units such as
\tdim{sing} \glossquote{thousand} and \tdim{tul}
\glossquote{ten thousand} are also recognized in the system, although
the clearest examples in this section remain in the lower ranges plus
selected large-number biblical expressions.

The evidence in hand supports a cautious but substantive description:
basic cardinals, compound numerals, noun-plus-numeral order, \tdim{-na}
ordinals, and at least one occurrence-counting expression are all
securely attested in formal examples.

\subsubsection{Cardinal inventory}\label{cardinal-inventory}
