The table summarizes the core inventory without forcing a full paradigm
of every possible constructional variant.

\subsubsection{Compound numerals}\label{compound-numerals}

Compound numerals are straightforwardly visible in both Old Testament
and Gospel examples.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:num-sawmkua}
\gll \tdimword{kúm} \tdimword{sàwm-kua} \\
     year ten-nine \\
\glt \glossquote{ninety years} (Genesis 5:9)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:num-kum-sawm-le-nih}
\gll \tdimword{kúm} \tdimword{sàwm} \tdimword{lè} \tdimword{nìh} \\
     year ten and two \\
\glt \glossquote{twelve years} (Matthew 9:20)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:num-large}
\gll \tdimword{kúm} \tdimword{zā-kua} \tdimword{lè} \tdimword{kúm} \tdimword{sàwm-gùk} \tdimword{lè} \tdimword{kua} \\
     year hundred-nine and year ten-six and who \\
\glt \glossquote{nine hundred sixty and nine years} (Genesis 5:27)
\end{exe}

These examples support a decimal-compositional analysis for tens and
larger expressions. The Genesis 5:27 line is structurally numeral
despite analyzer irregularities in the final token gloss.

\subsubsection{Noun-plus-numeral word
order}\label{noun-plus-numeral-word-order}

The strongest current examples show counted nouns preceding numerals.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:num-kum-nih}
\gll \tdimword{kúm} \tdimword{nìh} \\
     year two \\
\glt \glossquote{two years} (Genesis 11:10)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:num-ni-sagih}
\gll \tdimword{nì} \tdimword{sagih} \\
     day seven \\
\glt \glossquote{seven days} (Genesis 7:10)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:num-ni-li}
\gll \tdimword{nì} \tdimword{li} \\
     day four \\
\glt \glossquote{four days} (John 11:39)
\end{exe}

These data justify a conservative claim that noun-plus-numeral order is
well supported in the best current examples.

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{Ordinals and the \tdim{-na}
boundary}{Ordinals and the  boundary}}\label{ordinals-and-the-boundary}

Ordinal formation with \tdim{-na} is securely attested.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:num-nihna}
\gll \tdimword{nìh-ná} \\
     two-\textsc{nmlz} \\
\glt \glossquote{second} (Genesis 7:11)
\end{exe}

Here \tdim{-na} marks ordinal derivation; this use should not be
collapsed with the broader nominalizing functions of \tdim{-na}
elsewhere in the grammar.

No equally clean Gospel example is currently used for this construction.

\subsubsection{Multiplicative and counting
expressions}\label{multiplicative-and-counting-expressions}

Occurrence counting is represented by \tdim{sawmvei}
\glossquote{ten times}.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:num-sawmvei}
\gll \tdimword{sàwm-véi} \\
     ten-times \\
\glt \glossquote{ten times} (Genesis 31:7)
\end{exe}

This expression is retained in the fused form \tdim{sawmvei}; it is not
rewritten here as a different segmentation.

No equally clean Gospel example is currently used for this construction.

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{Ambiguity controls: \tdim{kua} and
\tdim{khat}}{Ambiguity controls:  and }}\label{ambiguity-controls-and}

Numeral interpretation still requires constructional control in two
high-risk areas.

{\def\LTcaptype{none} % do not increment counter
\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Form
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Numeral-side interpretation
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Competing interpretation
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Caution
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\tdim{kua} & \glossquote{nine} in numeral compounds such as
\tdim{sawmkua} and larger arithmetic frames & interrogative
\glossquote{who} & treat as numeral only in clearly numeral
constructions \\
\tdim{khat} & cardinal \glossquote{one} & indefinite-like NP uses & do
not overgeneralize indefinite-like uses as pure numeral syntax \\
\end{longtable}
}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:num-mi-khat}
\gll \tdimword{mì} \tdimword{khàt} \\
     person one \\
\glt \glossquote{a man} / \glossquote{one person} (Genesis 32:24)
\end{exe}

The interrogative control remains explicit: \textbf{Hihte kua ahi hiam?}
is a \tdim{kua} \glossquote{who} clause, not numeral evidence.

No equally clean Gospel example is currently used for this construction.

\subsubsection{Deferred and boundary
material}\label{deferred-and-boundary-material-4}

This section does not yet settle the full classifier system, full
quantification patterns, the full range of indefinite-like uses of
\tdim{khat}, all interrogative-side \tdim{kua} behavior, or broader NP
syntax. It therefore keeps numeral claims narrow where overlap with
quantifiers, NP structure, and clause-level interpretation remains
unresolved.

\subsection{Quantifiers}\label{quantifiers}

The current quantifier evidence centers on \tdim{khempeuh}
\glossquote{all}, \tdim{pawlkhat} \glossquote{some people},
\tdim{kuamah} \glossquote{nobody}, \tdim{bangmah} \glossquote{nothing},
and noun-plus-quantifier phrases such as \tdim{mi tampi}
\glossquote{many people}.

\subsubsection{Overview of quantification in
Tedim}\label{overview-of-quantification-in-tedim}

The safest current analysis supports a small set of quantifier domains
rather than a single neat paradigm. Universal quantification is clearest
with \tdim{khempeuh} \glossquote{all}. Existential and partitive-like
quantification is visible with \tdim{pawlkhat}
\glossquote{some group / some}, but this remains construction-sensitive.
Negative quantification is clearest with \tdim{kuamah}
\glossquote{nobody} and \tdim{bangmah} \glossquote{nothing / anything}
in clauses licensed by negation. Quantity expressions are represented by
\tdim{tampi} \glossquote{many}, especially in noun phrases such as
\tdim{mi} \glossquote{person} \tdim{tampi} \glossquote{many}
\glossquote{many people} and in degree-like expressions such as
\tdim{tampi} \glossquote{many} \tdim{tak} \glossquote{truly}
\glossquote{very many / exceedingly}
\citep{henderson1965, zamngaihcing2017}.

Quantification therefore overlaps with numeral interpretation, noun
phrase structure, and negation. The section keeps those interfaces
explicit instead of collapsing them into one category.

\subsubsection{Quantifier inventory}\label{quantifier-inventory}

{\def\LTcaptype{none} % do not increment counter
\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Form
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Provisional function
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Current status
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Notes
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\tdim{khempeuh} & universal / total quantifier & core & strongest
universal anchor \\
\tdim{pawlkhat} & existential / partitive-like quantifier & core with
caveat & grouping and partitive nuance remains important \\
\tdim{kuamah} & negative quantifier (human) & core with caveat &
requires clear negation licensing \\
\tdim{bangmah} & negative quantifier (nonhuman) & core with caveat &
requires clear negation licensing; has bang-family overlap outside this
use \\
\tdim{tampi}; \tdim{mi tampi} & quantity expression & core with caveat &
overlaps with broader degree-modification behavior \\
\tdim{khat} in \tdim{mi khat} & numeral / indefinite boundary form &
boundary & kept explicitly tied to numeral overlap \\
\tdim{peuhpeuh} & free-choice or distributive-looking form & deferred
boundary & not yet stabilized as core quantifier evidence \\
\tdim{tawm} & low-quantity edge form & deferred boundary & current
evidence remains too noisy for promotion \\
\tdim{zaw} & comparative edge form & boundary & visible, but not part of
a full comparison analysis \\
\tdim{mahmah} & intensifier edge form & boundary & visible, but not part
of a full intensifier analysis \\
\end{longtable}
}

\subsubsection{Universal quantification}\label{universal-quantification}

\tdim{Khempeuh} \glossquote{all} is the clearest total-quantifier anchor
in the present evidence.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:quant-khempeuh}
\gll \tdimword{vàn-tùng} \tdimword{léi-tùng} \tdimword{lè} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{sùng-à} \tdimword{ōm-tè} \tdimword{khèmpeuh} \\
     sky-on land-on and \textsc{3sg} inside-\textsc{3sg} exist-\textsc{pl} all \\
\glt \glossquote{the heavens and the earth, and all that was in them} (Genesis 2:1)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:quant-mi-khempeuh}
\gll \tdimword{mì} \tdimword{khèmpeuh} \\
     person all \\
\glt \glossquote{all people} / \glossquote{everyone} (Luke 2:1)
\end{exe}

Together these examples support a conservative claim: universal
quantification is clearly available in noun phrase contexts, and
\tdim{khempeuh} \glossquote{all} can be treated as the central form for
this function.

\subsubsection{Existential / partitive-like
quantification}\label{existential-partitive-like-quantification}

\tdim{Pawlkhat} is the main existential or partitive-like form, but the
interpretation remains construction-sensitive.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:quant-pawlkhat}
\gll \tdimword{pàwl-khàt} \\
     some-one \\
\glt \glossquote{one group} / \glossquote{one company} (Genesis 32:8)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:quant-mi-pawlkhat}
\gll \tdimword{mì} \tdimword{pàwl-khàt} \\
     person some-one \\
\glt \glossquote{some people} (Matthew 2:1)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:quant-mi-khat}
\gll \tdimword{mì} \tdimword{khàt} \\
     person one \\
\glt \glossquote{a man} / \glossquote{one person} (Genesis 32:24)
\end{exe}

These examples justify a cautious description: \tdim{pawlkhat}
\glossquote{some group / some} supports partitive-like or grouped
existential readings, while \tdim{mi} \glossquote{person} \tdim{khat}
\glossquote{one} remains boundary material between numeral and
indefinite-like interpretation.

\subsubsection{Negative quantifiers and
negation}\label{negative-quantifiers-and-negation}

\tdim{Kuamah} and \tdim{bangmah} are treated as quantifiers only where
clause-level negation licenses them.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:quant-kuamah}
\gll \tdimword{kuamah} \tdimword{mú} \tdimword{lò} \\
     nobody see.\textsc{i} \textsc{neg} \\
\glt \glossquote{he saw nobody} / \glossquote{he saw no man} (Exodus 2:12)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:quant-bangmah}
\gll \tdimword{bangmah} \tdimword{ōm} \tdimword{lò} \tdimword{hì} \\
     nothing exist \textsc{neg} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{there is nothing} / \glossquote{nothing exists} (Genesis 39:9)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:quant-kuamah-bangmah}
\gll \tdimword{kuamah} \tdimword{ìn} \tdimword{bangmah} \tdimword{híh} \tdimword{théi} \tdimword{lò} \tdimword{hì} \\
     nobody \textsc{erg} nothing \textsc{prox} know \textsc{neg} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{nobody can do anything} (John 3:27)
\end{exe}

The section therefore aligns with the negation analysis without
duplicating it: negative quantifier interpretation depends on explicit
negation licensing, and bang-family material outside that environment is
kept separate from this core description.

\subsubsection{Quantity expressions}\label{quantity-expressions}

Quantity is represented by \tdim{tampi} \glossquote{many}, both in
degree-like expression and in noun phrase quantification.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:quant-tampi-tak}
\gll \tdimword{tampi} \tdimword{tak} \\
     many truly \\
\glt \glossquote{very many} / \glossquote{exceedingly} (Genesis 17:2)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:quant-mi-tampi}
\gll \tdimword{mì} \tdimword{tampi} \\
     person many \\
\glt \glossquote{many people} (Mark 6:34)
\end{exe}

These rows support a narrow claim: quantity expressions are clearly
available, but they overlap with broader degree and modification systems
that are not resolved here.

\subsubsection{Boundary with numerals}\label{boundary-with-numerals}

The numerals section remains the primary analysis for counting and
ordinal material. In this section, \tdim{khat} \glossquote{one} is kept
primarily on the numeral side unless context clearly supports an
indefinite-like reading, and \tdim{pawlkhat}
\glossquote{some group / some} is not reduced to a simple compositional
reading of \glossquote{one} plus an unspecified element. Ordinal and
counting constructions are therefore not absorbed into quantifier
analysis.

\subsubsection{Boundary with NP structure and
negation}\label{boundary-with-np-structure-and-negation}

Noun-plus-quantifier patterns such as \tdim{mi} \glossquote{person}
\tdim{khempeuh} \glossquote{all} \glossquote{all people}, \tdim{mi}
\glossquote{person} \tdim{pawlkhat} \glossquote{some group / some}
\glossquote{some people}, and \tdim{mi} \glossquote{person} \tdim{tampi}
\glossquote{many} \glossquote{many people} also belong to the NP
structure domain. Likewise, negative quantifiers belong partly to the
negation domain because their interpretation is licensed by clause-level
negation. The present section therefore coordinates with NP structure
and negation without absorbing their full analyses.

\subsubsection{Deferred material}\label{deferred-material-3}

This section does not yet settle full free-choice quantification, all
indefinite uses, intensifier systems, comparative and degree morphology,
or the complete NP template. \tdim{Peuhpeuh} \glossquote{every, each},
\tdim{tawm} \glossquote{few}, \tdim{zaw} \glossquote{comparative edge},
and \tdim{mahmah} \glossquote{very, truly} remain visible as boundary or
deferred material while core quantifier claims are kept narrow.

\reviewchapterbreak

\section{Predicate structure and verbal
morphology}\label{predicate-structure-and-verbal-morphology}

\subsection{Stem alternation}\label{stem-alternation}

The current stem-alternation evidence supports a controlled Form I /
Form II contrast around \tdim{mu / muh} \glossquote{see},
\tdim{ne / nek} \glossquote{eat}, and \tdim{nei / neih}
\glossquote{have}, while promoted caveated pairs, one-sided controls,
and blocked rows remain explicit boundaries.

\subsubsection{Overview of Form I / Form II stem
alternation}\label{overview-of-form-i-form-ii-stem-alternation}

Tedim has a two-form stem alternation system. Henderson describes this
contrast as Form I and Form II, while Zam Ngaih Cing uses Stem 1 and
Stem 2 terminology \citep{henderson1965, zamngaihcing2017}. The same
descriptive pattern is visible in present corpus-backed examples.

This section keeps to a controlled grammar-facing account. It shows
stable contrasts first, then keeps difficult, one-sided, and noisy
material visible without turning this chapter into a complete monograph
on verbal stem alternation.

\subsubsection{Current stem alternation
overview}\label{current-stem-alternation-overview}

{\def\LTcaptype{none} % do not increment counter
\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Form
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Typical distribution
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Strongest examples
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Caveat
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Form I & especially common in finite and main-clause predication &
\tdim{mu} \glossquote{see}, \tdim{ne} \glossquote{eat}, \tdim{nei}
\glossquote{have} & Form II can also occur in matrix clauses for some
lexemes \\
Form II & especially common in dependent and constructionally bound
contexts & \tdim{muh} \glossquote{see.II}, \tdim{nek}
\glossquote{eat.II}, \tdim{neih} \glossquote{have-NOM} & Form II is not
restricted to one context such as negation or nominalization \\
\end{longtable}
}

\subsubsection{Distribution by syntactic
context}\label{distribution-by-syntactic-context}

The strongest distributional pattern is construction-sensitive rather
than mechanical. \tdim{Ciangin} \glossquote{when} and \tdim{ni-in}
\glossquote{on the day / when} are productive temporal frames that often
favor Form II in dependent clauses. \tdim{Kipan}
\glossquote{from / since} also hosts clause-linking evidence, while
purposive \tdim{nadingin} \glossquote{in order to} favors non-final
forms in several promoted pairs.

Nominalized \tdim{-na} \glossquote{nominalizer} contexts and attributive
\tdim{mi} \glossquote{person / one who} contexts add further evidence,
but they require careful filtering so lexicalized compounds and
category-mixed tokens are not overpromoted as simple stem alternants.

The practical distribution can therefore be summarized as follows:

{\def\LTcaptype{none} % do not increment counter
\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Syntactic context
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Form tendency in current evidence
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Representative pairs
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Caution
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
finite and main-clause predication & Form I most transparent &
\tdim{mu / muh}, \tdim{ne / nek}, \tdim{nei / neih} & some Form II
matrix tokens remain real \\
imperatives and directives & Form I usually clearest & \tdim{za / zak},
\tdim{bia / biak} & directive evidence does not define the whole
system \\
negative clauses & mixed but informative & \tdim{ne / nek},
\tdim{nei / neih}, \tdim{thei / theih} & Form II does not simply equal
negation \\
dependent temporal clauses (\tdim{ciangin}, \tdim{ni-in}) & Form II
often strongest & \tdim{muh}, \tdim{zak}, \tdim{theih}, \tdim{nek} &
temporal contexts are strong but not exclusive diagnostics \\
clause-linking with \tdim{kipan} and purposive \tdim{nadingin} & Form II
often constructionally prominent & \tdim{nusia / nusiat},
\tdim{pia / piak}, \tdim{bia / biak} & many rows stay caveated \\
nominalized \tdim{-na} and attributive/relative \tdim{mi} contexts &
Form II frequently visible & \tdim{neih}, \tdim{muhna}, \tdim{theihna} &
lexicalized nouns must be filtered \\
modal and ability uses & pair-specific behavior & \tdim{thei / theih} &
modal pairs should not be forced into a simple finite/non-finite
slogan \\
quotative and complement contexts & mixed and often functional &
\tdim{ci / cih} boundary & functional predicates can obscure lexical
pair behavior \\
\end{longtable}
}

\subsubsection{Core showcase pairs}\label{core-showcase-pairs}

The core showcase remains \tdim{mu / muh} \glossquote{see},
\tdim{ne / nek} \glossquote{eat}, and \tdim{nei / neih}
\glossquote{have}. These pairs still give the clearest first pass
through Form I and Form II contrasts.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:stem-mu-gen1}
\gll \tdimword{Pasian} \tdimword{ìn} \tdimword{túa} \tdimword{khuavak} \tdimword{hòih} \tdimword{hì,} \tdimword{cí-ìn} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{mú} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     God \textsc{erg} \textsc{dist} light good \textsc{decl} say-\textsc{quot} \textsc{3sg} see.\textsc{i} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{God saw the light, that it was good} (Genesis 1:4)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:stem-muh-gen19}
\gll \tdimword{Lot} \tdimword{ìn} \tdimword{àmaute} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{mùh} \tdimword{cìang-ìn,} \tdimword{àmaute} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{dawn} \tdimword{dìng-ìn} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{à.} \\
     \textsc{lot} \textsc{erg} \textsc{3pl}.\textsc{pro} \textsc{3sg} see.\textsc{ii} then-\textsc{erg} \textsc{3pl}.\textsc{pro} \textsc{3sg} top \textsc{irr}-\textsc{erg} \textsc{irr} \textsc{3sg} \\
\glt \glossquote{when Lot saw them, he rose to meet them} (Genesis 19:1)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:stem-ne-gen2}
\gll \tdimword{Àhìh} \tdimword{hàng-ìn} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{phà} \tdimword{lè} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{sía} \tdimword{thèih-ná} \tdimword{sing-kung} \tdimword{gah} \tdimword{pèn} \tdimword{nà} \tdimword{né} \tdimword{kèi} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     be.\textsc{3sg}.\textsc{rel} because-\textsc{erg} \textsc{3sg} branch and \textsc{3sg} evil know.\textsc{ii}-\textsc{nmlz} tree branch \textsc{top} \textsc{2sg} eat \textsc{neg} \textsc{irr} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{but you shall not eat of the tree of the knowledge of good and evil} (Genesis 2:17)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:stem-nek-gen2}
\gll \tdimword{Bàng} \tdimword{hàng} \tdimword{hìam} \tdimword{cìh} \tdimword{lèh} \tdimword{túa} \tdimword{nà} \tdimword{nèk} \tdimword{nì-ìn} \tdimword{nà} \tdimword{sí} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     like reason \textsc{q} say.\textsc{nom} and \textsc{dist} \textsc{2sg} eat.\textsc{ii} day-\textsc{erg} \textsc{2sg} die \textsc{irr} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{for in the day that you eat of it, you will surely die} (Genesis 2:17)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:stem-nei-gen11}
\gll \tdimword{Tù-ìn} \tdimword{Sarai} \tdimword{ciing} \tdimword{à,} \tdimword{tá} \tdimword{néi} \tdimword{lò} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     now-\textsc{erg} \textsc{sarai} barren \textsc{3sg} child have \textsc{neg} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{now Sarai was barren; she had no child} (Genesis 11:30)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:stem-neih-2sam23}
\gll \tdimword{David'} \tdimword{nèih} \tdimword{mì} \tdimword{thahatte'} \tdimword{mīn-tè.} \\
     \textsc{david}.\textsc{poss} have-\textsc{nom} person strong-\textsc{pl}.\textsc{poss} name-\textsc{pl} \\
\glt \glossquote{the names of David's mighty men} (2 Samuel 23:8)
\end{exe}

No equally clean Gospel example is currently used for these exact
core-pair contrasts, so Gospel comparanda remain prose-only at this
stage for the core showcase.

\subsubsection{Promoted caveated pairs}\label{promoted-caveated-pairs}

Beyond the core, the strongest promoted caveated rows include
\tdim{za / zak} \glossquote{hear / listen}, \tdim{pia / piak}
\glossquote{give}, \tdim{nusia / nusiat} \glossquote{leave},
\tdim{bia / biak} \glossquote{speak / worship / address},
\tdim{thei / theih} \glossquote{can / be able}, \tdim{piang / pian}
\glossquote{be born / arise}, \tdim{zui / zuih} \glossquote{follow},
\tdim{khial / khialh} \glossquote{err / sin}, \tdim{kia / kiak}
\glossquote{fall}, and \tdim{sawlkhia / sawlkhiat}
\glossquote{send out}.

  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Pair
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright

Caveat
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\tdim{za / zak} & directive Form I plus dependent Form II evidence is
real & Form I citation quality is weaker because of clause-initial
punctuation and discourse packaging \\
\tdim{pia / piak} & both sides are robustly attested in transfer
predicates & many rows are context-crowded and not ideal for first
teaching examples \\
\tdim{nusia / nusiat} & Form II appears in clause-linking structure with
\tdim{kipan} & best Form II row is still descriptive and
category-sensitive \\
\tdim{bia / biak} & both forms are real in worship and purposive frames
& distribution is strongly domain-specific \\
\tdim{thei / theih} & modal and dependent uses show genuine alternation
& this pair is constructionally special and not a simple finite
contrast \\
\tdim{piang / pian} & eventive and dependent rows survive with good
signal & derived \tdim{piangsak} family requires explicit filtering \\
\tdim{zui / zuih} & conditional and non-final Form II evidence is
visible & Form II quotations still need conservative selection \\
\tdim{khial / khialh} & clean dependent Form II evidence survives &
derivational families remain nearby \\
\tdim{kia / kiak} & exact \tdim{kiak} rows survive and should remain
visible & Form II lemma stability still needs philological checking \\
\tdim{sawlkhia / sawlkhiat} & both forms are attested, including a
usable Gospel Form II row & Form II evidence remains sparse \\
\end{longtable}
}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:stem-sawlkhia-ps43}
\gll \tdimword{Nà} \tdimword{khuavak} \tdimword{lè} \tdimword{nà} \tdimword{thù-màn} \tdimword{hòng} \tdimword{sawlkhia} \tdimword{ìnlà.} \\
     \textsc{2sg} light and \textsc{2sg} word-reason \textsc{3→1} send.forth and.then \\
\glt \glossquote{send out your light and your truth} (Psalms 43:3)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:stem-sawlkhiat-luke22}
\gll \tdimword{Nòtè} \tdimword{ìn} \tdimword{sum-bawm,} \tdimword{ip,} \tdimword{lè} \tdimword{khedap} \tdimword{keng} \tdimword{lò-ìn} \tdimword{kòng} \tdimword{sawlkhiat} \tdimword{lái-ìn} \tdimword{kī-tāng-sap-ná} \tdimword{nà} \tdimword{néi} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{hìam?} \\
     \textsc{2pl}.\textsc{pro} \textsc{erg} money-bag cover and shoe only \textsc{neg}-\textsc{erg} \textsc{1sg→3} send.forth midst-\textsc{erg} \textsc{refl}-force-call-\textsc{nmlz} \textsc{2sg} have 2/3PL \textsc{q} \\
\glt \glossquote{when I sent you without purse, bag, or sandals, did you lack anything} (Luke 22:35)
\end{exe}

\subsubsection{Difficult but grammatically important
pairs}\label{difficult-but-grammatically-important-pairs}

The difficult set remains central: \tdim{ngai / ngaih}
\glossquote{need / love / listen}, \tdim{pua / puak}
\glossquote{carry.on.back / spill}, \tdim{pai / paih} \glossquote{go},
\tdim{tua / tuah} \glossquote{do}, and \tdim{tua / tuak}
\glossquote{meet / receive}. These pairs matter because they reveal
lexical-family contamination, one-sided evidence, shared-base
competition, and analyzer overgeneration.

\tdim{Ngai / ngaih} \glossquote{need / love / listen} is especially
important. Exact verbal rows exist, but the \tdim{ngaihsun} and
\tdim{ngaihsutna} family can still contaminate the argument unless it is
filtered explicitly.

\subsubsection{One-sided and same-form
controls}\label{one-sided-and-same-form-controls}

The current one-sided or constructionally skewed controls include
\tdim{bawl / bawlh}, \tdim{dipkua / dipkuat}, \tdim{gen / genh},
\tdim{hawlkhia / hawlkhiat}, \tdim{husia / husiat}, \tdim{kho / khoh},
\tdim{kido / kidot}, \tdim{lua / luah}, \tdim{tu / tuh},
\tdim{tuahpha / tuahphat}, and \tdim{vial / vialh}.

Same-form controls are also important: \tdim{dawn / dawn},
\tdim{hong / hong}, \tdim{om / om}, \tdim{ci / ci}, \tdim{hi / hi},
\tdim{bawl / bawl}, \tdim{zui / zui}, and \tdim{pai / pai}. These
controls stay visible because they test questionnaire and corpus
alignment, but they are not overt alternating pairs in the present Bible
layer.

\subsubsection{Blocked or noisy
material}\label{blocked-or-noisy-material}

Representative blocked rows include \tdim{keu / keuh},
\tdim{khai / khaih}, \tdim{sia / siah}, \tdim{tan / tanh},
\tdim{mual / mualh}, \tdim{sum / sumh}, \tdim{thu / thuh},
\tdim{lampi / lampih}, \tdim{khua / khuat}, and \tdim{gamla / gamlat}.

These rows remain blocked for clear grammatical reasons: nominal
compounds, lexicalized families, derivational \tdim{-sak}
\glossquote{causative / benefactive} overlap, homophone and shared-base
contamination, category mismatch, and analyzer overgeneration. Keeping
them visible matters, but promoting them as ordinary stem alternation
evidence would overstate the data.

\subsubsection{Deferred and boundary
material}\label{deferred-and-boundary-material-5}

Several issues remain outside the present account.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Core pairs such as \tdim{mu / muh} \glossquote{see}, \tdim{ne / nek}
  \glossquote{eat}, and \tdim{nei / neih} \glossquote{have} are strong
  enough for grammar-facing use, but they do not exhaust the alternation
  system.
\item
  Promoted caveated pairs remain uneven in evidence quality, especially
  where Form II is sparse or constructionally restricted.
\item
  Difficult pairs such as \tdim{ngai / ngaih}
  \glossquote{need / love / listen} and \tdim{pua / puak}
  \glossquote{carry.on.back / spill} remain grammatically important
  without being fully settled.
\item
  One-sided and same-form controls remain necessary for coverage, even
  when they are not overt alternating pairs.
\item
  Blocked rows remain visible as explicit exclusions so nominal,
  lexicalized, or analyzer-noisy material is not converted into grammar
  facts.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Summary}\label{summary-4}

Tedim stem alternation can now be stated in controlled grammar-facing
prose: Form I and Form II contrasts are real, the core showcase pairs
remain stable, and distribution by syntactic context is stronger than
any one-line slogan. At the same time, promoted caveated rows, difficult
pairs, one-sided controls, and blocked material remain explicit so the
section stays accurate without overclaiming.

\subsection{Verb paradigms}\label{verb-paradigms}

The current verb-paradigm evidence is strongest for finite predicate
frames such as \tdim{kanei} \glossquote{I have}, \tdim{na si ding hi}
\glossquote{2SG die IRR DECL}, and \tdim{a en uh hi}
\glossquote{3SG look.at PL DECL}, while negation-heavy, TAM-heavy,
stem-alternation, and object-prefix rows remain boundary material.

\subsubsection{Overview of basic finite verb paradigms in
Tedim}\label{overview-of-basic-finite-verb-paradigms-in-tedim}

This section is a finite-predicate-frame and person-marked-predicate
first slice within the broader verb-paradigm domain. It focuses on the
safest finite clause anchors and keeps adjacent domains explicit as
boundaries.

The strongest starting anchors are \tdim{ka nei hi}
\glossquote{1SG-have DECL}, \tdim{na si ding hi}
\glossquote{2SG die IRR DECL}, \tdim{a suak hi}
\glossquote{3SG become DECL}, and \tdim{a en uh hi}
\glossquote{3SG look.at PL DECL}. These support finite-frame analysis
without expanding into chapter-scale verbal morphology.

Surface orthography is kept in prose and inventory forms (for example
\tdim{ka nei hi}), while segmented analysis is kept in segmentation
tiers and parse-oriented discussion (for example \tdim{ka-nei}
\glossquote{1SG-have}).

This section is not a complete verbal paradigm system, not a full TAM
chapter, not a full negation chapter, not a full stem-alternation
chapter, not a full agreement chapter, not a full object-prefix chapter,
not a full transitivity chapter, and not a full voice chapter.

\subsubsection{Basic finite paradigm
inventory}\label{basic-finite-paradigm-inventory}

\subsubsection{Safest finite predicate
frame}\label{safest-finite-predicate-frame}

The most stable frame in this first slice is a person-marked predicate
followed by clause-final \tdim{hi} in declarative contexts. In this
section, \tdim{hi} is treated as a clause-final element in selected
finite frames, not as an independent account of the full sentence-final
system.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:vp-frame-gen21}
\gll \tdimword{Àhì} \tdimword{zòng-ìn} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{teek} \tdimword{khit-ìn} \tdimword{àmàh} \tdimword{tàwh} \tdimword{tá-pà} \tdimword{khàt} \tdimword{kà} \tdimword{néi} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     be.\textsc{3sg} although-\textsc{erg} \textsc{3sg} master \textsc{compl}-\textsc{erg} \textsc{3sg}.\textsc{pro} \textsc{com} child-male one \textsc{1sg} have \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{Yet when she was old, I have a son by him.} (Genesis 21:7)
\end{exe}

Matthew 27:36 provides the Gospel comparandum for the same finite frame.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:vp-frame-matt27}
\gll \tdimword{Jesuh} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{ēn} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{jesuh} \textsc{3sg} look 2/3PL \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{They watched Jesus.} (Matthew 27:36)
\end{exe}

Together these rows show a finite frame that is useful for
grammar-facing description even before a full paradigm grid is
attempted.

\subsubsection{Safest person-marked finite
predicates}\label{safest-person-marked-finite-predicates}

The clearest person-marked finite examples currently available show
second-, third-, and first-person subject-marking patterns in controlled
clauses.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:vp-person-gen2}
\gll \tdimword{Bàng} \tdimword{hàng} \tdimword{hìam} \tdimword{cìh} \tdimword{lèh} \tdimword{túa} \tdimword{nà} \tdimword{nèk} \tdimword{nì-ìn} \tdimword{nà} \tdimword{sí} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     like reason \textsc{q} say.\textsc{nom} and \textsc{dist} \textsc{2sg} eat.\textsc{ii} day-\textsc{erg} \textsc{2sg} die \textsc{irr} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{For in the day that you eat of it, you will surely die.} (Genesis 2:17)
\end{exe}

Genesis 26:13 adds a third-person finite predicate row.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:vp-person-gen26}
\gll \tdimword{à} \tdimword{hau} \tdimword{màhmàh} \tdimword{khàt} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{sùak} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{3sg} rich very one \textsc{3sg} wither \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{He became very rich.} (Genesis 26:13)
\end{exe}

John 4:17 supplies the Gospel first-person row.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:vp-person-john4}
\reviewwarninginline{[REVIEW PREVIEW WARNING: ex:vp-person-john4: Bible reference mapped, but slice example is not a direct verse span; analyzed slice wording]}
\gll \tdimword{Nu-méi} \tdimword{ìn,} \tdimword{pasal} \tdimword{kà} \tdimword{néi} \tdimword{kèi} \tdimword{hì"} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{cí} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     woman \textsc{erg} husband \textsc{1sg} have \textsc{neg} \textsc{decl} \textsc{3sg} say \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{The woman said, "I have no husband."} (John 4:17)
\end{exe}

These rows are enough for a narrow person-marked finite-predicate claim,
while still leaving larger person-prefix architecture to dedicated
sections.

\subsubsection{A small set of paradigm-friendly verb
anchors}\label{a-small-set-of-paradigm-friendly-verb-anchors}

For this first slice, the safest lexical anchors are \tdim{nei}
\glossquote{have} and \tdim{suak} \glossquote{become}, with \tdim{en}
\glossquote{look at} as supporting finite-frame evidence. This keeps the
section finite-frame oriented instead of trying to normalize every verb
class at once.

\subsubsection{Boundary with negation and
TAM}\label{boundary-with-negation-and-tam}

Finite-frame evidence intersects negation and TAM quickly. Rows such as
\tdim{ta nei lo hi} and \tdim{lutthei ding uh hi} remain visible, but
they are treated as boundary material here so the established negation
and TAM sections remain authoritative for those analyses. The promoted
rows \tdim{na si ding hi} and \tdim{ka nei kei hi} are kept because
their finite-frame and person-marking value is clear, while TAM/negation
interpretation remains boundary-routed.

\subsubsection{Boundary with stem
alternation}\label{boundary-with-stem-alternation}

The \tdim{mu / muh} \glossquote{see} family remains a key boundary. It
is important for paradigms, but the Form I/Form II distribution and
caveats are handled in the dedicated stem-alternation section.

\subsubsection{Boundary with object-prefix / inverse-like
material}\label{boundary-with-object-prefix-inverse-like-material}

Participant-oriented object-prefix material remains outside the core
finite table in this section. The person-sensitive prefix contrasts are
already treated in the dedicated hong/kong section and stay
boundary-controlled here.

\subsubsection{Boundary with analyzer-gap and report-table-only
material}\label{boundary-with-analyzer-gap-and-report-table-only-material}

Analyzer-gap material and report-table-only artifacts remain blocked or
deferred in this first slice. Inclusive report paradigms (\tdim{i pai})
and artifact-prone rows such as report-table \tdim{ka pai}
(\tdim{kap-ai} segmentation) are kept visible as limits rather than
promoted as settled grammar facts.

Clause-final \tdim{hi} alignment is likewise kept narrow here: this
section uses \tdim{hi} only for the selected finite frames above, while
broader treatment remains in the sentence-final particles section and in
TAM/negation overlap discussions.

\subsubsection{Deferred material}\label{deferred-material-4}

Several issues remain outside the present account.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Full person-prefix paradigms, including clusivity and plural routing,
  remain for dedicated sections.
\item
  Full TAM and modal stacking remains outside this finite-frame slice.
\item
  Full negation architecture remains outside this finite-frame slice.
\item
  Full stem alternation, voice-like behavior, and object-prefix
  selection remain outside this finite-frame slice.
\item
  Raw report counts or report-table templates are not used as grammar
  facts by themselves.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Summary}\label{summary-5}

The strongest defensible claim remains modest: Tedim has stable finite
predicate frames and a small set of clear person-marked finite anchors
that can already be described in grammar-facing prose. This section is
therefore a finite-predicate-frame/person-marked first slice, and it
keeps TAM, negation, stem alternation, object-prefix, and analyzer-gap
issues explicit as boundaries rather than treating them as solved
paradigm coverage.

\subsection{Prefix / agreement}\label{prefix-agreement}

The current prefix-routing evidence is strongest for host-sensitive
contrast between \tdim{kanei} \glossquote{I have} and \tdim{kainn}
\glossquote{my house}, where \tdim{ka-nei} \glossquote{1SG-have}
patterns as verbal agreement and \tdim{ka-inn}
\glossquote{1SG.POSS-house} patterns as nominal possession, while
\tdim{hongmu} \glossquote{see me / come-see} and \tdim{kongmu}
\glossquote{I see you / 1SG-see.2} remain boundary material.

\subsubsection{Overview of prefix and agreement routing in this
section}\label{overview-of-prefix-and-agreement-routing-in-this-section}

This section treats a controlled routing contrast in Tedim pronominal
prefixes. The forms \tdim{kanei} \glossquote{I have} and \tdim{kainn}
\glossquote{my house} show that the same prefixal material can be
analyzed as agreement before a verbal host and as possession before a
nominal host. The segmented forms \tdim{ka-nei} \glossquote{1SG-have}
and \tdim{ka-inn} \glossquote{1SG.POSS-house} make that host-sensitive
contrast explicit.

\subsubsection{Current prefix and agreement
inventory}\label{current-prefix-and-agreement-inventory}

{\def\LTcaptype{none} % do not increment counter

  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Form or pattern
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Rough function
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright

\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright

Boundary issue
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\tdim{kanei} & agreement before a verbal host & \tdim{ka nei} clauses &
well supported & broader person-prefix paradigm remains open \\
\tdim{ka-nei} & segmented agreement form & \tdim{1SG-have} parsing &
well supported & does not settle all verbal prefix routing \\
\tdim{kainn} & possession before a nominal host & \tdim{ka inn} clauses
& well supported & broader possessor syntax remains open \\
\tdim{ka-inn} & segmented possessive form & \tdim{1SG.POSS-house}
parsing & well supported & does not settle apostrophe possession \\
\tdim{ainn} & third-person nominal prefix row & \tdim{a inn} phrases &
boundary material & shared \tdim{a-} family overlaps other domains \\
\tdim{ipai} & independent pronoun/clusivity boundary row & \tdim{i pai}
patterns & boundary material & belongs mainly with pronoun and clusivity
analysis \\
\tdim{hongmu} / \tdim{kongmu} & object-prefix or inverse-like boundary
row & \tdim{hong mu} / \tdim{kong mu} patterns & boundary material &

\tdim{kipan} & \tdim{ki-} boundary row & \tdim{hong kipan} clauses &
boundary material & overlaps reflexive/middle and derivational
analysis \\

& boundary material & belongs mainly with broader possession analysis \\
\tdim{a bawl mi} / \tdim{ahih} & relative-clause boundary rows &
relative or clause-linkage contexts & boundary material & overlaps
relative-clause and clause-linkage structure \\
\end{longtable}
}

\subsubsection{Agreement before verbal
hosts}\label{agreement-before-verbal-hosts}

The prefix \tdim{ka-} \glossquote{first-person prefix} is treated as
agreement when it attaches to a verbal host. In this controlled
contrast, \tdim{kanei} \glossquote{I have} is the clearest anchor, and
the segmentation \tdim{ka-nei} \glossquote{1SG-have} expresses the
agreement-side analysis.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:pref-kanei-gen21}
\gll \tdimword{Àhì} \tdimword{zòng-ìn} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{teek} \tdimword{khit-ìn} \tdimword{àmàh} \tdimword{tàwh} \tdimword{tá-pà} \tdimword{khàt} \tdimword{kà} \tdimword{néi} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     be.\textsc{3sg} although-\textsc{erg} \textsc{3sg} master \textsc{compl}-\textsc{erg} \textsc{3sg}.\textsc{pro} \textsc{com} child-male one \textsc{1sg} have \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{Yet when she was old, I have a son by him.} (Genesis 21:7)
\end{exe}

A Gospel counterpart appears in John 4:17.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:pref-kanei-john4}
\reviewwarninginline{[REVIEW PREVIEW WARNING: ex:pref-kanei-john4: Bible reference mapped, but slice example is not a direct verse span; analyzed slice wording]}
\gll \tdimword{Nu-méi} \tdimword{ìn,} \tdimword{pasal} \tdimword{kà} \tdimword{néi} \tdimword{kèi} \tdimword{hì"} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{cí} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     woman \textsc{erg} husband \textsc{1sg} have \textsc{neg} \textsc{decl} \textsc{3sg} say \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{The woman said, "I have no husband."} (John 4:17)
\end{exe}

\subsubsection{Possession before nominal
hosts}\label{possession-before-nominal-hosts}

Before a nominal host, the same prefixal material is analyzed as
possession. The form \tdim{kainn} \glossquote{my house} and segmentation
\tdim{ka-inn} \glossquote{1SG.POSS-house} provide the clearest
possessive-routing anchor.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:pref-kainn-gen15}
\gll \tdimword{Tá} \tdimword{nong} \tdimword{pía} \tdimword{kèi} \tdimword{à,} \tdimword{kà} \tdimword{ínn} \tdimword{sùng-à} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{sùak} \tdimword{sila} \tdimword{ìn} \tdimword{kèima} \tdimword{gàmh} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{lùah} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{hì} \tdimword{tá} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     child \textsc{2→1} give \textsc{neg} \textsc{3sg} \textsc{1sg} house inside-\textsc{3sg} \textsc{3sg} wither servant \textsc{erg} \textsc{1sg}.self land.\textsc{ii} \textsc{3sg} carry \textsc{irr} \textsc{decl} child \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{You gave me no child, and a male servant from my house will inherit me.} (Genesis 15:3)
\end{exe}

A Gospel counterpart appears in Luke 7:6.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:pref-kainn-luke7}
\gll \tdimword{Topa} \tdimword{àw,} \tdimword{kà} \tdimword{ínn-àh} \tdimword{nong} \tdimword{pái} \tdimword{nàdìngìn} \tdimword{kèi} \tdimword{kī-lawm} \tdimword{zò} \tdimword{lò} \tdimword{kà} \tdimword{híh} \tdimword{màn-ìn} \tdimword{hòng} \tdimword{pái} \tdimword{dah} \tdimword{hoh} \tdimword{ìn.} \\
     Lord voice \textsc{1sg} house-\textsc{loc} \textsc{2→1} go \textsc{purp}.\textsc{erg} \textsc{neg} \textsc{refl}-worthy south \textsc{neg} \textsc{1sg} \textsc{prox} reason-\textsc{erg} \textsc{3→1} go put today \textsc{erg} \\
\glt \glossquote{Lord, I am not worthy for you to come under my roof.} (Luke 7:6)
\end{exe}

\subsubsection{Agreement versus possession
routing}\label{agreement-versus-possession-routing}

Agreement versus possession routing is therefore host-sensitive.
Verbal-host \tdim{ka-nei} supports agreement routing, while nominal-host
\tdim{ka-inn} supports possessive routing. This controlled contrast does
not yet settle the whole prefixal person system.

\subsubsection{Boundary with independent pronouns and
clusivity}\label{boundary-with-independent-pronouns-and-clusivity}

Independent pronoun and clusivity material remains secondary here. The
form \tdim{ipai} \glossquote{we go / inclusive go} is kept as boundary
material, because clusivity contrasts are handled primarily in the
pronoun section rather than in this host-routing contrast.

\subsubsection{Boundary with relative clauses and a-
material}\label{boundary-with-relative-clauses-and-a--material}

The form \tdim{ainn} \glossquote{his/her house} and prefix \tdim{a-}
\glossquote{third-person prefix} are important boundary material because
they overlap possession, agreement, and relative-clause analysis.
Relative-clause forms such as \tdim{a bawl mi} \glossquote{one who made}
and clause-linkage material with \tdim{ahih}
\glossquote{it is / which is} confirm that the \tdim{a-} family cannot
yet be reduced to one finished account.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:pref-abawlmi-exod30}
\gll \tdimword{Túa} \tdimword{tàwh} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{kibang-ìn} \tdimword{pāk-nam-tui} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{bāwl} \tdimword{mì} \tdimword{peuhmah} \tdimword{àmà} \tdimword{mite'} \tdimword{kìang} \tdimword{pàn-ìn} \tdimword{kī-khen-khìa} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{dist} \textsc{com} \textsc{3sg} be.alike-\textsc{cvb} ointment \textsc{3sg} make person whosoever \textsc{3sg}.\textsc{poss} person-\textsc{pl}.\textsc{poss} beside \textsc{abl}-\textsc{erg} \textsc{refl}-divide-\textsc{away} \textsc{irr} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{Whoever makes perfume like it shall be cut off from his people.} (Exodus 30:38)
\end{exe}

A Gospel counterpart appears in Matthew 13:41.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:pref-abawlmi-matt13}
\gll \tdimword{Mì-hìng} \tdimword{Tá-pà} \tdimword{ìn} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{vàn-tùng} \tdimword{mì-tè} \tdimword{hòng} \tdimword{sawl} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{à,} \tdimword{àmà} \tdimword{ukna} \tdimword{sùng-àh} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{ōm} \tdimword{migilo-tè} \tdimword{lè} \tdimword{mì} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{màwh} \tdimword{nàdìngìn} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{bāwl} \tdimword{mì} \tdimword{khèmpeuh} \tdimword{kai-khàwm-ìn.} \\
     person-kind child-male \textsc{erg} \textsc{3sg} sky-on person-\textsc{pl} \textsc{3→1} send \textsc{irr} \textsc{3sg} \textsc{3sg}.\textsc{poss} rule.\textsc{nmlz} inside-\textsc{loc} \textsc{3sg} exist enemy-\textsc{pl} and person \textsc{3sg} sin \textsc{purp}.\textsc{erg} \textsc{3sg} make person all gather-together-\textsc{erg} \\
\glt \glossquote{The Son of Man will send his angels, and they will gather all who cause sin.} (Matthew 13:41)
\end{exe}

\subsubsection{Boundary with object-prefix or inverse-like
material}\label{boundary-with-object-prefix-or-inverse-like-material}

Object-prefix or inverse-like material is kept as boundary evidence.
\tdim{hongmu} \glossquote{SAP-oriented see / venitive support} and
\tdim{kongmu} \glossquote{I see you / 1→2} remain visible because they
are part of the same prefixal domain, but they are not promoted here to
a full object-prefix or inverse chapter.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:pref-kongmu-jer7}
\gll \tdimword{Kèimah} \tdimword{ìn} \tdimword{nòtè'} \tdimword{híh} \tdimword{khèmpeuh} \tdimword{kòng} \tdimword{mú} \tdimword{khín} \tdimword{hì,} \tdimword{Topa} \tdimword{ìn} \tdimword{kòng} \tdimword{cí} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{1sg}.\textsc{pro} \textsc{erg} \textsc{2pl}.\textsc{pro}.\textsc{poss} \textsc{prox} all \textsc{1sg→3} see.\textsc{i} \textsc{imm} \textsc{decl} Lord \textsc{erg} \textsc{1sg→3} say \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{I have seen all these things, says the Lord.} (Jeremiah 7:11)
\end{exe}

A Gospel counterpart appears in Matthew 25:37.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:pref-hongmu-matt25}
\reviewwarninginline{[REVIEW PREVIEW WARNING: ex:pref-hongmu-matt25: Bible reference mapped, but slice example is not a direct verse span; analyzed slice wording]}
\gll \tdimword{Topa} \tdimword{àw,} \tdimword{cí-kin} \tdimword{nà} \tdimword{gil-kial} \tdimword{hòng} \tdimword{mú-ìn} \tdimword{àn} \tdimword{hòng} \tdimword{pía} \tdimword{kà} \tdimword{hì} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{hìam?} \\
     Lord voice say-half \textsc{2sg} bowels \textsc{3→1} see-\textsc{erg} \textsc{3pl}.\textsc{poss} \textsc{3→1} give \textsc{1sg} \textsc{decl} 2/3PL \textsc{q} \\
\glt \glossquote{Lord, when did we see you hungry and give you food?} (Matthew 25:37)
\end{exe}

\subsubsection{Boundary with ki-}\label{boundary-with-ki-}

The form \tdim{kipan} \glossquote{from / begin / REFLEXIVE-boundary}
remains boundary-only material. It overlaps reflexive/middle analysis
and wider derivational behavior, so it is not treated here as a settled
agreement prefix.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:pref-kipan-gen4}
\gll \tdimword{Túa} \tdimword{hùn} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{ki-pan-ìn} \tdimword{mì-tè} \tdimword{ìn} \tdimword{Topa} \tdimword{bía-ìn} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{mīn} \tdimword{làwh} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{kī-pan} \tdimword{tá} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{dist} time \textsc{3sg} from person-\textsc{pl} \textsc{erg} Lord worship-\textsc{erg} \textsc{3sg} name \textsc{perf} \textsc{irr} \textsc{refl}-begin child 2/3PL \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{From that time people began to call on the name of the Lord.} (Genesis 4:26)
\end{exe}

A Gospel counterpart appears in Matthew 4:17.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:pref-kipan-matt4}
\gll \tdimword{Túa} \tdimword{hùn} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{kī-pan} \tdimword{Jesuh} \tdimword{ìn} \tdimword{thù-hìlh} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{kī-pan} \tdimword{à.} \\
     \textsc{dist} time \textsc{3sg} \textsc{refl}-begin \textsc{jesuh} \textsc{erg} word-teach \textsc{irr} \textsc{refl}-begin \textsc{3sg} \\
\glt \glossquote{From that time Jesus began to preach.} (Matthew 4:17)
\end{exe}

\subsubsection{Boundary with apostrophe
possession}\label{boundary-with-apostrophe-possession}

Apostrophe possession is also treated as boundary material. Forms such
as \tdim{Topa' inn} \glossquote{the Lord's house} belong primarily with
the broader possession discussion and are not reopened here as a full
possession analysis.

\subsubsection{Deferred and boundary
material}\label{deferred-and-boundary-material-6}

Several issues remain outside the present account.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \tdim{ka-nei} and \tdim{ka-inn} support a controlled host-sensitive
  routing contrast, but they do not settle the full person-prefix
  paradigm.
\item
  \tdim{ainn} and the wider \tdim{a-} family remain boundary material
  shared with possession, agreement, and relative-clause structure.
\item
  \tdim{ipai}, \tdim{ko}, and \tdim{ei} remain boundary material shared
  with independent pronoun and clusivity analysis.
\item
  \tdim{hongmu} and \tdim{kongmu} remain boundary material until
  object-prefix and inverse-like selection conditions are treated
  directly.
\item
  \tdim{kipan} remains boundary material shared with reflexive, middle,
  and derivational analysis.
\item
  \tdim{Topa' inn} remains boundary material shared with apostrophe
  possession and possessor syntax.
\item
  Raw occurrence counts are not treated as grammar facts without
  constructional control.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Summary}\label{summary-6}

The safest grammar-facing conclusion is a controlled routing contrast:
verbal-host \tdim{ka-nei} supports agreement routing, and nominal-host
\tdim{ka-inn} supports possessive routing. Boundary forms such as
\tdim{ainn}, \tdim{ipai}, \tdim{hongmu}, \tdim{kongmu}, \tdim{kipan},
\tdim{a bawl mi}, \tdim{ahih}, and \tdim{Topa' inn} remain explicit so
the section stays narrow and constructionally controlled.

\subsection{Hong / kong object-prefix or
inverse-like}\label{hong-kong-object-prefix-or-inverse-like}

The current \tdim{hong} \glossquote{come / venitive} and \tdim{kong}
\glossquote{1→2 / direct-like} evidence is strongest for a narrow
participant-oriented prefix contrast: \tdim{hongbia}
\glossquote{worship me} and \tdim{kongpia} \glossquote{give to you} are
the cleanest paired Gospel rows, \tdim{kongkoih} \glossquote{place you}
adds an OT anchor, and \tdim{hongmu} \glossquote{see me} / \tdim{kongmu}
\glossquote{I see you} remain supportive but not diagnostic.

\subsubsection{Overview of hong and kong object-prefix or inverse-like
marking}\label{overview-of-hong-and-kong-object-prefix-or-inverse-like-marking}

\tdim{hong} \glossquote{come / venitive} remains mixed: some rows look
inverse-like, while others are clearly deictic, motion, or formulaic.
\tdim{kong} \glossquote{1→2 / direct-like} is the cleaner side of the
contrast. The section keeps the clearest rows separate from deictic
motion, speech-formula rows, and lexicalized or unclear material.

The safest grammar-facing conclusion is narrow: Tedim has a small
participant-oriented prefix domain, but this is not a full agreement
chapter and not a full inverse-system chapter. The core comparison comes
from a paired Gospel row, supported by a second OT \tdim{kong} anchor
and by cautious \tdim{hongmu} \glossquote{SAP-oriented see} /
\tdim{kongmu} \glossquote{I see you} support rows.

inventory}\label{current-hong-and-kong-inventory}

\subsubsection{Safest hong and kong
rows}\label{safest-hong-and-kong-rows}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:hk-core-matt4}
\gll \tdimword{Kà} \tdimword{mái-àh} \tdimword{kun-ìn} \tdimword{kèi} \tdimword{hòng} \tdimword{bía} \tdimword{lecin} \tdimword{híh-tè} \tdimword{khèmpeuh} \tdimword{nàngmà} \tdimword{tùngàh} \tdimword{kòng} \tdimword{pía} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{hì,} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{cí} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{1sg} face-\textsc{loc} bow-\textsc{erg} \textsc{neg} \textsc{3→1} worship offend \textsc{prox}-\textsc{pl} all \textsc{2sg}.self on-\textsc{loc} \textsc{1sg→3} give \textsc{irr} \textsc{decl} \textsc{3sg} say \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{If you worship me, I will give you all these things.} (Matthew 4:9)
\end{exe}

The cleanest paired Gospel comparison is \tdim{hongbia}
\glossquote{worship me} versus \tdim{kongpia} \glossquote{give to you}
in Matthew 4:9. That same verse makes the contrast visible in one
clause: \tdim{hongbia} is the clearest hong-side diagnostic, and
\tdim{kongpia} is the clearest kong-side diagnostic.

Genesis 41:41 adds \tdim{kongkoih} \glossquote{place you} as a second OT
anchor.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:hk-core-gen41}
\gll \tdimword{Egypt} \tdimword{gàm} \tdimword{khèmpeuh} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{ùk} \tdimword{dìng-ìn} \tdimword{nàng} \tdimword{kòng} \tdimword{kòih} \tdimword{khin-zò} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{egypt} land all \textsc{3sg} rule \textsc{irr}-\textsc{erg} \textsc{2sg}.\textsc{pro} \textsc{1sg→3} put \textsc{compl}-\textsc{compl} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{I have placed you over all the land of Egypt.} (Genesis 41:41)
\end{exe}

Together, these rows are enough to support a narrow participant-oriented
prefix pocket without claiming a full system.

\subsubsection{Support but not diagnostic
rows}\label{support-but-not-diagnostic-rows}

The familiar \tdim{hongmu} \glossquote{see you (SAP-oriented)} and
\tdim{kongmu} \glossquote{I see you} rows remain useful but not
decisive. They show that participant orientation is real, but they do
not by themselves settle whether every \tdim{hong} token is
object-prefixal rather than venitive/deictic.

\subparagraph{Matthew 25:37 support row}\label{matthew-2537-support-row}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:hk-support-matt25}
\reviewwarninginline{[REVIEW PREVIEW WARNING: ex:hk-support-matt25: Bible reference mapped, but slice example is not a direct verse span; analyzed slice wording]}
\gll \tdimword{Topa} \tdimword{àw,} \tdimword{cí-kin} \tdimword{nà} \tdimword{gil-kial} \tdimword{hòng} \tdimword{mú-ìn} \tdimword{àn} \tdimword{hòng} \tdimword{pía} \tdimword{kà} \tdimword{hì} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{hìam?} \\
     Lord voice say-half \textsc{2sg} bowels \textsc{3→1} see-\textsc{erg} \textsc{3pl}.\textsc{poss} \textsc{3→1} give \textsc{1sg} \textsc{decl} 2/3PL \textsc{q} \\
\glt \glossquote{Lord, when did we see you hungry and give you food?} (Matthew 25:37)
\end{exe}

In this quoted clause, the speaker group is first-person plural and the
addressee is second person. The clause-level participant configuration
is therefore 1PL→2SG, but the two \tdim{hong} tokens are better treated
here as SAP-oriented/venitive support rather than as clean person-prefix
diagnostics. Both tokens are glossed the same way.

\subparagraph{Jeremiah 7:11 support row}\label{jeremiah-711-support-row}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:hk-support-jer7}
\gll \tdimword{Kèimah} \tdimword{ìn} \tdimword{nòtè'} \tdimword{híh} \tdimword{khèmpeuh} \tdimword{kòng} \tdimword{mú} \tdimword{khín} \tdimword{hì,} \tdimword{Topa} \tdimword{ìn} \tdimword{kòng} \tdimword{cí} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{1sg}.\textsc{pro} \textsc{erg} \textsc{2pl}.\textsc{pro}.\textsc{poss} \textsc{prox} all \textsc{1sg→3} see.\textsc{i} \textsc{imm} \textsc{decl} Lord \textsc{erg} \textsc{1sg→3} say \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{I have seen all these things, says the Lord.} (Jeremiah 7:11)
\end{exe}

Matthew 8:22 (\tdim{Nangmah in kei hong zui in}) remains a useful
supporting row for inverse-like \tdim{hong} with explicit \tdim{nangmah}
and \tdim{kei}, but imperative venitive overlap keeps it below the
promoted diagnostic tier.

\subsubsection{Boundary with deictic / venitive
hong}\label{boundary-with-deictic-venitive-hong}

Motion-heavy rows such as \tdim{hongpai} and \tdim{hongbei} stay
boundary material and should not be promoted as object-prefix
diagnostics. Overlap rows such as \tdim{hongsawl} also remain on this
boundary side.

\subsubsection{Boundary with lexicalized or unclear
rows}\label{boundary-with-lexicalized-or-unclear-rows}

Formulaic, nominalized, and speech-formula rows stay boundary-controlled
rather than being promoted as object-prefix diagnostics.

\subsubsection{Boundary with transitivity, valency, and
pronoun/agreement
overlap}\label{boundary-with-transitivity-valency-and-pronounagreement-overlap}

Rows that mainly expose clause type, verb class, or person-marking
overlap stay boundary-controlled. This slice does not widen into a full
agreement chapter or a full inverse chapter.

\subsubsection{Deferred material}\label{deferred-material-5}

The literature-backed inverse claims remain visible in the background
sources, but this slice keeps the domain narrow and leaves the broader
system for later review.

\subsubsection{Summary}\label{summary-7}

The safest grammar-facing conclusion is that Tedim has a narrow
participant-oriented prefix pocket. \tdim{hong} remains mixed between
venitive/deictic and inverse-like uses, while \tdim{kong} is the cleaner
direct-like / 1→2 side. Keep the paired diagnostics (\tdim{hongbia},
\tdim{kongpia}) and the OT \tdim{kongkoih} anchor central, treat
\tdim{hongmu}, \tdim{kongmu}, and \tdim{hongzui} as supporting, and
leave deictic, lexicalized, and speech-formula rows boundary-only.

Raw report counts do not decide the analysis.

\subsection{Transitivity}\label{transitivity}

The current transitivity evidence is strongest for simple intransitive
anchors such as \tdim{sih} \glossquote{die} and \tdim{suak}
\glossquote{become}, and for transitive anchors such as \tdim{hawl}
\glossquote{seek / steer} and \tdim{en} \glossquote{look at / see},
while stem families, derivational predicates, and case- or prefix-heavy
rows remain boundary material.

\subsubsection{Overview of transitivity
contrasts}\label{overview-of-transitivity-contrasts}

This section treats a controlled part of Tedim transitivity: simple
intransitive and transitive predicate anchors plus selected boundary
material. The clearest present anchors are \tdim{sih} \glossquote{die},
\tdim{suak} \glossquote{become}, \tdim{hawl} \glossquote{seek / steer},
and \tdim{en} \glossquote{look at / see}, while stem families such as
\tdim{mu / muh} \glossquote{see}, derivation-heavy forms such as
\tdim{piangsak} \glossquote{cause to be born / create}, and case- or
prefix-heavy rows remain explicit boundaries.

That is enough for a narrow grammar-facing claim. It does not yet
establish a full verb-class inventory or a complete argument-structure
chapter.

\subsubsection{Current transitivity
inventory}\label{current-transitivity-inventory}
