Example context
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Current grammar-facing status
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Boundary issue
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\tdim{sih} & simple intransitive predicate & \tdim{Haran si hi},
\tdim{a numei zong a si hi} & supported & cleanest death-predicate
anchor \\
\tdim{suak} & change-of-state intransitive predicate &
\tdim{a hau mahmah khat a suak hi}, \tdim{tua gam a sia suak ding hi} &
supported with caution & broader lexical range remains open \\
\tdim{hawl} & transitive predicate with a direct object &
\tdim{a leeng a hawl uh hi} & supported with caution & printable example
range is narrower than the report gloss \\
\tdim{en} & perception verb with a transitive profile &
\tdim{a bawlsate khempeuh en a}, \tdim{Jesuh a en uh hi} & supported &
clearer OT/Gospel support than \tdim{hawl} \\
\tdim{mu / muh} & perception family at the stem-alternation boundary &
\tdim{Topa' maipha mu hi}, \tdim{aksi a muh uh ciangin} & boundary
material & overlaps Form I / Form II alternation \\
\tdim{za / zak}, \tdim{nei / neih}, \tdim{ngai / ngaih} & stem-family or
labile-looking material & report-level verb-family rows & boundary
material & belongs with stem alternation and labile behavior \\
\tdim{piangsak}, \tdim{pia(k)sak} & derivation-heavy transitivity &
\tdim{piangsak}, \tdim{pia(k)sak} & boundary material & overlaps
derivation / valency \\
\tdim{pia}, \tdim{gen}, \tdim{tom} & argument-structure-sensitive
predicates & ditransitive or comitative rows & boundary material &
depends strongly on case and complement structure \\
\tdim{hong}, \tdim{ki-} & prefix-heavy or voice-like material &
intransitive and prefixal report rows & boundary material & overlaps
prefix/agreement and voice-like analysis \\
\end{longtable}
}

The table keeps the first contrast compact. A grammar-facing section
should show the clearest predicate types now, while leaving the larger
problems of stem alternation, derivation, case, and prefixal structure
visible as boundaries rather than flattening them into one verb-class
system.

\subsubsection{Intransitive predicates}\label{intransitive-predicates}

The cleanest intransitive anchor is \tdim{sih} \glossquote{die}. It
supports a simple one-participant predicate without immediately forcing
broader case, derivational, or prefixal analysis into the section.
\tdim{Suak} \glossquote{become} adds a second intransitive line and
shows that the section is not limited to death predicates alone.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:trans-sih-gen11}
\gll \tdimword{Haran} \tdimword{sí} \tdimword{hì} \\
     \textsc{haran} die \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{Haran died} (Genesis 11:28)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:trans-sih-matt22}
\gll \tdimword{à} \tdimword{nu-méi} \tdimword{zòng} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{sí} \tdimword{hì} \\
     \textsc{3sg} woman also \textsc{3sg} die \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{the woman also died} (Matthew 22:27)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:trans-suak-gen26}
\gll \tdimword{à} \tdimword{hau} \tdimword{màhmàh} \tdimword{khàt} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{sùak} \tdimword{hì} \\
     \textsc{3sg} rich very one \textsc{3sg} wither \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{he became very rich} (Genesis 26:13)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:trans-suak-matt12}
\gll \tdimword{túa} \tdimword{gàm} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{sía} \tdimword{sùak} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{hì} \\
     \textsc{dist} land \textsc{3sg} evil wither \textsc{irr} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{that kingdom will become ruined} (Matthew 12:25)
\end{exe}

These examples support a narrow intransitive side: \tdim{sih} gives a
plain intransitive event predicate, while \tdim{suak} gives a
change-of-state predicate. They do not by themselves define the whole
intransitive inventory, but they are strong enough for a controlled
first contrast.

\subsubsection{Transitive predicates}\label{transitive-predicates}

The transitive side stays equally narrow. Among the report-backed simple
rows, \tdim{hawl} \glossquote{seek / steer} remains the least
theory-heavy anchor, but its currently printable evidence is not as
balanced as the rest of the section. \tdim{En}
\glossquote{look at / see} therefore serves as the clearer supporting
transitive row with matched Old Testament and Gospel examples.

No equally clean Gospel example is currently used for this exact
\tdim{hawl} row, so the section keeps a single Old Testament example
here and lets \tdim{en} \glossquote{look at / see} carry the balanced
transitive support.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:trans-hawl-1chr13}
\gll \tdimword{Uzza} \tdimword{lè} \tdimword{Ahio} \tdimword{ìn} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{leeng} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{hawl} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{hì} \\
     \textsc{uzza} and \textsc{ahio} \textsc{erg} \textsc{3sg} chariot \textsc{3sg} seek 2/3PL \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{Uzza and Ahio drove the cart} (1 Chronicles 13:7)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:trans-en-gen1}
\gll \tdimword{Pasian} \tdimword{ìn} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{bāwl-sá-tè} \tdimword{khèmpeuh} \tdimword{ēn} \tdimword{à} \\
     God \textsc{erg} \textsc{3sg} make-flesh-\textsc{pl} all look \textsc{3sg} \\
\glt \glossquote{God looked at all that he had made} (Genesis 1:31)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:trans-en-matt27}
\gll \tdimword{Jesuh} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{ēn} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{hì} \\
     \textsc{jesuh} \textsc{3sg} look 2/3PL \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{they watched Jesus} (Matthew 27:36)
\end{exe}

Taken together, these rows support a practical transitive side without
claiming that all Tedim transitives behave alike. \tdim{Hawl} remains
useful because it keeps a simple object-taking predicate in view, while
\tdim{en} supplies the better balanced formal support.

\subsubsection{The narrow intransitive / transitive
contrast}\label{the-narrow-intransitive-transitive-contrast}

The practical contrast is therefore modest. \tdim{Sih} \glossquote{die}
and \tdim{suak} \glossquote{become} support the intransitive side, while
\tdim{hawl} \glossquote{seek / steer} and \tdim{en}
\glossquote{look at / see} support the transitive side.

That is enough for a first grammar-facing contrast, but it is not yet a
complete verb-class system. The section distinguishes a small set of
stable anchors rather than claiming that every Tedim predicate can
already be assigned to a single settled transitivity class.

\subsubsection{Boundary with stem alternation and labile
behavior}\label{boundary-with-stem-alternation-and-labile-behavior}

The clearest boundary beyond the core anchors is \tdim{mu / muh}
\glossquote{see}. This family matters because it shows that transitivity
quickly meets the Form I / Form II system, and it therefore cannot be
treated as ordinary class evidence without reopening the
stem-alternation chapter. The same caution applies to \tdim{za / zak}
\glossquote{hear / listen}, \tdim{nei / neih} \glossquote{have}, and
\tdim{ngai / ngaih} \glossquote{need / love / listen}.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:trans-mu-gen6}
\gll \tdimword{Noah} \tdimword{ìn} \tdimword{Topa'} \tdimword{mái-phà} \tdimword{mú} \tdimword{hì} \\
     \textsc{noah} \textsc{erg} Lord.\textsc{poss} face-good see.\textsc{i} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{Noah found favor with the Lord} (Genesis 6:8)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:trans-muh-matt2}
\gll \tdimword{Túa} \tdimword{aksi} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{mùh} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{cìang-ìn} \\
     \textsc{dist} star \textsc{3sg} see.\textsc{ii} 2/3PL then-\textsc{erg} \\
\glt \glossquote{when they saw the star} (Matthew 2:10)
\end{exe}

This pair is useful precisely because it stops short of a class claim.
It shows why transitivity and stem alternation must be discussed
together at the boundary, while still leaving the larger alternation
system outside the present section.

\subsubsection{Boundary with derivation /
valency}\label{boundary-with-derivation-valency}

The derivational boundary is represented most clearly by \tdim{piangsak}
\glossquote{cause to be born / create} and \tdim{pia(k)sak}
\glossquote{give-CAUS / cause to give}. These forms are important, but
they are poor core anchors for a first transitivity section because
their argument profile cannot be separated cleanly from the causative
and valency-changing uses of \tdim{-sak}
\glossquote{causative / benefactive}.

That is why the present section keeps them visible only as adjacent
evidence. They belong to transitivity, but they belong just as directly
to the derivation / valency domain.

\subsubsection{Boundary with case marking and argument
structure}\label{boundary-with-case-marking-and-argument-structure}

Predicates such as \tdim{pia} \glossquote{give}, \tdim{gen}
\glossquote{say}, and \tdim{tom} \glossquote{meet / accompany} are
likewise better treated as boundary material than as first-line
transitivity anchors. Their interpretation depends heavily on recipient,
complement, or comitative structure, so they quickly become questions
about case marking and broader argument structure rather than about a
simple intransitive/transitive contrast.

For the same reason, this section does not try to turn ditransitive and
complement-taking predicates into a full argument-structure chapter. It
keeps them visible, but not central.

\subsubsection{Boundary with prefix/agreement and voice-like
material}\label{boundary-with-prefixagreement-and-voice-like-material}

Some report rows also overlap with prefixal analysis. \tdim{Hong}
\glossquote{come / venitive} remains mixed between lexical motion and
prefix-heavy verbal structure, while \tdim{ki-}
\glossquote{reflexive / middle / passive-like} belongs with the broader
voice-like boundary rather than with a simple predicate-class list.

The section therefore leaves prefix/agreement-heavy material to its own
domain. A narrow transitivity section should not absorb pronominal
routing, venitive morphology, or reflexive and middle-like structure
just because those issues touch verbal argument patterns.

\subsubsection{Deferred and boundary
material}\label{deferred-and-boundary-material-7}

Several issues remain outside the present account.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \tdim{hawl} \glossquote{seek / steer} remains useful, but the
  currently printable \tdim{hawl} evidence is not as Gospel-balanced as
  the rest of the section.
\item
  \tdim{mu / muh} \glossquote{see}, \tdim{za / zak}
  \glossquote{hear / listen}, \tdim{nei / neih} \glossquote{have}, and
  \tdim{ngai / ngaih} \glossquote{need / love / listen} remain
  stem-family and labile boundary material rather than core anchors.
\item
  \tdim{piangsak} \glossquote{cause to be born / create} and
  \tdim{pia(k)sak} \glossquote{give-CAUS / cause to give} remain
  derivation-heavy predicates.
\item
  \tdim{pia} \glossquote{give}, \tdim{gen} \glossquote{say}, and
  \tdim{tom} \glossquote{meet / accompany} remain dominated by case and
  complement structure.
\item
  \tdim{hong} \glossquote{come / venitive} and \tdim{ki-}
  \glossquote{reflexive / middle / passive-like} remain prefix-heavy or
  voice-like boundary material.
\item
  The section therefore stays smaller than a full verb-class chapter or
  a full argument-structure account.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Summary}\label{summary-8}

The safest current conclusion is that Tedim supports a narrow
intransitive / transitive contrast that can already be stated in
grammar-facing prose. \tdim{Sih} \glossquote{die} and \tdim{suak}
\glossquote{become} anchor the intransitive side, while \tdim{hawl}
\glossquote{seek / steer} and \tdim{en} \glossquote{look at / see}
anchor the transitive side. At the same time, stem alternation,
derivation / valency, case-sensitive predicates, and prefix-heavy
material remain visible as boundaries, so the section stays controlled
rather than expanding into a full verb-class chapter.

\subsection{VP structure / suffix
stacking}\label{vp-structure-suffix-stacking}

The current VP-structure evidence is strongest for a small checked set
of suffix stacks such as \tdim{bawlzoding} \glossquote{make-COMPL-IRR},
directional-plus-irrealis sequences, modal-plus-irrealis strings, and
derivational stacks whose ordering can be discussed without forcing a
full verb-template chapter.

\subsubsection{Overview of VP structure and suffix
stacking}\label{overview-of-vp-structure-and-suffix-stacking}

The safest current claim is that Tedim allows a small but real set of
multi-suffix verbal complexes and clause-internal stacking patterns,
without requiring a full verb-template chapter. The clearest checked
anchors are \tdim{bawlzoding} \glossquote{make-COMPL-IRR}, attested
\tdim{taisakzo ding} \glossquote{put to flight / flee-CAUS-COMPL-IRR},
directional material such as \tdim{a kilaktoh ding hun}
\glossquote{the time for him to be taken up}, modal strings such as
\tdim{khiathei ding om lo}
\glossquote{there is no one who can interpret it}, and derivational
stacks such as \tdim{ciahsakkik} \glossquote{send back} and
\tdim{paikhiatsak} \glossquote{cause to go out}.

This section therefore keeps its descriptive scope controlled. It
describes the clearest stack types that can already be stated in
grammar-facing prose, while keeping the boundaries with TAM,
directionals, negation, derivation, and clause linkage explicit.

\subsubsection{Current VP stacking
inventory}\label{current-vp-stacking-inventory}

{\def\LTcaptype{none} % do not increment counter
\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Form or pattern
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Rough function
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Example context
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Current grammar-facing status
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Boundary issue
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\tdim{bawlzoding} & completive plus irrealis after a verbal stem &
\tdim{bawlzoding} & supported with caveat & analyzer gloss remains
noisier than the ordering evidence \\
V-\tdim{zo ding} & aspect before irrealis & \tdim{taisakzo ding} &
supported with caveat & derivational material often enters the same
string \\
DIR + \tdim{ding} & directional material before irrealis in a larger
verbal complex & \tdim{a kilaktoh ding hun} & supported with caveat &
quickly overlaps clause structure \\
\tdim{khia-ta} & directional plus completive boundary & \tdim{khia-ta} &
boundary material & TAM and directional analysis remain intertwined \\
\tdim{khiathei ding om lo} & ability plus irrealis plus negation &
\tdim{mang a khiathei ka hi kei hi}, \tdim{lutthei ding uh hi} &
supported with caveat & already reaches clause-sized modal-negation
material \\
\tdim{ciahsakkik} & causative plus iterative or return morphology &
\tdim{hong ciahsakkik un}, \tdim{a guak ciahsakkik uh hi} & supported
with caveat & derivational \tdim{-sak} remains central \\
\tdim{paikhiatsak} & directional plus causative morphology &
\tdim{hong paikhiatsak ciangin} & boundary material & sits between
directionals and derivation \\
\tdim{dingin} & clause-bound irrealis or purposive linker &
\tdim{a khen dingin}, \tdim{maksim dingin} & boundary material & better
treated with clause linkage than with a compact VP inventory \\
\end{longtable}
}

The table keeps \tdim{khia-ta} \glossquote{out-PFV} and \tdim{dingin}
\glossquote{for... to / clause-bound irrealis} visible as boundary rows
rather than as core stacking anchors. That matters because a
grammar-facing section should show where VP-structure evidence stops
being simple, not only where it begins.

\subsubsection{Aspect plus irrealis
stacking}\label{aspect-plus-irrealis-stacking}

\tdim{Bawlzoding} \glossquote{make-COMPL-IRR} is the central checked
anchor because it keeps the ordering question in view without forcing a
full paradigm. The best attested formal Old Testament example for this
order is \tdim{taisakzo ding}
\glossquote{put to flight / flee-CAUS-COMPL-IRR}, where completive
\tdim{-zo} \glossquote{already / completive} appears before \tdim{ding}
\glossquote{prospective / irrealis}. No equally clean Gospel example is
currently used for this exact construction, so the section keeps the
compact Old Testament anchor and lets the later subsections show how the
same right-edge order continues into Gospel material.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:vp-taisakzo-ding-deut32}
\gll \tdimword{tái-sakzo} \tdimword{dìng} \\
     bright-\textsc{caus}.\textsc{compl} \textsc{irr} \\
\glt \glossquote{put ten thousand to flight} (Deuteronomy 32:30)
\end{exe}

This row does not settle every detail of the middle suffixes, and it
does not replace the checked \tdim{bawlzoding} anchor. It is useful
because it shows the same broad order in attested text: verbal stem
material, then completive material, then irrealis.

\subsubsection{Directional and TAM boundary in the verbal
complex}\label{directional-and-tam-boundary-in-the-verbal-complex}

Directional material can stand inside larger verbal complexes, but the
present evidence does not collapse directionals and TAM into one solved
suffix template. Genesis 20:13 gives \tdim{paikhiatsak}
\glossquote{cause to go out}, where \tdim{-khia} \glossquote{outward}
remains visible inside a causative stack. Luke 9:51 gives
\tdim{a kilaktoh ding hun} \glossquote{the time for him to be taken up},
where upward \tdim{-toh} \glossquote{upward} stands before \tdim{ding}
\glossquote{irrealis / prospective}. The boundary clue \tdim{khia-ta}
\glossquote{out-PFV} belongs in the same zone, but it remains safer here
as prose evidence than as a core formal anchor.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:vp-paikhiatsak-gen20}
\gll \tdimword{hòng} \tdimword{pái-khìa-sàk} \tdimword{cìang-ìn} \\
     \textsc{3→1} go-out-\textsc{caus} then-\textsc{erg} \\
\glt \glossquote{when he caused me to go out} (Genesis 20:13)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:vp-kilaktoh-ding-luke9}
\gll \tdimword{à} \tdimword{kī-làk-tòh} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{hùn} \\
     \textsc{3sg} \textsc{refl}-take-\textsc{up} \textsc{irr} time \\
\glt \glossquote{the time for him to be taken up} (Luke 9:51)
\end{exe}

These examples are not identical constructions, but together they show
why VP structure has to remain boundary-aware: directional morphology
can appear inside larger complexes, yet the surrounding ordering is
still sensitive to TAM, derivation, and clause structure.

\subsubsection{Ability, irrealis, and negation at the VP
boundary}\label{ability-irrealis-and-negation-at-the-vp-boundary}

Ability material frequently participates in longer strings rather than
standing alone. Genesis 41:16 uses \tdim{khiathei}
\glossquote{interpret-ABIL} under negation, while Matthew 7:21 uses
\tdim{lutthei ding} \glossquote{enter-ABIL-IRR} in a projected event.
The stronger clause-sized boundary row \tdim{khiathei ding om lo}
\glossquote{there is no one who can interpret it} shows why the section
must stop short of a full account of negative modal syntax.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:vp-khiathei-gen41}
\gll \tdimword{máng} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{khìat-thèi} \tdimword{kà} \tdimword{hì} \tdimword{kèi} \tdimword{hì} \\
     dream \textsc{3sg} interpret-\textsc{abil} \textsc{1sg} \textsc{decl} \textsc{neg} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{I cannot interpret a dream} (Genesis 41:16)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:vp-lutthei-ding-matt7}
\gll \tdimword{lút-thèi} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{hì} \\
     enter-\textsc{abil} \textsc{irr} 2/3PL \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{they will be able to enter} (Matthew 7:21)
\end{exe}

This pair shows a stable local point: \tdim{-thei} can be followed by
further material such as negation or \tdim{-ding}, so ability belongs
inside VP-structure discussion. It also shows why the present section
should not be widened into a full modal chapter.

\subsubsection{Derivational stacking and valency
overlap}\label{derivational-stacking-and-valency-overlap}

Derivational stacking is clearly real, but it is not reducible to suffix
order alone. \tdim{Ciahsakkik} \glossquote{send back} provides the
cleanest matched pair now available, with an Old Testament row and a
Gospel row. The same area also keeps \tdim{bawlsakthei}
\glossquote{make-CAUS-ABIL} and \tdim{paikhiatsak}
\glossquote{go-out-CAUS} visible, which is why derivation and valency
remain unavoidable boundaries for the present section.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:vp-ciahsakkik-gen24}
\gll \tdimword{hòng} \tdimword{ciah-sàk-kík} \tdimword{ùn} \\
     \textsc{3→1} return-\textsc{caus}-\textsc{iter} \textsc{imp}.\textsc{pl} \\
\glt \glossquote{send me back} (Genesis 24:54)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:vp-ciahsakkik-mark12}
\gll \tdimword{à} \tdimword{guak} \tdimword{ciah-sàk-kík} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{hì} \\
     \textsc{3sg} back return-\textsc{caus}-\textsc{iter} 2/3PL \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{they sent him back empty} (Mark 12:3)
\end{exe}

Because \tdim{-sak} is central in both examples, this subsection is best
read as a boundary zone between VP structure and derivation / valency.
The examples are strong enough to show real stacking, but they do not by
themselves justify a full derivational template.

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{Clause-linking boundary with
\tdim{dingin}}{Clause-linking boundary with }}\label{clause-linking-boundary-with}

\tdim{Dingin} \glossquote{for... to / clause-bound irrealis} is
important because it shows how quickly a stacking-looking form becomes
clause linkage. Genesis 1:14 uses \tdim{a khen dingin}
\glossquote{for dividing}, and Matthew 1:19 uses \tdim{maksim dingin}
\glossquote{to dismiss quietly}. Those rows are helpful because they
keep right-edge irrealis material visible, but they are safer as
clause-linking evidence than as core VP suffixes.

This is also why the present section does not treat every
\tdim{ding}-plus-following-material sequence as the same construction.
Some rows belong to local VP stacking, while others already belong to
purposive or clause-linking structure.

\subsubsection{Deferred and boundary
material}\label{deferred-and-boundary-material-8}

Several issues remain outside the present account.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \tdim{bawlzoding} \glossquote{make-COMPL-IRR} remains the central
  checked anchor, but its exact internal semantics are still noisier
  than its ordering value.
\item
  \tdim{khia-ta} \glossquote{out-PFV} remains a TAM-directional boundary
  row rather than a core formal example here.
\item
  \tdim{khiathei ding om lo}
  \glossquote{there is no one who can interpret it} and related strings
  reach clause-sized modal-negation structure too quickly.
\item
  \tdim{bawlsakthei} \glossquote{make-CAUS-ABIL} and \tdim{paikhiatsak}
  \glossquote{go-out-CAUS} show why derivational stacks need separate
  treatment.
\item
  \tdim{dingin} \glossquote{for... to / clause-bound irrealis} is kept
  at the clause-linkage boundary rather than inside a full VP template.
\item
  The section does not attempt a full suffix slot template, a full
  derivation chapter, or a full account of sentence-final material.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Summary}\label{summary-9}

The safest grammar-facing conclusion is that Tedim permits a small but
real set of multi-suffix verbal complexes. \tdim{Bawlzoding}
\glossquote{make-COMPL-IRR} remains the central checked anchor, while
\tdim{taisakzo ding}, \tdim{lutthei ding}, \tdim{ciahsakkik}, and
\tdim{a kilaktoh ding hun} show how aspect, modality, directionals, and
derivation can stack in attested material. At the same time, the
boundaries with TAM, directionals, negation, derivation / valency, and
clause linkage remain visible, so the section stays controlled rather
than turning into a full verbal template chapter.

\subsection{TAM / aspect / modal}\label{tam-aspect-modal}

The current TAM evidence is strongest for a small checked set of
aspectual and modal anchors such as \tdim{-ta}
\glossquote{completive / change-of-state}, \tdim{-zo}
\glossquote{already / completive}, \tdim{-gige}
\glossquote{habitually / always}, \tdim{-ding}
\glossquote{prospective / irrealis}, \tdim{-thei}
\glossquote{can / be able}, and \tdim{-kik} \glossquote{again / back}.

\subsubsection{Overview of TAM / aspect / modal
marking}\label{overview-of-tam-aspect-modal-marking}

The safest current claim is that Tedim shows a controlled set of
aspectual and modal anchors that can already be described in
grammar-facing prose, without pretending that the whole verbal paradigm
is settled. The present section therefore stays with compact suffixal or
particle-like material rather than a full account of clause type,
sentence-final particles, negation, or verb-phrase stacking.

The clearest anchors in this section are \tdim{-ta}
\glossquote{completive / change-of-state}, \tdim{-zo}
\glossquote{already / completive}, \tdim{-gige}
\glossquote{habitually / always}, \tdim{-zel}
\glossquote{continuative / keep doing}, \tdim{-ding}
\glossquote{prospective / irrealis}, \tdim{-thei}
\glossquote{can / be able}, \tdim{-kik} \glossquote{again / back}, and
\tdim{-ngei} \glossquote{ever / experiential}. Several wider questions
remain visible around these forms, but they are kept explicit as
boundaries rather than folded into a single large TAM chapter.

\subsubsection{Current TAM inventory}\label{current-tam-inventory}

{\def\LTcaptype{none} % do not increment counter
\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Form or pattern
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Rough function
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Example context
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Current grammar-facing status
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Boundary issue
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\tdim{-ta} & completive or change-of-state aspect & \tdim{kihta uh hi},
\tdim{nungta zaw hi} & supported with caveat & clause-final \tdim{ta hi}
remains separate \\
\tdim{-zo} & completive or endpoint reached & \tdim{ka bawlzo hi},
\tdim{a zuizo uh hi} & supported with caveat & bare \tdim{zo} still
overlaps sentence-final material \\
\tdim{-gige} & habitual or regularly repeated action &
\tdim{en gige hi}, \tdim{vil gige uh a} & well supported & orthography
may separate the habitual element \\
\tdim{-zel} & continuative or repeated continuation &
\tdim{hopih zel hi} & supported with caveat & wider continuative and
lexicalized uses remain open \\
\tdim{-ngei} & experiential or \glossquote{ever} aspect &
\tdim{a paingei bangin} & supporting evidence & many other rows cluster
with negation or \tdim{nawn} \\
\tdim{-ding} & prospective or irrealis marking & \tdim{na si ding hi},
\tdim{kiphuak ding hi} & well supported & clause-bound \tdim{dingin}
remains separate \\
\tdim{-thei} & ability or possibility & \tdim{khiathei ka hi kei hi},
\tdim{lutthei ding uh hi} & supported with caveat & negation and
irrealis often stack with it \\
\tdim{-kik} & repetition or return & \tdim{na ciahkik matengin},
\tdim{a ciahkik uh hi} & well supported & return and motion readings
still need constructional care \\
\end{longtable}
}

The table keeps \tdim{-ngei} \glossquote{ever / experiential} visible
even though its cleanest formal example is narrower than the habitual
and irrealis pairs. That matters because a grammar-facing inventory
should show both the secure anchors and the places where the evidence is
still thinner.

\subsubsection{Perfect, completive, and change-of-state
material}\label{perfect-completive-and-change-of-state-material}

\tdim{-ta} \glossquote{completive / change-of-state} is safest where a
result state or completed change remains attached to a compact verbal
host. \tdim{-zo} \glossquote{already / completive} likewise works best
inside tight verbal material rather than as a clause-final particle. The
section therefore starts from short verbal rows and keeps broader
sentence-final issues for a later boundary discussion.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:tam-kihta-exod1}
\gll \tdimword{kih-tà} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{hì} \\
     abhor-\textsc{pfv} 2/3PL \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{they were afraid} (Exodus 1:12)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:tam-nungta-matt4}
\gll \tdimword{nùng-tà} \tdimword{zàw} \tdimword{hì} \\
     life-\textsc{pfv} more \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{lives rather} (Matthew 4:4)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:tam-bawlzo-exod34}
\gll \tdimword{kà} \tdimword{bāwl-zò} \tdimword{hì} \\
     \textsc{1sg} make-\textsc{compl} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{I have made it} (Exodus 34:27)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:tam-zuizo-matt7}
\gll \tdimword{à} \tdimword{zúi-zò} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{hì} \\
     \textsc{3sg} follow-\textsc{compl} 2/3PL \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{they followed it} (Matthew 7:14)
\end{exe}

Taken together, these rows support a modest aspectual claim: Tedim can
mark completed or result-state readings in compact verbal material. They
do not license every orthographic \tdim{ta} or \tdim{zo} token as
settled TAM evidence, and they do not erase the clause-final boundary
that remains visible elsewhere in the grammar.

\subsubsection{Habitual, continuative, and experiential
aspect}\label{habitual-continuative-and-experiential-aspect}

\tdim{-gige} \glossquote{habitually / always} is the most even habitual
anchor in the current controlled evidence. \tdim{-zel}
\glossquote{continuative / keep doing} is also visible, but its neatest
checked row is still Old Testament-led. \tdim{-ngei}
\glossquote{ever / experiential} belongs in the same zone, although the
clearest compact formal row at present is the Gospel phrase
\tdim{paingei} \glossquote{go-EXP}.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:tam-paingei-luke4}
\gll \tdimword{à} \tdimword{pái-ngèi} \tdimword{bàng-ìn} \\
     \textsc{3sg} go-\textsc{exp} like-\textsc{erg} \\
\glt \glossquote{as was his custom} (Luke 4:16)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:tam-gige-ps33}
\gll \tdimword{ēn} \tdimword{gìgè} \tdimword{hì} \\
     look \textsc{hab} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{keeps watching} (Psalms 33:15)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:tam-gige-luke20}
\gll \tdimword{vil} \tdimword{gìgè} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{à} \\
     watch \textsc{hab} 2/3PL \textsc{3sg} \\
\glt \glossquote{they kept watching} (Luke 20:20)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:tam-zel-exod33}
\gll \tdimword{Topa} \tdimword{ìn} \tdimword{Moses} \tdimword{ho-pìh} \tdimword{zèl} \tdimword{hì} \\
     Lord \textsc{erg} \textsc{moses} greet-\textsc{appl} scatter \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{the Lord kept speaking with Moses} (Exodus 33:11)
\end{exe}

The paired \tdim{-gige} rows show that habitual meaning is not confined
to one testament. The \tdim{-zel} row is included because it keeps
continuative aspect visible inside the same section, while
\tdim{paingei} shows that experiential \tdim{-ngei} is real even though
many other \tdim{ngei} examples sit closer to negated or ``ever again''
environments.

\subsubsection{Prospective and irrealis
marking}\label{prospective-and-irrealis-marking}

\tdim{-ding} \glossquote{prospective / irrealis} is productive enough to
mark future-like or projected events in ordinary clauses. At the same
time, many wider report hits are clause-bound forms such as
\tdim{dingin} \glossquote{for... to / clause-bound irrealis}, so the
section starts with simpler predicates and leaves purposive or
clause-linking material for the boundary note below.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:tam-ding-gen2}
\gll \tdimword{nà} \tdimword{sí} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{hì} \\
     \textsc{2sg} die \textsc{irr} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{you will die} (Genesis 2:17)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:tam-ding-matt1}
\gll \tdimword{Emmanuel} \tdimword{kī-phuak} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{hì} \\
     \textsc{emmanuel} \textsc{refl}-utter \textsc{irr} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{will be called Emmanuel} (Matthew 1:23)
\end{exe}

These examples are enough for a controlled grammar-facing statement that
\tdim{-ding} marks prospective or irrealis meaning in ordinary verbal or
predicate structure. They are not enough to settle the full relation
between \tdim{-ding}, purpose clauses, and broader clause linkage.

\subsubsection{Ability and modal
marking}\label{ability-and-modal-marking}

\tdim{-thei} \glossquote{can / be able} often appears as a close
verb-plus-modal sequence rather than as a fully separate lexical verb.
The Gospel data provide the cleanest clause-level positive anchor, while
Old Testament rows often show the same modal material under negation or
inside quoted discourse.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:tam-thei-gen41}
\gll \tdimword{máng} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{khìat-thèi} \tdimword{kà} \tdimword{hì} \tdimword{kèi} \tdimword{hì} \\
     dream \textsc{3sg} interpret-\textsc{abil} \textsc{1sg} \textsc{decl} \textsc{neg} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{I cannot interpret a dream} (Genesis 41:16)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:tam-thei-matt7}
\gll \tdimword{lút-thèi} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{hì} \\
     enter-\textsc{abil} \textsc{irr} 2/3PL \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{they will be able to enter} (Matthew 7:21)
\end{exe}

The subsection therefore keeps two points in view at once. First,
\tdim{-thei} really does mark ability or possibility. Second, the Old
Testament row already shows how quickly the modal anchor meets negation,
which is why the present section stays narrower than a full modal
chapter.

\subsubsection{Repetition and return
marking}\label{repetition-and-return-marking}

\tdim{-kik} \glossquote{again / back} regularly marks repeated or
return-oriented events. The present section keeps that claim modest: the
suffix is real and productive enough to discuss, but it is not expanded
here into a full motion-verb chapter or a full discourse account of
repetition.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:tam-kik-gen3}
\gll \tdimword{nà} \tdimword{ciah-kík} \tdimword{matengin} \\
     \textsc{2sg} return-\textsc{iter} until \\
\glt \glossquote{until you return} (Genesis 3:19)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:tam-kik-matt2}
\gll \tdimword{à} \tdimword{ciah-kík} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{hì} \\
     \textsc{3sg} return-\textsc{iter} 2/3PL \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{they returned} (Matthew 2:12)
\end{exe}

The Old Testament and Gospel rows line up closely enough to support a
stable grammar-facing statement that \tdim{-kik} belongs in the
repetitive or return-marking zone. Broader motion semantics still have
to be handled construction by construction.

\subsubsection{Boundary with negation and sentence-final
particles}\label{boundary-with-negation-and-sentence-final-particles}

The present section does not reopen the negation or sentence-final
chapters. That boundary matters most around \tdim{-thei}
\glossquote{can / be able}, which often appears in strings such as
\tdim{khiathei ding om lo}
\glossquote{there is no one who can interpret it}, where ability,
irrealis, and negation belong to one clause-sized complex rather than to
a single neat suffix slot.

The same caution applies to \tdim{-ta}
\glossquote{completive / change-of-state} and \tdim{-zo}
\glossquote{already / completive}. Compact verbal rows are safe enough
to discuss here, but clause-final \tdim{ta hi} and bare \tdim{zo} remain
better treated as boundary material with sentence-final particles than
as already-settled TAM categories.

\subsubsection{Boundary with directionals and VP
structure}\label{boundary-with-directionals-and-vp-structure}

Directional and TAM material can stack, but the present section keeps
that interaction explicit rather than solved. The row \tdim{khia-ta}
\glossquote{out-PFV} shows why a tidy aspect label can quickly become a
directional-plus-TAM problem, while \tdim{bawlzoding}
\glossquote{make-COMPL-IRR} and \tdim{bawlsakthei}
\glossquote{make-CAUS-ABIL} show why fuller suffix ordering belongs with
verb-phrase structure rather than with the compact inventory above.

Clause-bound \tdim{dingin}
\glossquote{for... to / clause-bound irrealis} belongs at the same
boundary. It is important evidence for the wider system, but it turns
prospective marking into clause linkage too quickly to serve as the core
anchor for the present section.

\subsubsection{Deferred and boundary
material}\label{deferred-and-boundary-material-9}

Several issues remain outside the present account.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \tdim{pailai} \glossquote{go-midst / prospective form} still overlaps
  lexical \tdim{lai} too heavily for the present section.
\item
  \tdim{dingin} \glossquote{for... to / clause-bound irrealis} and
  similar purpose-like rows remain clause-linkage material.
\item
  \tdim{mangngilh ta hi} \glossquote{has forgotten} keeps clause-final
  \tdim{ta hi} visible without making it the model for completive
  aspect.
\item
  \tdim{khiathei ding om lo}
  \glossquote{there is no one who can interpret it}, \tdim{khia-ta}
  \glossquote{out-PFV}, \tdim{bawlzoding} \glossquote{make-COMPL-IRR},
  and \tdim{bawlsakthei} \glossquote{make-CAUS-ABIL} remain overlap
  material rather than core anchors.
\item
  \tdim{-nawn} \glossquote{again / any longer} and \tdim{-khin}
  \glossquote{already / after completion} are not expanded here.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Summary}\label{summary-10}

The safest grammar-facing conclusion is that Tedim already shows a small
but real TAM, aspect, and modal profile through compact anchors.
\tdim{-ta}, \tdim{-zo}, \tdim{-gige}, \tdim{-zel}, \tdim{-ngei},
\tdim{-ding}, \tdim{-thei}, and \tdim{-kik} can all be discussed now,
but only if their boundaries with sentence-final particles, negation,
directionals, and wider VP structure remain visible. That is why the
section stays controlled: it offers a usable core inventory without
pretending that the full verbal system has already been normalized.

\subsection{Directionals}\label{directionals}

The current directional evidence is strongest for post-verbal forms such
as \tdim{-khia} \glossquote{outward}, \tdim{-khiat} \glossquote{away},
\tdim{-toh} \glossquote{upward}, \tdim{-sawn} \glossquote{toward}, and
\tdim{-suk} \glossquote{downward}, while deictic prefixes remain
separate.

\subsubsection{Overview of directional
expressions}\label{overview-of-directional-expressions}

The safest current claim is that this section treats a cautious subset
of post-verbal directional morphology and directional-looking verbal
derivatives, rather than the whole Tedim deictic system. The clearest
anchors are \tdim{-khia} \glossquote{outward}, \tdim{-khiat}
\glossquote{away}, \tdim{-toh} \glossquote{upward}, \tdim{-sawn}
\glossquote{toward}, and \tdim{-suk} \glossquote{downward}. Forms such
as \tdim{hotkhiatna} \glossquote{salvation} and \tdim{kahtohna}
\glossquote{going up} show that the same morphology also appears inside
nominalized material, while pre-verbal deictic markers such as
\tdim{va-} \glossquote{away} and \tdim{hong-}
\glossquote{toward the deictic center} remain better handled as a
separate subsystem
\citep{henderson1965, zamngaihcing2017, otsuka_kurabe_directional_affixes}.

The section therefore keeps its descriptive scope modest. It focuses on
forms that are transparent enough for grammar-facing prose now, while
leaving broader motion-verb lexicon, deictic prefixing, and full
verb-phrase stacking for later treatment.

\subsubsection{Current directional
inventory}\label{current-directional-inventory}

{\def\LTcaptype{none} % do not increment counter
\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Form or pattern
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Rough function
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Example context
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Current grammar-facing status
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Boundary issue
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\tdim{-khia} & outward or outward-result direction & \tdim{pokhia} &
well supported & should not be generalized from every orthographic
\tdim{khia} sequence \\
\tdim{-khiat} & away or out-of-domain direction & \tdim{nawhkhiat} &
supported with caveat & analyzer-label caution remains relevant \\
\tdim{-khiat-na} & nominalized away morphology & \tdim{hotkhiatna} &
boundary material & not equivalent to a simple finite directional
verb \\
\tdim{-toh} & upward direction & \tdim{kilaktoh},
\tdim{a kilaktoh ding hun} & supported with caveat & blocked
\tdim{paitoh} overlap prevents a blanket UP reading \\
\tdim{-toh-na} & nominalized upward morphology & \tdim{kahtohna} &
boundary material & nominalization keeps the directional segment visible
without yielding a simple finite predicate \\
\tdim{-sawn} & toward or toward-directed transfer & \tdim{piasawn},
\tdim{nausawn} & cautious support & easy hits drift into kinship-heavy
or lexicalized domains \\
\tdim{-suk} & downward direction & \tdim{paisuk} & supported with caveat
& still represented by a narrow checked set \\
\tdim{-lam} & sideward or direction-side material & \tdim{tawplam} &
boundary material & too noun-like for a clean verbal directional
analysis \\
\tdim{paitoh} pattern & blocked overlap control & \tdim{paitoh} &
excluded as a directional anchor & lexicalized accompany/comitative
reading blocks a simple upward analysis \\
\end{longtable}
}

The table also keeps \tdim{-lam} \glossquote{sideward / toward a side}
and \tdim{paitoh} \glossquote{go-accompany} visible as boundary material
rather than as core anchors. That boundary work matters because a
grammar-facing section should show where directional evidence stops
being clean, not only where it starts.

\subsubsection{Outward and away
direction}\label{outward-and-away-direction}

The clearest outward and away examples stay conservative. \tdim{Pokhia}
\glossquote{grow out} shows outward orientation, while \tdim{nawhkhiat}
\glossquote{drive out / hurry away} keeps away-directed \tdim{-khiat}
visible in a compact verbal form. No equally clean Gospel example is
currently used for this construction, so the section keeps the compact
Old Testament anchors.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:dir-pokhia}
\gll \tdimword{po-khìa} \\
     grow-out \\
\glt \glossquote{grew out} (Genesis 2:5)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:dir-nawhkhiat}
\gll \tdimword{nawh-khìat} \\
     hurry-away \\
\glt \glossquote{drove them out} (Deuteronomy 9:4)
\end{exe}

The same away morphology also surfaces in \tdim{hotkhiatna}
\glossquote{salvation}, but that form is nominalized and therefore
belongs at the boundary between directional morphology and deverbal
nominalization rather than as a second finite anchor.

\subsubsection{Upward direction and directionals in the verb
phrase}\label{upward-direction-and-directionals-in-the-verb-phrase}

Current evidence is strongest for \tdim{-toh} \glossquote{upward}.
Numbers 9:17 gives the clearest compact Old Testament anchor.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:dir-kilaktoh-num9}
\gll \tdimword{kī-làk-tòh} \\
     \textsc{refl}-take-\textsc{up} \\
\glt \glossquote{was taken up} (Numbers 9:17)
\end{exe}

Luke 9:51 then shows the same upward material inside a larger verbal
sequence:

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:dir-kilaktoh-luke9}
\gll \tdimword{à} \tdimword{kī-làk-tòh} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{hùn} \\
     \textsc{3sg} \textsc{refl}-take-\textsc{up} \textsc{irr} time \\
\glt \glossquote{the time for him to be taken up} (Luke 9:51)
\end{exe}

The Gospel row matters for more than source balance. It shows
directional material after the verbal core and before \tdim{ding}
\glossquote{irrealis / prospective}, which supports a modest verb-phrase
claim without turning this section into a full stacking analysis. At the
same time, \tdim{paitoh} \glossquote{go-accompany} remains the necessary
blocked control, and \tdim{kahtohna} \glossquote{going up} keeps upward
morphology visible in nominalized form rather than in a simple finite
predicate.

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{Toward direction with
\tdim{-sawn}}{Toward direction with }}\label{toward-direction-with}

\tdim{Piasawn} \glossquote{give toward} is the clearest checked Old
Testament row.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:dir-piasawn}
\gll \tdimword{pía-sàwn} \\
     give-toward \\
\glt \glossquote{extend to us} (Ezra 9:9)
\end{exe}

Luke 20:31 provides a Gospel comparandum in \tdim{nausawn}
\glossquote{elder-sibling-toward}:

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:dir-nausawn}
\gll \tdimword{nà-u-sàwn} \\
     \textsc{2sg}-elder.sibling-toward \\
\glt \glossquote{took her in turn as an elder brother} (Luke 20:31)
\end{exe}

The Luke example is still kinship-heavy, so it remains a supporting
comparandum rather than the main anchor. Even so, it confirms that
toward-oriented \tdim{-sawn} material belongs in the present section,
while wider kinship and lexicalization issues still require caution.

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{Downward direction with
\tdim{-suk}}{Downward direction with }}\label{downward-direction-with}

\tdim{Paisuk} \glossquote{go down} gives the clearest checked downward
row. No equally clean Gospel example is currently used for this
construction, so the section keeps the compact Old Testament anchor.
This construction is rare in the controlled evidence, so one example is
used here and broader source balancing remains outside the present
account.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:dir-paisuk}
\gll \tdimword{pái-sùk} \\
     go-\textsc{down} \\
\glt \glossquote{came down} (Genesis 11:5)
\end{exe}

The row is compact, analyzable, and grammatically useful. It shows that
downward \tdim{-suk} is part of the directional profile, but it does not
justify a larger claim about every downward-looking verb in the
language.

\subsubsection{Deictic boundary}\label{deictic-boundary}

Several directional meanings in Tedim are better described from the
deictic center than from post-verbal suffixes alone. The literature
discusses pre-verbal \tdim{va-} \glossquote{away} and \tdim{hong-} or
\tdim{ong-} \glossquote{toward the speaker / venitive} as a separate
subsystem
\citep{henderson1965, zamngaihcing2017, otsuka_kurabe_directional_affixes}.
The present section does not merge those markers with the post-verbal
forms above, because the checked examples here are strongest for
post-verbal or post-stem material rather than for a full deictic
paradigm.

This also keeps the present section distinct from the demonstrative
system. Directional perspective is clearly relevant, but the forms
treated here are verbal rather than demonstrative.

\subsubsection{TAM and VP-structure
boundary}\label{tam-and-vp-structure-boundary}

The present section also stops short of a full account of TAM or
serial-like verbal sequencing. The upward Gospel row
\tdim{a kilaktoh ding hun} \glossquote{the time for him to be taken up}
shows that a directional element can stand before \tdim{ding}
\glossquote{irrealis / prospective}, but that single pattern is not
enough to settle the full order of aspect, direction, TAM, and clause
linkage.

Likewise, noun-like forms such as \tdim{hotkhiatna}
\glossquote{salvation} and \tdim{kahtohna} \glossquote{going up} show
why some directional-looking material belongs with nominalization or
broader verb-phrase structure rather than with a simple inventory of
finite directional suffixes.

\subsubsection{Deferred and boundary
material}\label{deferred-and-boundary-material-10}

Several issues remain outside the present account.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \tdim{tawplam} \glossquote{toward the side / at the edge} keeps
  \tdim{-lam} visible, but the form is still too noun-like for a clean
  verbal directional claim.
\item
  \tdim{paitoh} \glossquote{go-accompany} remains a blocked overlap row
  rather than upward evidence.
\item
  \tdim{uilut}, \tdim{paiphei}, \tdim{cip}, and \tdim{tang} are still
  too noisy or too weakly supported for the present account.
\item
  The section does not attempt a full lexicon of motion verbs, a full
  deictic paradigm, or a full suffix-order analysis.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Summary}\label{summary-11}

The safest grammar-facing conclusion is that Tedim directionals are
currently best described through a small checked set of post-verbal
forms. \tdim{-khia} \glossquote{outward}, \tdim{-khiat}
\glossquote{away}, \tdim{-toh} \glossquote{upward}, \tdim{-sawn}
\glossquote{toward}, and \tdim{-suk} \glossquote{downward} are all
visible, but only \tdim{-toh} and \tdim{-sawn} currently show balanced
Old Testament and Gospel formal examples in this section. The rest
remains explicitly delimited by nominalization, deictic, or verb-phrase
boundaries rather than expanded into a full directional chapter.

\subsection{Derivation / valency}\label{derivation-valency}

The current derivation and valency evidence is strongest around
\tdim{-sak} \glossquote{causative / benefactive}, especially
\tdim{paisak} \glossquote{cause to go} and \tdim{muhsak}
\glossquote{show / make see}, while \tdim{-pih}
\glossquote{with / accompanying}, \tdim{ki-}
\glossquote{reflexive / middle / passive-like}, and heavier suffix
stacks remain boundary material.

\subsubsection{Overview of derivation and valency
change}\label{overview-of-derivation-and-valency-change}

This section treats a controlled part of Tedim derivation and
valency-changing morphology rather than the whole derivational system.
The clearest present anchors are \tdim{-sak}
\glossquote{causative / benefactive}, especially \tdim{paisak}
\glossquote{cause to go} and \tdim{muhsak} \glossquote{show / make see},
while \tdim{-pih} \glossquote{with / accompanying}, \tdim{ki-}
\glossquote{reflexive / middle / passive-like}, and heavier suffix
stacks such as \tdim{ciahsakkik} \glossquote{send back},
\tdim{bawlsakthei} \glossquote{can cause to make}, and
\tdim{paikhiatsak} \glossquote{cause to go out} remain explicit
boundaries.

The goal is therefore modest. It is enough to show that Tedim has a
productive causative use of \tdim{-sak}
\glossquote{causative / benefactive}, a distinct benefactive or
applicative-like use centered on \tdim{muhsak}
\glossquote{show / make see}, and several nearby constructions that
should not yet be folded into the same account.

\subsubsection{Current derivation / valency
inventory}\label{current-derivation-valency-inventory}

{\def\LTcaptype{none} % do not increment counter
\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Form or pattern
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Rough function
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Example context
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Current grammar-facing status
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Boundary issue
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Form I + \tdim{-sak} & productive causative & \tdim{paisak} & supported
& safest core claim \\
Form II + \tdim{-sak} & benefactive or applicative-like extension &
\tdim{muhsak} & supported with caution & higher-level suffix analysis
remains open \\
\tdim{paisak} & cause to go & \tdim{Israel mite paisak in},
\tdim{ka kiangah hong paisak un} & supported & may interact with motion
semantics \\
\tdim{muhsak} & show / make see & \tdim{ka hong muhsak thu khempeuh},
\tdim{na mit khua hong bangci muhsak ahi hiam} & supported with caution
& exact benefactive versus applicative label remains open \\
\tdim{paipih} & go with / accompany & \tdim{paipih} & boundary material
& \tdim{-pih} still overlaps comitative and benefactive readings \\
\tdim{ki-} forms & reflexive, middle, or passive-like material &
\tdim{kisep}, \tdim{kigen} & boundary material & needs a separate
account \\
\tdim{ciahsakkik}, \tdim{bawlsakthei}, \tdim{paikhiatsak} &
derivation-heavy stacking & \tdim{hong ciahsakkik un},
\tdim{bawlsakthei}, \tdim{hong paikhiatsak ciangin} & boundary material
& overlaps VP structure and suffix stacking \\
\tdim{piangsak} & lexicalized causative-looking verb & \tdim{piangsak} &
boundary material & belongs with transitivity as well as derivation \\
\end{longtable}
}

The table keeps \tdim{paipih} \glossquote{go with / accompany},
\tdim{kisep} \glossquote{REFL-work / do by oneself}, \tdim{kigen}
\glossquote{be said / it is said}, and \tdim{piangsak}
\glossquote{cause to be born / create} visible without turning them into
solved core categories. A controlled section should show where the
evidence is strongest and where it still shifts into neighboring
domains.

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{Causative
\tdim{-sak}}{Causative }}\label{causative}

The form \tdim{paisak} \glossquote{cause to go} shows a productive
causative use of \tdim{-sak} \glossquote{causative / benefactive}. The
clearest current point is practical rather than maximal: Form I plus
\tdim{-sak} regularly supports a straightforward causative reading, and
\tdim{paisak} gives that pattern in a compact and transparent way.

The pair below keeps that point grounded in both Old Testament and
Gospel material. In Exodus the form is an imperative meaning
\glossquote{let the people go}, while in Mark it again licenses motion
toward the speaker.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:deriv-paisak-exod10}
\reviewwarninginline{[REVIEW PREVIEW WARNING: ex:deriv-paisak-exod10: Bible reference mapped, but slice example is not a direct verse span; analyzed slice wording]}
\gll \tdimword{Israel} \tdimword{mì-tè} \tdimword{pái-sàk} \tdimword{ìn} \\
     \textsc{israel} person-\textsc{pl} go-\textsc{caus} \textsc{erg} \\
\glt \glossquote{let the people of Israel go} (Exodus 10:7)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:deriv-paisak-mark10}
\gll \tdimword{kà} \tdimword{kìang-àh} \tdimword{hòng} \tdimword{pái-sàk} \tdimword{ùn} \\
     \textsc{1sg} beside-\textsc{loc} \textsc{3→1} go-\textsc{caus} \textsc{imp}.\textsc{pl} \\
\glt \glossquote{let them come to me} (Mark 10:14)
\end{exe}

These examples do not require a full derivational paradigm. They are
enough to support the narrower point that \tdim{paisak} keeps a
productive causative use clearly visible in ordinary discourse.

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{Benefactive and applicative-like
\tdim{-sak}}{Benefactive and applicative-like }}\label{benefactive-and-applicative-like}

The form \tdim{muhsak} \glossquote{show / make see} supports a distinct
benefactive or applicative-like use of \tdim{-sak}
\glossquote{causative / benefactive}. This subsection keeps the wording
cautious on purpose: the evidence is strong enough to separate
\tdim{muhsak} from the plain causative line, but it does not yet force a
final theoretical choice about exactly how many suffix senses should be
recognized.

The formal examples again show the same row across Old Testament and
Gospel material. Habakkuk presents \tdim{muhsak} in a reporting context,
while John uses it in a question about restored sight.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:deriv-muhsak-hab2}
\gll \tdimword{Nangmah} \tdimword{kà} \tdimword{hòng} \tdimword{mùh-sak} \tdimword{thù} \tdimword{khèmpeuh} \\
     \textsc{2sg}.\textsc{pro} \textsc{1sg} \textsc{3→1} see.\textsc{ii}-\textsc{benf} word all \\
\glt \glossquote{everything that I have shown you} (Habakkuk 2:2)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:deriv-muhsak-john9}
\gll \tdimword{nà} \tdimword{mìt} \tdimword{khùa} \tdimword{hòng} \tdimword{bangci} \tdimword{mùh-sak} \tdimword{àhì} \tdimword{hìam} \\
     \textsc{2sg} eye town \textsc{3→1} how see.\textsc{ii}-\textsc{benf} be.\textsc{3sg} \textsc{q} \\
\glt \glossquote{how did he open your eyes} (John 9:26)
\end{exe}

This pair is best treated as a distinct \tdim{-sak} line rather than as
a simple paraphrase of the causative examples above. At the same time,
the section stays smaller than a full applicative chapter and avoids
turning one well-supported row into a complete system claim.

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{The practical split within
\tdim{-sak}}{The practical split within }}\label{the-practical-split-within}

The practical descriptive split is therefore modest. Form I plus
\tdim{-sak} supports causative use, while Form II plus \tdim{-sak}
supports a distinct benefactive or applicative-like use.

That contrast is enough for a controlled grammar account, but the final
theoretical status of the split remains open. It may prove best to
describe these patterns as two readings within one suffixal domain, or
as two tightly related derivational uses that should still be separated
in description.

For the present account, the important point is simply that
\tdim{paisak} \glossquote{cause to go} and \tdim{muhsak}
\glossquote{show / make see} should not be flattened into the same gloss
line. The contrast is real even if the larger suffix theory remains
under discussion.

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{Boundary with
\tdim{-pih}}{Boundary with }}\label{boundary-with}

The nearest boundary beyond \tdim{-sak} is \tdim{-pih}
\glossquote{with / accompanying}. The form \tdim{paipih}
\glossquote{go with / accompany} is transparent enough to keep in view,
but its function still overlaps comitative, benefactive, and applicative
readings too strongly for this section to promote it as a second core
derivational anchor.

The same boundary also includes \tdim{mipihte}
\glossquote{companions / associated people}, which looks
\tdim{pih}-based but is noun-centered rather than a clear verbal valency
pattern. That is why the present section names \tdim{-pih} as an
adjacent domain without turning it into a separate applicative chapter.

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{Boundary with
\tdim{ki-}}{Boundary with }}\label{boundary-with-1}

The prefix \tdim{ki-} \glossquote{reflexive / middle / passive-like}
likewise remains outside the present core account. The clearest future
anchor is still \tdim{kisep} \glossquote{REFL-work / do by oneself},
while \tdim{kigen} \glossquote{be said / it is said} shows the more
agent-defocused or passive-like side of the same domain.

Both forms matter because they show that valency change in Tedim is not
confined to suffixes alone. Even so, the present section does not absorb
reflexive, middle, passive-like, or prefixal structure into a narrow
\tdim{-sak} discussion.

\subsubsection{Boundary with VP
stacking}\label{boundary-with-vp-stacking}

The overlap with VP structure is easiest to see in derivation-heavy
stacks. \tdim{Ciahsakkik} \glossquote{send back} is the clearest matched
pair, and it shows why derivational morphology and suffix ordering must
sometimes be discussed together without being collapsed into one
chapter. The same boundary keeps \tdim{bawlsakthei}
\glossquote{can cause to make} and \tdim{paikhiatsak}
\glossquote{cause to go out} visible as nearby stacked forms.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:deriv-ciahsakkik-gen24}
\gll \tdimword{hòng} \tdimword{ciah-sàk-kík} \tdimword{ùn} \\
     \textsc{3→1} return-\textsc{caus}-\textsc{iter} \textsc{imp}.\textsc{pl} \\
\glt \glossquote{send me back} (Genesis 24:54)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:deriv-ciahsakkik-mark12}
\gll \tdimword{à} \tdimword{guak} \tdimword{ciah-sàk-kík} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{hì} \\
     \textsc{3sg} back return-\textsc{caus}-\textsc{iter} 2/3PL \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{they sent him back empty} (Mark 12:3)
\end{exe}

These examples are useful precisely because they stop short of a larger
template claim. They show that derivational material can participate in
bigger suffix strings, while still leaving the full architecture of
those strings to the VP section.

\subsubsection{Boundary with
transitivity}\label{boundary-with-transitivity}

Some causative-looking verbs remain better treated as
transitivity-adjacent lexical items than as first-line derivational
evidence. \tdim{Piangsak} \glossquote{cause to be born / create} is the
clearest current example: it is too important to ignore, but too
lexicalized to carry the same descriptive weight as \tdim{paisak}
\glossquote{cause to go}.

This is why the present section does not try to settle every
transitivity question inside the \tdim{-sak} domain. Productive
causative morphology and lexicalized transitive verbs meet here, but
they should not yet be treated as one solved class.

\subsubsection{Deferred and boundary
material}\label{deferred-and-boundary-material-11}

Several issues remain outside the present account.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \tdim{paisak} \glossquote{cause to go} and \tdim{muhsak}
  \glossquote{show / make see} are the clearest current anchors, but
  they do not exhaust the derivational system.
\item
  \tdim{paipih} \glossquote{go with / accompany} and \tdim{mipihte}
  \glossquote{companions / associated people} keep the \tdim{-pih}
  boundary visible without settling its broader function.
\item
  \tdim{kisep} \glossquote{REFL-work / do by oneself} and \tdim{kigen}
  \glossquote{be said / it is said} show that \tdim{ki-} needs a
  separate reflexive, middle, and passive-like treatment.
\item
  \tdim{ciahsakkik} \glossquote{send back}, \tdim{bawlsakthei}
  \glossquote{can cause to make}, and \tdim{paikhiatsak}
  \glossquote{cause to go out} belong to the derivation-VP boundary
  rather than to a compact suffix list.
\item
  \tdim{piangsak} \glossquote{cause to be born / create} remains
  adjacent evidence at the edge of transitivity and lexicalization.
\item
  The section therefore stays with a narrow \tdim{-sak} account instead
  of expanding into a full derivational morphology or valency-changing
  chapter.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Summary}\label{summary-12}

The safest conclusion is that Tedim already shows a controlled
derivation / valency contrast centered on \tdim{-sak}
\glossquote{causative / benefactive}. \tdim{Paisak}
\glossquote{cause to go} supports a productive causative use,
\tdim{muhsak} \glossquote{show / make see} supports a distinct
benefactive or applicative-like use, and derivation-heavy stacks such as
\tdim{ciahsakkik} \glossquote{send back} show how quickly the same
material meets VP structure. The broader systems of \tdim{-pih},
\tdim{ki-}, and lexicalized transitivity remain visible, but they remain
outside the present account.

\subsection{ki- reflexive / reciprocal /
middle}\label{ki--reflexive-reciprocal-middle}

The current \tdim{ki-} evidence is strongest for reciprocal
\tdim{ki-gawm} \glossquote{join together} and subject-affected
\tdim{ki-lak-toh} \glossquote{be taken up}, while passive-like
\tdim{kigen} \glossquote{be said / be told} and lexicalized forms such
as \tdim{kipan} \glossquote{begin / from} remain explicit boundary
material.

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{Overview of \tdim{ki-} reflexive /
reciprocal / middle-like
marking}{Overview of  reflexive / reciprocal / middle-like marking}}\label{overview-of-reflexive-reciprocal-middle-like-marking}

This section isolates \tdim{ki-}
\glossquote{reflexive / reciprocal / middle-like} so that voice-like
evidence is no longer scattered across unrelated chapters. The first
claim is deliberately narrow: reciprocal \tdim{ki-gawm}
\glossquote{join together} and subject-affected \tdim{kilaktoh}
\glossquote{be taken up} are the clearest currently promoted
constructions.

The forms \tdim{kisep} \glossquote{REFL-work / do by oneself} and
\tdim{kigen} \glossquote{be said / be told} remain useful but cautious
support because lexicalization risk remains real. This section therefore
does not claim a full voice chapter, a full transitivity chapter, a full
prefix/agreement chapter, a full derivation chapter, or a full VP-slot
template.

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{Current \tdim{ki-}
inventory}{Current  inventory}}\label{current-inventory}

{\def\LTcaptype{none} % do not increment counter
\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Form or pattern
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Rough function
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright

\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Current grammar-facing status
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Boundary issue
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\tdim{ki-gawm} & reciprocal predicate & spouse-pair and dual-participant
clauses & supported core & reciprocal evidence does not settle all
\tdim{ki-} behavior \\
\tdim{kilaktoh} & middle or subject-affected predicate & ``be taken up''
rows & supported with caution & overlaps directionals and VP stacking \\
\tdim{kigen} & passive-like or agent-defocused reporting & ``be told /
cannot be told'' rows & supported with caution & analyzer glossing is
partly lexicalized \\
\tdim{kisep} & reflexive or self-directed support row & ``work done''
rows & supporting candidate & can be lexicalized in procedural
discourse \\
\tdim{kipan} & lexicalized or frozen \tdim{ki-} form & begin/from
sequences & boundary material & not a safe reflexive core claim \\
\tdim{ki-phuak}, \tdim{ki-piak-na} & \tdim{ki-} with derivational or VP
layering & naming and nominalized-event rows & boundary material &
overlaps TAM, nominalization, and valency \\
report-only \tdim{kibawl} counts & discovery-only inventory &
count-heavy report tables & blocked & count totals are not
constructional proof \\
\end{longtable}
}

\subsubsection{Safest reflexive / reciprocal / middle-like
evidence}\label{safest-reflexive-reciprocal-middle-like-evidence}

Reciprocal \tdim{ki-gawm} \glossquote{join together} is the strongest
starting point because participant structure is explicit and parallel
across Old Testament and Gospel examples.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:ki-rec-kigawm-gen2}
\gll \tdimword{à} \tdimword{zi} \tdimword{tàwh} \tdimword{kī-gawm} \tdimword{à,} \tdimword{àmau} \tdimword{tegel} \tdimword{pum} \tdimword{khàt} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{bàng} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{3sg} wife \textsc{com} \textsc{refl}-seize \textsc{3sg} \textsc{3pl}.\textsc{pro} both body one \textsc{3sg} like 2/3PL \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{He is joined with his wife, and the two become one body.} (Genesis 2:24)
\end{exe}

Matthew 19:5 provides the Gospel counterpart with the same reciprocal
predicate.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:ki-rec-kigawm-matt19}
\gll \tdimword{à} \tdimword{zi} \tdimword{tàwh} \tdimword{kī-gawm} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{à,} \tdimword{àmau} \tdimword{tegel} \tdimword{pumkhat} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{bàng} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{3sg} wife \textsc{com} \textsc{refl}-seize \textsc{irr} \textsc{3sg} \textsc{3pl}.\textsc{pro} both body \textsc{3sg} like \textsc{irr} 2/3PL \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{He will be joined with his wife, and the two will become one body.} (Matthew 19:5)
\end{exe}

Subject-affected \tdim{kilaktoh} \glossquote{be taken up} supplies the
cleanest middle-like support now available, while still keeping
directional and VP overlap visible.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:ki-mid-kilaktoh-num9}
\gll \tdimword{kī-làk-tòh} \\
     \textsc{refl}-take-\textsc{up} \\
\glt \glossquote{was taken up} (Numbers 9:17)
\end{exe}

Luke 9:51 gives the Gospel counterpart for the same subject-affected
construction.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:ki-mid-kilaktoh-luke9}
\gll \tdimword{à} \tdimword{kī-làk-tòh} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{hùn} \\
     \textsc{3sg} \textsc{refl}-take-\textsc{up} \textsc{irr} time \\
\glt \glossquote{the time for him to be taken up} (Luke 9:51)
\end{exe}

\subsubsection{Passive-like or agent-defocused
evidence}\label{passive-like-or-agent-defocused-evidence}

Passive-like or agent-defocused \tdim{kigen}
\glossquote{be said / be told} is useful, but it must remain cautious
because lexicalized reporting uses are common.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:ki-pass-kigen-gen27}
\reviewwarninginline{[REVIEW PREVIEW WARNING: ex:ki-pass-kigen-gen27: Bible reference mapped, but slice example is not a direct verse span; analyzed slice wording]}
\gll \tdimword{Rebekah} \tdimword{tùngàh} \tdimword{ki-gēn} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{rebekah} on-\textsc{loc} conversation \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{The words were told to Rebekah.} (Genesis 27:42)
\end{exe}

Matthew 2:10 keeps the same \tdim{kigen} line in a Gospel clause with
modal negation.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:ki-pass-kigen-matt2}
\gll \tdimword{à} \tdimword{lungdam-ná} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{ki-gēn} \tdimword{théi} \tdimword{lò} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{3sg} rejoice-\textsc{nmlz} 2/3PL conversation know \textsc{neg} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{their joy could not be told.} (Matthew 2:10)
\end{exe}

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{Lexicalized or frozen \tdim{ki-}
boundary}{Lexicalized or frozen  boundary}}\label{lexicalized-or-frozen-boundary}

Forms such as \tdim{kipan} \glossquote{begin / from} remain boundary
material in this section. They are important for coverage, but they do
not justify promotion into the core reflexive/reciprocal/middle claim.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:ki-lex-kipan-gen4}
\gll \tdimword{Túa} \tdimword{hùn} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{ki-pan-ìn} \tdimword{mì-tè} \tdimword{ìn} \tdimword{Topa} \tdimword{bía-ìn} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{mīn} \tdimword{làwh} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{kī-pan} \tdimword{tá} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{dist} time \textsc{3sg} from person-\textsc{pl} \textsc{erg} Lord worship-\textsc{erg} \textsc{3sg} name \textsc{perf} \textsc{irr} \textsc{refl}-begin child 2/3PL \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{From that time people began to call on the name of the Lord.} (Genesis 4:26)
\end{exe}

Matthew 4:17 gives the Gospel counterpart with the same lexicalized
begin/from profile.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:ki-lex-kipan-matt4}
\gll \tdimword{Túa} \tdimword{hùn} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{kī-pan} \tdimword{Jesuh} \tdimword{ìn} \tdimword{thù-hìlh} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{kī-pan} \tdimword{à.} \\
     \textsc{dist} time \textsc{3sg} \textsc{refl}-begin \textsc{jesuh} \textsc{erg} word-teach \textsc{irr} \textsc{refl}-begin \textsc{3sg} \\
\glt \glossquote{From that time Jesus began to preach.} (Matthew 4:17)
\end{exe}

\subsubsection{Boundary with derivation, VP stacking, transitivity, and
prefix/agreement}\label{boundary-with-derivation-vp-stacking-transitivity-and-prefixagreement}

The forms \tdim{ki-phuak} \glossquote{be called} and \tdim{ki-piak-na}
\glossquote{REFL-give-NMLZ} show that \tdim{ki-} rapidly crosses into
derivation, nominalization, TAM layering, and VP stacking. The same is
true for overlap with transitivity and prefix/agreement boundaries. For
that reason, these rows remain boundary material rather than new core
evidence in this first section.

\subsubsection{Deferred and boundary
material}\label{deferred-and-boundary-material-12}

Several issues remain outside the present account.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \tdim{ki-gawm} and \tdim{kilaktoh} support a controlled first claim,
  but they do not settle the full voice system.
\item
  \tdim{kisep} and \tdim{kigen} remain cautious support rows with
  lexicalization risk.
\item
  \tdim{kipan} remains lexicalized or frozen boundary material.
\item
  \tdim{ki-} plus derivational, TAM, and VP-heavy material remains
  boundary material.
\item
  Raw report counts are not treated as grammar facts without
  constructional control.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Summary}\label{summary-13}

The safest grammar-facing conclusion is a narrow one: reciprocal
\tdim{ki-gawm} and subject-affected \tdim{kilaktoh} provide clear first
anchors for a dedicated \tdim{ki-} section, while passive-like
\tdim{kigen}, supporting \tdim{kisep}, lexicalized \tdim{kipan}, and
derivation/VP overlaps remain explicit boundaries.

\subsection{-pih comitative
applicative}\label{pih-comitative-applicative}

The current \tdim{-pih} evidence is strongest for verbal comitative
applicative forms such as \tdim{paipih}
\glossquote{go with / accompany}, \tdim{nekpih} \glossquote{eat with},
and \tdim{tunpih} \glossquote{arrive with / bring with}, while nominal
\tdim{-pih} forms such as \tdim{innkuanpihte}
\glossquote{family members} remain explicit boundary material.

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{Overview of verbal \tdim{-pih} comitative
applicative}{Overview of verbal  comitative applicative}}\label{overview-of-verbal-comitative-applicative}

This section isolates verbal \tdim{-pih} as a comitative applicative
suffix so that its function is no longer treated only as a boundary note
within the derivation/valency account. The first claim is deliberately
narrow but now empirically stabilized: verbal \tdim{-pih} is best
treated as a Stem 2 / Form II selecting suffix that introduces or
indexes a comitative participant or comitative object.

Verbal \tdim{-pih} must be kept distinct from nominal \tdim{-pih}
meaning \glossquote{member / group member}, which is documented
independently by Henderson (1965) and illustrated in forms such as
\tdim{innkuanpihte} \glossquote{family members} and \tdim{mipihte}
\glossquote{group members / companions}. This section does not claim a
full applicative chapter, a full valency chapter, a full derivational
morphology chapter, a full VP-slot template, or a complete comitative
system. It also does not treat every frequent \tdim{-pih} form as
equally diagnostic for stem alternation: \tdim{nekpih} is currently the
clearest internal diagnostic, while \tdim{paipih} and \tdim{paikhiatpih}
require morphophonological caution around \tdim{pai / paih}.

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{Current \tdim{-pih}
inventory}{Current  inventory}}\label{current-inventory-1}

{\def\LTcaptype{none} % do not increment counter
\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}

  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}

  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}

\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright

\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead

\endlastfoot
\tdim{paipih} & comitative motion applicative & \tdim{paipih} in
bring/lead contexts & supported core with stem caveat & strong
functional evidence, but \tdim{pai / paih} diagnostics remain
unresolved \\

member'' (Henderson); must not be treated as verbal applicative \\

members'' contexts & boundary material & nominal -pih ``group member''
(Henderson); boundary only \\

directional & boundary material & directional -khiat stacked before
-pih; VP-stacking boundary \\
