\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{Verbal \tdim{-pih} as comitative
applicative}{Verbal  as comitative applicative}}\label{verbal-as-comitative-applicative}

The clearest current evidence for verbal \tdim{-pih} as a comitative
applicative is \tdim{paipih} \glossquote{go with / accompany / bring}.
The form is transparent and highly productive across the corpus, and it
is consistent with the Otsuka (2011) analysis of \tdim{-pih} as a
comitative applicative that attaches to Form II / Stem 2 verbs and
introduces a new core argument representing either a comitative
participant or a comitative object.

In Genesis the form appears as God bringing the woman to the man,
introducing the woman as the accompanying participant.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:pih-core-paipih-gen2}
\gll \tdimword{mipa} \tdimword{kìang-àh} \tdimword{túa} \tdimword{nù} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{pái-pìh} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     man beside-\textsc{loc} \textsc{dist} female \textsc{3sg} go-\textsc{appl} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{[God] brought her to the man.} (Genesis 2:22)
\end{exe}

The same form \tdim{paipih} appears in the Gospels in a
motion-to-wilderness context where the Holy Spirit leads Jesus.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:pih-core-paipih-matt4}
\gll \tdimword{Khá} \tdimword{Sìangtho} \tdimword{ìn} \tdimword{gàm-lak-àh} \tdimword{Jesuh} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{pái-pìh} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{neg}.\textsc{perf} holy \textsc{erg} land-midst-\textsc{loc} \textsc{jesuh} \textsc{3sg} go-\textsc{appl} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{The Holy Spirit led Jesus into the wilderness.} (Matthew 4:1)
\end{exe}

These two examples are enough to establish \tdim{paipih} as a productive
verbal comitative applicative form. They do not, by themselves, prove
the full stem-selection rule, because the exact \tdim{pai / paih} stem
relation remains morphophonologically difficult in current section
evidence.

\subsubsection{Form II / Stem 2
restriction}\label{form-ii-stem-2-restriction}

The Form II restriction is established primarily by literature evidence.
Otsuka (2011) states that comitative \tdim{-pih} attaches only to Form
II verbs, and ZNC (2017/2018) independently documents this with explicit
grammaticality contrasts.

The current section uses a split diagnostic logic rather than one
undifferentiated claim:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Diagnostic row:} \tdim{nekpih}, because \tdim{ne / nek} is
  independently stabilized as a Form I / Form II pair in the
  stem-alternation section.
\item
  \textbf{Compatible but not independently diagnostic rows:}
  \tdim{tunpih} and \tdim{ompih}.
\item
  \textbf{Morphophonological-boundary rows:} \tdim{paipih} and
  \tdim{paikhiatpih}, because \tdim{pai / paih} remains unresolved in
  current section evidence and may involve non-realization of stem-final
  \tdim{h} before \tdim{-pih}.
\end{itemize}

The safest statement is therefore strong but bounded: Stem 2 selection
is the best-supported analysis, but the corpus alone does not prove that
rule equally for every \tdim{-pih} form.

This note is not a claim to have verified the restriction exhaustively.
A human reviewer should check whether any counter-example exists in the
wider corpus.

\subsubsection{Transitive verb contexts}\label{transitive-verb-contexts}

The comitative applicative also attaches to transitive verbs, as shown
by \tdim{nekpih} \glossquote{eat together with}. In a Proverbs context
the form appears in a purposive, clause-bound irrealis construction
where the speaker invites the addressee to share a meal.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:pih-trans-nekpih-prov7}
\gll \tdimword{Kà} \tdimword{nèk-pìh} \tdimword{dìng-ìn} \tdimword{nàng} \tdimword{kà} \tdimword{hòng} \tdimword{zòng} \tdimword{à,} \tdimword{tù-ìn} \tdimword{kòng} \tdimword{mú} \tdimword{pet-màh} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{1sg} eat-with \textsc{irr}-\textsc{erg} \textsc{2sg}.\textsc{pro} \textsc{1sg} \textsc{3→1} also \textsc{3sg} now-\textsc{erg} \textsc{1sg→3} see.\textsc{i} find-exactly \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{I came looking for you, to eat together with me.} (Proverbs 7:15)
\end{exe}

The Gospel counterpart shows the same \tdim{nekpih} in an invitation
clause where Jesus is the comitative participant.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:pih-trans-nekpih-luke11}
\gll \tdimword{Farisi} \tdimword{mì} \tdimword{khàt} \tdimword{ìn} \tdimword{àn} \tdimword{nèk-pìh} \tdimword{dìng-ìn} \tdimword{Jesuh} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{sam} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{farisi} person one \textsc{erg} \textsc{3pl}.\textsc{poss} eat-with \textsc{irr}-\textsc{erg} \textsc{jesuh} \textsc{3sg} open \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{A Pharisee invited Jesus to eat with him.} (Luke 11:37)
\end{exe}

Both rows show \tdim{nekpih} in purposive or clause-bound irrealis
environments (\tdim{ding-in}), matching the grammar-facing treatment
already used in TAM, clause-linkage, and VP-structure boundary
discussions.

\subsubsection{Motion verbs and comitative
objects}\label{motion-verbs-and-comitative-objects}

Otsuka (2011) distinguishes two types of comitative argument introduced
by \tdim{-pih}: a comitative participant (a person who goes along) and a
comitative object (a thing that is brought along). The form
\tdim{tunpih} \glossquote{arrive with / bring with / come with}
illustrates both types.

In Genesis 8 the dove arrives with a freshly plucked olive leaf as
comitative object.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:pih-motion-tunpih-gen8}
\gll \tdimword{oliv} \tdimword{teh} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{tukkhiat} \tdimword{thàk} \tdimword{khàt} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{mú-kàh} \tdimword{pet-ìn} \tdimword{hòng} \tdimword{tun-pìh} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     olive measure \textsc{3sg} pluck.off new one \textsc{3sg} see-mouth pluck-leaf \textsc{3→1} arrive-\textsc{appl} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{[the dove] arrived with a freshly plucked olive leaf in its beak.} (Genesis 8:11)
\end{exe}

In Matthew 9 the same form \tdim{tunpih} introduces a comitative
participant (the paralytic brought to Jesus).

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:pih-motion-tunpih-matt9}
\gll \tdimword{Mì} \tdimword{pàwl-khàt} \tdimword{ìn} \tdimword{khùt-lè-khe-zàw} \tdimword{mì} \tdimword{khàt} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{lupna} \tdimword{pheek} \tdimword{tàwh} \tdimword{hòng} \tdimword{pua-ìn} \tdimword{Jesuh} \tdimword{kìang} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{hòng} \tdimword{tun-pìh} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     person some-one \textsc{erg} hand-and-foot-more person one \textsc{3sg} bed bed \textsc{com} \textsc{3→1} carry.on.back-\textsc{erg} \textsc{jesuh} beside \textsc{3sg} \textsc{3→1} arrive-\textsc{appl} 2/3PL \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{Some people brought a paralytic to Jesus.} (Matthew 9:2)
\end{exe}

The contrast between these two rows keeps both comitative object and
comitative participant uses visible without forcing a single analysis of
all \tdim{tunpih} occurrences.

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{Boundary with nominal
\tdim{-pih}}{Boundary with nominal }}\label{boundary-with-nominal}

Nominal \tdim{-pih} meaning \glossquote{member / group member} must
remain a visible boundary in any grammar-facing account of verbal
\tdim{-pih}. Henderson (1965) documents this suffix with examples such
as \tdim{a inkuan-pih te} \glossquote{his family members},
\tdim{ka kho-pih} \glossquote{my village-mate}, and \tdim{na sang-pih}
\glossquote{your school-mate}. The corpus contains over a hundred
occurrences of \tdim{innkuanpihte} \glossquote{family members} and
\tdim{mipihte} \glossquote{group members}, all in clearly nominal
contexts.

No rows from this nominal domain have been promoted as verbal
applicative evidence. The homophony is real and the synchronic
relationship between the two \tdim{-pih} forms remains an open question
in the literature.

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{Boundary with directionals, VP stacking,
and \tdim{-pih}
interactions}{Boundary with directionals, VP stacking, and  interactions}}\label{boundary-with-directionals-vp-stacking-and-interactions}

The form \tdim{paikhiatpih} \glossquote{bring out with} shows
\tdim{-pih} stacked after the directional suffix \tdim{-khiat}
\glossquote{away / out}. ZNC (2017/2018) also documents
\tdim{pai-pih-suk} \glossquote{walk down along with}, and Otsuka (2011)
notes that \tdim{-pih} and the relinquitive \tdim{-san} can co-occur.
These stacked forms are important for completeness but they remain
boundary material in this first account.

Similarly, the high-frequency forms \tdim{hopih}, \tdim{hehpih}, and
\tdim{ompih} appear extensively in the corpus in contexts that may
involve lexicalized interaction, grace/mercy vocabulary, or
stative-comitative readings. These rows remain supporting or boundary
material: they are useful for distribution and coverage, but they are
not treated as primary stem diagnostics.

\subsubsection{Deferred and boundary
material}\label{deferred-and-boundary-material-13}

Several areas remain outside this account.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \tdim{paipih}, \tdim{nekpih}, and \tdim{tunpih} are the clearest
  promoted forms, but they play different diagnostic roles for stem
  alternation.
\item
  \tdim{hopih}, \tdim{hehpih}, \tdim{ompih}, \tdim{ciahpih}, and
  \tdim{tenpih} appear frequently but their precise applicative vs
  lexicalized status remains unresolved.
\item
  Stem 2 selection remains the best-supported rule, but \tdim{paipih}
  and \tdim{paikhiatpih} remain morphophonological-boundary rows for
  that rule.
\item
  Nominal \tdim{innkuanpihte} and \tdim{mipihte} remain boundary
  material and must not be promoted as verbal evidence.
\item
  Stacked directional forms such as \tdim{paikhiatpih} remain boundary
  material pending VP-slot analysis.
\item
  The potential benefactive reading of \tdim{-pih} (noted by Otsuka as a
  possible secondary sense) is not promoted and remains an open
  question.
\item
  Raw report counts are not grammar facts without constructional and
  stem-diagnostic control.
\item
  The comitative system as a whole --- including \tdim{-khawm}
  \glossquote{together} and case-marked comitative strategies --- lies
  outside the present narrow account.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Summary}\label{summary-14}

The safest grammar-facing conclusion is a narrow but concrete one: Tedim
has a verbal \tdim{-pih} comitative applicative suffix best analyzed as
Stem 2 selecting. \tdim{Nekpih} provides the clearest section-internal
diagnostic support for that stem claim; \tdim{paipih} and \tdim{tunpih}
remain strong functional evidence, with \tdim{paipih} explicitly
caveated as a morphophonological-boundary row around \tdim{pai / paih}.
Nominal \tdim{-pih} \glossquote{member / group member} remains a
separate boundary category, and stacked directional or VP-heavy forms
remain outside the present core claim.

\subsection{Nominalization}\label{nominalization}

The current nominalization evidence is strongest for productive deverbal
\tdim{-na} \glossquote{nominalizer}, with \tdim{bawlna}
\glossquote{making / creation} as a compact anchor, while \tdim{-pa}
\glossquote{agentive / person} and \tdim{-mi}
\glossquote{person / one who} material remains explicit boundary
evidence.

\subsubsection{Overview of nominalization in this
section}\label{overview-of-nominalization-in-this-section}

This section treats a controlled part of Tedim nominalization centered
on productive deverbal \tdim{-na} \glossquote{nominalizer}. It keeps
agentive or person-head material and nominalized relative-clause
material as boundaries so the discussion remains grammar-facing without
turning into a full nominalization, relative-clause, or derivational
chapter.

\subsubsection{Current nominalization
inventory}\label{current-nominalization-inventory}

{\def\LTcaptype{none} % do not increment counter
\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Form or pattern
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright

\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Current grammar-facing status

\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\tdim{-na} & deverbal nominalizer & \tdim{theihna}, \tdim{ciaptehna} &
well supported & broader nominalization inventory remains open \\
\tdim{bawlna} & deverbal nominal from \tdim{bawl} & lexical anchor in
running prose & supported with caveat & needs wider textual distribution
work \\
\tdim{bawl-na} & segmented deverbal nominal & morpheme-level analysis &
supported with caveat & segmentation is clearer than full paradigm
scope \\
\tdim{-pa} & agentive or person-head nominalization & \tdim{lawmpa},
\tdim{bawlpa} & boundary material & overlaps lexicalized and title-like
uses \\
\tdim{-mi} & person-head or relative-linked nominalization &
\tdim{a hong pai mi}, \tdim{a bawl mi} & boundary material & overlaps
relative-clause analysis \\
\tdim{omna} & nominalized clause-like form & \tdim{omna mun} patterns &
boundary material & overlaps clause linkage and NP structure \\

\tdim{Pasian' muhna-ah} & boundary material & overlaps case marking and
clause linkage \\

boundary material & not a transparent productive \tdim{-pa} pattern \\
\tdim{Topa} & title-like lexical form & reverential title contexts &
boundary material & cannot be treated as a productive \tdim{-pa}
derivation \\
\end{longtable}
}

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{Deverbal nominalization with
\tdim{-na}}{Deverbal nominalization with }}\label{deverbal-nominalization-with}

The suffix \tdim{-na} \glossquote{nominalizer} forms deverbal nouns and
remains the safest productive anchor in the current evidence. The form
\tdim{bawlna} \glossquote{making / creation} with segmentation
\tdim{bawl-na} \glossquote{make-NMLZ} is the clearest compact anchor for
this function, even though a full paradigm is still outside the present
account.

The same morphology appears in stable textual examples such as
\tdim{theihna} \glossquote{knowledge} and \tdim{ciaptehna}
\glossquote{record / writing}. Together they support a grammar-facing
claim for productive deverbal nominalization without forcing a full
inventory of all nominal outcomes.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:nmlz-theihna-gen2}
\gll \tdimword{à} \tdimword{phà} \tdimword{lè} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{sía} \tdimword{thèih-ná} \tdimword{sing-kung} \tdimword{gah} \\
     \textsc{3sg} branch and \textsc{3sg} evil know.\textsc{ii}-\textsc{nmlz} tree branch \\
\glt \glossquote{the fruit of the tree of the knowledge of good and evil} (Genesis 2:17)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:nmlz-ciaptehna-matt1}
\gll \tdimword{Abraham'} \tdimword{suan} \tdimword{David,} \tdimword{David'} \tdimword{suan} \tdimword{Jesuh} \tdimword{Khrih'} \tdimword{khang} \tdimword{ciapteh-ná} \tdimword{híh} \tdimword{bàng} \tdimword{àhì} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{abraham}.\textsc{poss} plant \textsc{david} \textsc{david}.\textsc{poss} plant \textsc{jesuh} \textsc{khrih}.\textsc{poss} generation receive-\textsc{nmlz} \textsc{prox} like be.\textsc{3sg} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{This is the record of the genealogy of Jesus Christ, son of David, son of Abraham.} (Matthew 1:1)
\end{exe}

\subsubsection{Nominalization and stem
alternation}\label{nominalization-and-stem-alternation}

Nominalized contexts connect directly to Form II evidence in stem
alternation, especially where forms such as \tdim{theihna}
\glossquote{knowledge} keep a nominalizer on a stem family that also
participates in Form I/Form II contrasts. This is useful as
constructional evidence, but it does not reopen a full stem-alternation
chapter here.

The key point is local: nominalized environments help explain why Form
II-like material remains visible in nominalized stems, while broader
stem-class claims still depend on the stem-alternation section.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:nmlz-stem-theihna-gen2}
\gll \tdimword{à} \tdimword{phà} \tdimword{lè} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{sía} \tdimword{thèih-ná} \tdimword{sing-kung} \\
     \textsc{3sg} branch and \textsc{3sg} evil know.\textsc{ii}-\textsc{nmlz} tree \\
\glt \glossquote{the tree of the knowledge of good and evil} (Genesis 2:9)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:nmlz-stem-theihna-matt7}
\gll \tdimword{nà} \tdimword{lakkhiat} \tdimword{thèih-ná} \tdimword{dìng-ùn} \\
     \textsc{2sg} snatch know.\textsc{ii}-\textsc{nmlz} \textsc{irr}-\textsc{pl}.\textsc{imp} \\
\glt \glossquote{so that you may be able to remove it} (Matthew 7:5)
\end{exe}

\subsubsection{Agentive or person-head nominalization
boundary}\label{agentive-or-person-head-nominalization-boundary}

Agentive and person-head material is visible through \tdim{-pa}
\glossquote{agentive / person} and \tdim{-mi}
\glossquote{person / one who}, including forms such as \tdim{bawlpa}
\glossquote{maker / doer} and \tdim{hong pai mi}
\glossquote{one who came}. The current evidence supports discussing
these forms as boundaries, but not promoting them to the core
nominalization system yet.

This caution matters because \tdim{-pa} patterns are easily mixed with
lexicalized titles, while \tdim{-mi} patterns are often
indistinguishable from person-head relative constructions.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:nmlz-lawmpa-judg7}
\gll \tdimword{Túa} \tdimword{cìang-ìn} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{lawm-pá} \tdimword{ìn} \tdimword{dawng-ìn.} \\
     \textsc{dist} then-\textsc{erg} \textsc{3sg} worthy-\textsc{nmlz}.\textsc{ag} \textsc{erg} receive-\textsc{erg} \\
\glt \glossquote{Then his companion answered.} (Judges 7:14)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:nmlz-lawmpa-matt1}
\gll \tdimword{À} \tdimword{lawm-pá} \tdimword{Josef} \tdimword{pèn} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{màn-ìn} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{gamta} \tdimword{núam} \tdimword{mì} \tdimword{khàt} \tdimword{àhìh} \tdimword{màn-ìn} \\
     \textsc{3sg} worthy-\textsc{nmlz}.\textsc{ag} \textsc{josef} \textsc{top} \textsc{3sg} reason-\textsc{erg} \textsc{3sg} send.away want person one be.\textsc{3sg}.\textsc{rel} reason-\textsc{erg} \\
\glt \glossquote{Because Joseph her husband was a righteous man} (Matthew 1:19)
\end{exe}

\subsubsection{Nominalized relatives and clause-derived nominalization
boundary}\label{nominalized-relatives-and-clause-derived-nominalization-boundary}

Forms such as \tdim{omna} \glossquote{place / being / existence} and
\tdim{a bawl mi} \glossquote{one who made} belong to the overlap between
nominalization and relative-clause structure. They are important for
analysis, but they remain boundary material in this section.

The same is true for \tdim{a hong pai mi} \glossquote{one who came}: it
is structurally informative, but it is safer to coordinate with clause
linkage than to treat it as a settled nominalization subtype here.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:nmlz-hong-pai-mi-zech14}
\gll \tdimword{bìak-ná} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{pía} \tdimword{dìng-ìn} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{hòng} \tdimword{pái} \tdimword{mì} \tdimword{khèmpeuh} \tdimword{ìn} \tdimword{túa} \tdimword{beel-tè} \tdimword{zang-ìn} \\
     worship-\textsc{nmlz} \textsc{3sg} give \textsc{irr}-\textsc{erg} \textsc{3sg} \textsc{3→1} go person all \textsc{erg} \textsc{dist} pan-\textsc{pl} side-\textsc{cvb} \\
\glt \glossquote{all who come to offer sacrifice will use those pots} (Zechariah 14:21)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:nmlz-hong-pai-mi-john6}
\gll \tdimword{kà} \tdimword{kìang-àh} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{hòng} \tdimword{pái} \tdimword{mì} \tdimword{peuhmah} \tdimword{kà} \tdimword{hawlkhia} \tdimword{kèi} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{1sg} beside-\textsc{loc} \textsc{3sg} \textsc{3→1} go person whosoever \textsc{1sg} drive.out \textsc{neg} \textsc{irr} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{I will never cast out anyone who comes to me.} (John 6:37)
\end{exe}

\subsubsection{Nominalization plus case
boundary}\label{nominalization-plus-case-boundary}

Nominalization-plus-case forms such as \tdim{muhna-ah}
\glossquote{in seeing / in the sight of} are well attested but
structurally mixed. They show nominalization and case-like morphology in
the same form, so they belong partly with case marking and partly with
clause linkage.

For that reason, this section treats them as boundary evidence rather
than as a core nominalization subtype.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:nmlz-muhna-ah-gen6}
\gll \tdimword{Tù-ìn} \tdimword{Pasian'} \tdimword{mùh-ná-àh} \tdimword{léi-tùng} \tdimword{sía} \tdimword{à.} \\
     now-\textsc{erg} God.\textsc{poss} see.\textsc{ii}-\textsc{nmlz}-\textsc{loc} land-on evil \textsc{3sg} \\
\glt \glossquote{Now the earth was corrupt in the sight of God.} (Genesis 6:11)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:nmlz-muhna-ah-luke19}
\gll \tdimword{kèima} \tdimword{mùh-ná-àh} \tdimword{thàt} \tdimword{ùn.} \\
     \textsc{1sg}.self see.\textsc{ii}-\textsc{nmlz}-\textsc{loc} kill \textsc{imp}.\textsc{pl} \\
\glt \glossquote{kill them before me.} (Luke 19:27)
\end{exe}

\subsubsection{Lexicalized and title-like boundary
material}\label{lexicalized-and-title-like-boundary-material}

Title-like forms such as \tdim{kumpipa} \glossquote{king} and
\tdim{Topa} \glossquote{Lord} are important to keep visible because they
look segmentable but behave as lexicalized items in many contexts.
Treating them as straightforward productive \tdim{-pa} nominalizations
would overstate what the controlled evidence currently supports.

These rows therefore remain lexicalized boundary material in the present
account.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:nmlz-kumpipa-gen26}
\gll \tdimword{Filistia} \tdimword{mite'} \tdimword{kùmpì-pà} \tdimword{Abimelek'} \tdimword{ōm-ná} \tdimword{Gerar-àh} \tdimword{Isaac} \tdimword{pái} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{filistia} person-\textsc{pl}.\textsc{poss} king-male \textsc{abimelek}.\textsc{poss} exist-\textsc{nmlz} \textsc{gerar}-\textsc{loc} \textsc{isaac} go \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{Isaac went to Gerar, where Abimelech king of the Philistines lived.} (Genesis 26:1)
\end{exe}

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:nmlz-Topa-luke1}
\gll \tdimword{Topa'} \tdimword{mùh-ná-àh} \tdimword{àmàh} \tdimword{mì-lìan} \tdimword{khàt} \tdimword{hì} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     Lord.\textsc{poss} see.\textsc{ii}-\textsc{nmlz}-\textsc{loc} \textsc{3sg}.\textsc{pro} person-great one \textsc{decl} \textsc{irr} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{He will be great in the sight of the Lord.} (Luke 1:15)
\end{exe}

\subsubsection{Deferred and boundary
material}\label{deferred-and-boundary-material-14}

Several issues remain outside the present account.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \tdim{bawlna} and \tdim{bawl-na} support a controlled deverbal core,
  but they do not by themselves settle the full nominalization paradigm.
\item
  \tdim{-pa}, \tdim{bawlpa}, \tdim{-mi}, and \tdim{hong pai mi} remain
  boundary material until lexicalized-title and relative-clause overlap
  is clearer.
\item
  \tdim{omna} and \tdim{a bawl mi} \glossquote{person who} remain
  boundary forms shared with clause linkage and relative structure.
\item
  \tdim{muhna-ah} remains a boundary form shared with case marking and
  clause linkage.
\item
  \tdim{kumpipa} and \tdim{Topa} remain lexicalized or title-like rows
  rather than transparent productive derivations.
\item
  Bare \tdim{na} remains noisy and should not be promoted without
  stronger controlled evidence.
\item
  Raw occurrence counts are not treated as grammar facts without
  constructional control.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Summary}\label{summary-15}

The safest grammar-facing conclusion is that Tedim has productive
deverbal nominalization with \tdim{-na} \glossquote{nominalizer}, with
\tdim{bawlna} \glossquote{making / creation} and \tdim{bawl-na}
\glossquote{make-NMLZ} as compact anchors. Agentive and person-head
material, nominalized relative patterns, nominalization-plus-case forms,
and lexicalized title-like forms remain explicit boundaries so the
section stays controlled.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\tdim{mi}-headed and nominalized
relative-like
constructions}{-headed and nominalized relative-like constructions}}\label{headed-and-nominalized-relative-like-constructions}

The current relative-like evidence is narrow: the head noun \tdim{mi}
\glossquote{person} in the clearest source-resolved pattern is the main
diagnostic, \tdim{omte} \glossquote{those who exist} remains supporting,
and \tdim{omna} \glossquote{place / being / existence} plus
\tdim{muhna-ah} \glossquote{in the sight} remain boundary material. No
equally clean Gospel example is currently used, so the first slice stays
OT-heavy and cautious.

\subsubsection{Overview}\label{overview}

This section treats a narrow relative-like domain: the clearest
source-resolved pattern is \tdim{mi} \glossquote{person}, while
\tdim{omte} \glossquote{those who exist} is only supporting, and
nominalized \tdim{omna} \glossquote{place / being / existence} plus
case-marked \tdim{muhna-ah} \glossquote{in the sight} stay boundary
material. The scope is intentionally modest, and no equally clean Gospel
example is currently used for the first slice.

\subsubsection{Relative-like inventory}\label{relative-like-inventory}

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\tdim{mi}-headed
relatives}{-headed relatives}}\label{headed-relatives}

No equally clean Gospel example is currently used for this construction,
so the first slice stays OT-only.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:rc-abawlmi-ex30}
\gll \tdimword{pāk-nam-tui} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{bāwl} \tdimword{mì} \tdimword{peuhmah} \\
     ointment \textsc{3sg} make person whosoever \\
\glt \glossquote{whoever makes any like it} (Exodus 30:38)
\end{exe}

The form \tdim{a bawl mi} shows a person-noun \tdim{mi} (person)
modified by a prenominal predicate \tdim{a bawl} (3SG makes). The prefix
\tdim{a-} is ordinary third-person singular agreement, not a unique
relativizer; in prenominal position before a head noun, it yields a
relative-like reading. The head noun \tdim{mi} is the understood subject
of the relative predicate. The report documents 22 tokens of this
pattern, all with the same prenominal structure.

\subsubsection{Plural relative-like
support}\label{plural-relative-like-support}

No equally clean Gospel example is currently used here, so the support
row stays OT-only.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:rc-omte-gen2}
\gll \tdimword{à} \tdimword{sùng-à} \tdimword{ōm-tè} \tdimword{khèmpeuh} \\
     \textsc{3sg} inside-\textsc{3sg} exist-\textsc{pl} all \\
\glt \glossquote{all that was in it} (Genesis 2:1)
\end{exe}

The form \tdim{omte} combines \tdim{om} \glossquote{exist / be} with
plural \tdim{-te}. The reading is still ambiguous between a
headless/free relative and a plural nominalization, so this remains
support rather than a core diagnostic.

\subsubsection{Nominalization boundary}\label{nominalization-boundary-1}

\tdim{omna} \glossquote{place of being / presence} remains boundary
material here. It is source-resolved in Genesis 4:16, but the \tdim{-na}
analysis belongs with the nominalization section rather than with the
relative-clause core.

\subsubsection{Deferred material}\label{deferred-material-6}

\tdim{a om lai} and \tdim{a gen thu} remain report-only rows because no
specific Bible verse has been resolved for them yet. They stay in the
inventory, but they are not printed as formal examples.

\subsubsection{Summary}\label{summary-16}

The current evidence supports a cautious grammar-facing claim about
\tdim{mi}-headed relative-like constructions, with \tdim{omte} as
support and nominalized/case-marked forms kept separate. The \tdim{a-}
prefix remains ambiguous between relativizer and ordinary 3SG agreement.

\subsection{Clause linkage}\label{clause-linkage}

The current clause-linkage evidence is strongest for temporal
subordination with \tdim{ciangin} \glossquote{when}, marked
clause-finally as \tdim{tua ciangin} \glossquote{then / when}, while
purposive material with \tdim{dingin} \glossquote{in order to},
same-subject converb construction with \tdim{ngenin}
\glossquote{pray-CVB}, different-subject marking with
\tdim{ahih ciangin} \glossquote{when}, and relative clauses including
\tdim{a bawl mi} \glossquote{person who} remain explicit boundary
material.

\subsubsection{Overview of clause linkage in this
section}\label{overview-of-clause-linkage-in-this-section}

This section treats controlled temporal subordination with
\tdim{ciangin} \glossquote{when} as the core clause-linkage anchor.
Purposive marking with \tdim{dingin} \glossquote{in order to},
same-subject converb construction with \tdim{ngenin}
\glossquote{pray-CVB}, different-subject temporal linkage with
\tdim{ahih ciangin} \glossquote{when}, prenominal relative clauses with
\tdim{a bawl mi} \glossquote{person who}, and nominalization-plus-case
forms with \tdim{muhna-ah} \glossquote{in seeing / in the sight} remain
explicit boundaries. The section stays narrow without attempting a full
complex-sentence, switch-reference, relative-clause, or
nominalized-clause chapter.

\subsubsection{Core temporal subordination:
ciangin}\label{core-temporal-subordination-ciangin}

The suffix \tdim{ciangin} \glossquote{when} is the safest productive
anchor for clause linkage and forms temporal subordinate clauses. The
form \tdim{tua ciangin} \glossquote{then / when} with segmentation
\tdim{tua ciang-in} \glossquote{that when-ERG} is the clearest compact
anchor for temporal subordination, and it appears consistently across
Bible texts.

The same morphology appears in patterns such as \tdim{ahih ciangin}
\glossquote{when it was} where a relative marker \tdim{ahih} precedes
the subordinator, showing how temporal linkage interacts with
relative-clause structure. Together they support a grammar-facing claim
for controlled temporal subordination without forcing a full inventory
of all subordination outcomes.

The following example illustrates temporal subordination with the
clearest Old Testament anchor:

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:clause-ciangin-gen1p3}
\gll \tdimword{Pasian} \tdimword{ìn,} \tdimword{khuavak} \tdimword{ōm} \tdimword{hèn"} \tdimword{cí} \tdimword{hì;} \tdimword{túa} \tdimword{cìang-ìn} \tdimword{khuavak} \tdimword{ōm} \tdimword{pah} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     God \textsc{erg} light exist \textsc{juss} say \textsc{decl} \textsc{dist} then-\textsc{erg} light exist do.so \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{And God said, "Let there be light:" and there was light.} (Genesis 1:3)
\end{exe}

No equally clean Gospel example is currently used for this construction.

\subsubsection{Purposive or clause-bound irrealis boundary:
dingin}\label{purposive-or-clause-bound-irrealis-boundary-dingin}

\tdim{Dingin} \glossquote{in order to} is relevant to clause linkage
because it marks purposive or clause-bound irrealis use, but it remains
partly shared with the TAM system. At the current maturity level,
\tdim{dingin} is visible only as a caveated boundary row: it helps show
that clause linkage interacts with purposive and irrealis marking, but
it does not yet justify a broader clause-linkage or TAM rewrite.

The grammar claim stays cautious on purpose. The form \tdim{dingin} with
segmentation \tdim{ding-in} \glossquote{IRR-ERG} shows the same
\tdim{-in} \glossquote{ergative / agentive} suffix as temporal
\tdim{ciangin}, but the theoretical status of \tdim{-in} across these
contexts remains open.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:clause-dingin-gen1p14}
\gll \tdimword{Pasian} \tdimword{ìn,} \tdimword{sun} \tdimword{lè} \tdimword{zān} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{khen} \tdimword{dìng-ìn} \tdimword{vantung-àh} \tdimword{khuavak-tè} \tdimword{ōm} \tdimword{hèn;} \\
     God \textsc{erg} basket and break \textsc{3sg} divide \textsc{irr}-\textsc{erg} heaven-\textsc{loc} light-\textsc{pl} exist \textsc{juss} \\
\glt \glossquote{And God said, "Let there be lights in the firmament of the heaven to divide the day from the night..."} (Genesis 1:14)
\end{exe}

No equally clean Gospel example is currently used for this construction.

\subsubsection{Same-subject converb linkage boundary: VERB-in and
ngenin}\label{same-subject-converb-linkage-boundary-verb-in-and-ngenin}

Same-subject clause chaining is marked by the converb suffix \tdim{-in}
\glossquote{converbial / linking} on the first verb. The pattern is
notated as \tdim{VERB-in} \glossquote{converb} (e.g., \tdim{ngenin}
\glossquote{pray-CVB}), indicating that the subject of the linked clause
is identical to the subject of the following main clause. The form
\tdim{ngenin} \glossquote{pray-CVB} is the clearest anchor example.

This material remains boundary-only in the present account because the
exact status of \tdim{-in} as converb, subordinator, or broader linker
still needs clearer theoretical control. Treating same-subject chaining
as a settled part of clause linkage would require closing the
converb-versus-subordination question first.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:clause-ngenin-gen41p55}
\gll \tdimword{Egypt} \tdimword{gàm} \tdimword{khèmpeuh} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{kial} \tdimword{cìang-ìn,} \tdimword{àn} \tdimword{ngen-ìn} \tdimword{mì-tè} \tdimword{Faro'} \tdimword{tùngàh} \tdimword{kiko} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{egypt} land all \textsc{3sg} hunger then-\textsc{erg} \textsc{3pl}.\textsc{poss} net-\textsc{in} person-\textsc{pl} \textsc{faro}.\textsc{poss} on-\textsc{loc} cry.out 2/3PL \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{And when all the land of Egypt was famished, the people cried to Pharaoh for bread...} (Genesis 41:55)
\end{exe}

No equally clean Gospel example of same-subject converb construction is
currently used for this boundary row.

\subsubsection{Different-subject temporal linkage boundary: ahih
ciangin}\label{different-subject-temporal-linkage-boundary-ahih-ciangin}

Different-subject temporal linkage uses \tdim{ahih ciangin}
\glossquote{when / when it was} where a relative marker \tdim{ahih}
precedes the temporal subordinator \tdim{ciangin}. This construction
marks temporal clauses with different subjects from the main clause.

The construction is important structurally, but it remains boundary
material because different-subject linkage still overlaps with
relative-marker use and pronominal material. The form \tdim{ahih}
functions as a relative marker (glossed as a third-person plural or
relative form) rather than as a dedicated different-subject marker.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:clause-ahih-ciangin-gen1p21}
\gll \tdimword{Túa} \tdimword{àhìh} \tdimword{cìang-ìn} \tdimword{Pasian} \tdimword{ìn} \tdimword{tùi-pì} \tdimword{ganhing} \tdimword{gol-pì-tè} \tdimword{lè} \tdimword{àmau} \tdimword{nām} \tdimword{ciatin} \tdimword{tùi-pì} \tdimword{sùng-àh} \tdimword{tampi.} \\
     \textsc{dist} be.\textsc{3sg}.\textsc{rel} then-\textsc{erg} God \textsc{erg} water-big animal creature-great-\textsc{pl} and \textsc{3pl}.\textsc{pro} nation each.kind.\textsc{erg} water-big inside-\textsc{loc} many \\
\glt \glossquote{And God created great whales, and every living creature that moveth, which the waters brought forth...} (Genesis 1:21)
\end{exe}

No equally clean Gospel example of different-subject temporal linkage
with \tdim{ahih ciangin} is currently used for this construction.

\subsubsection{Prenominal relative-clause boundary: a bawl
mi}\label{prenominal-relative-clause-boundary-a-bawl-mi}

The prenominal relative clause uses the relativizer prefix \tdim{a-} to
mark a relative verb, with the head noun following. The form
\tdim{a bawl mi} \glossquote{person who makes} is the clearest anchor,
showing how relative clauses with human head nouns are formed.

This material remains boundary-only because relative clauses still
interact with prefix/agreement and nominalization material. Settling
relative-clause structure requires clearer boundary control with the
prefix system.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:clause-abawlmi-exodus30p38}
\gll \tdimword{pāk-nam-tui} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{bāwl} \tdimword{mì} \tdimword{peuhmah} \\
     ointment \textsc{3sg} make person whosoever \\
\glt \glossquote{whoever makes any like it} (Exodus 30:38)
\end{exe}

No equally clean Gospel example is currently used for this construction.

\subsubsection{Nominalized relative and clause-like form boundary:
omna}\label{nominalized-relative-and-clause-like-form-boundary-omna}

The form \tdim{omna} \glossquote{place / being / existence} is a
nominalized relative or clause-derived form. It appears in patterns like
\tdim{omna mun} where it marks a location or predication related to a
nominalized clause.

This material belongs to the overlap between nominalization and
relative-clause structure. Settling \tdim{omna} as a nominalized
relative would require fuller boundary control with the nominalization
section, which is why it remains explicitly boundary material here.

\subsubsection{Nominalization-plus-case boundary:
muhna-ah}\label{nominalization-plus-case-boundary-muhna-ah}

Forms such as \tdim{muhna-ah} \glossquote{in seeing / in the sight of}
combine nominalization with locative case-like morphology. The form
shows nominalizer \tdim{-na} \glossquote{nominalizer} plus locative
\tdim{-ah} \glossquote{locative / goal-like}, creating sight-expressions
used in temporal or spatial adjuncts.

This material is structurally mixed because it belongs partly with case
marking and partly with clause linkage. For that reason, this section
treats it as boundary evidence rather than as a core clause-linkage
subtype.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:clause-muhnaah-gen6p11}
\gll \tdimword{Tù-ìn} \tdimword{Pasian'} \tdimword{mùh-ná-àh} \tdimword{léi-tùng} \tdimword{sía} \tdimword{à.} \\
     now-\textsc{erg} God.\textsc{poss} see.\textsc{ii}-\textsc{nmlz}-\textsc{loc} land-on evil \textsc{3sg} \\
\glt \glossquote{Now the earth was corrupt in the sight of God.} (Genesis 6:11)
\end{exe}

A Gospel parallel shows the same nominalization-plus-case boundary in a
different context:

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:clause-muhnaah-luke19p27}
\gll \tdimword{kèima} \tdimword{mùh-ná-àh} \tdimword{thàt} \tdimword{ùn.} \\
     \textsc{1sg}.self see.\textsc{ii}-\textsc{nmlz}-\textsc{loc} kill \textsc{imp}.\textsc{pl} \\
\glt \glossquote{kill them before me.} (Luke 19:27)
\end{exe}

\subsubsection{Deferred and boundary
material}\label{deferred-and-boundary-material-15}

Several issues remain outside the present account.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \tdim{ciangin} and \tdim{tua ciangin} support a controlled temporal
  subordination core, but they do not by themselves settle the full
  clause-linkage paradigm or resolve whether subordinators form a
  unified system.
\item
  \tdim{dingin} remains a boundary row until the irrealis and purposive
  layers are clearer.
\item
  \tdim{VERB-in} and \tdim{ngenin} remain boundary material until the
  converb-versus-subordination distinction is theoretically settled.
\item
  \tdim{ahih ciangin} \glossquote{when / when it was} remains a boundary
  form shared with relative-marker and pronominal material.
\item
  \tdim{a bawl mi} and \tdim{omna} remain boundary forms shared with
  relative-clause and nominalization structure.
\item
  \tdim{muhna-ah} remains a boundary form shared with case marking and
  nominalization.
\item
  \tdim{leh} \glossquote{if / when / conditional} remains outside
  because sentence-final-particle, coordinator, and subordinator
  analyses still overlap.
\item
  \tdim{hangin} \glossquote{because / causal} and \tdim{bangin}
  \glossquote{like / as / comparative} remain broader report-inventory
  rows rather than the first safe clause-linkage anchors.
\item
  full switch-reference system claims remain deferred.
\item
  full relative-clause system claims remain deferred.
\item
  broader discourse-level linkage remains outside this controlled
  sentence-level slice.
\item
  Raw occurrence counts are not treated as grammar facts without
  constructional control.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Summary}\label{summary-17}

The safest grammar-facing conclusion is that Tedim has controlled
evidence for temporal subordination with \tdim{ciangin}
\glossquote{when}, with \tdim{tua ciangin} \glossquote{then / when} as a
compact anchor showing clause-final temporal marking. Purposive material
with \tdim{dingin}, same-subject converb construction with
\tdim{ngenin}, different-subject marking with \tdim{ahih ciangin},
relative clauses with \tdim{a bawl mi}, nominalized relatives with
\tdim{omna}, and nominalization-plus-case forms with \tdim{muhna-ah}
remain explicit boundaries so the section stays controlled.

\subsection{Same-subject and different-subject clause
linkage}\label{same-subject-and-different-subject-clause-linkage}

The current clause-linkage evidence is narrow: same-subject
\tdim{bawlin} \glossquote{make-CVB} and \tdim{semin}
\glossquote{serve-CVB} are secure, \tdim{bawlin} is printed as a trimmed
excerpt because Genesis 11:4 is crowded with boundary \tdim{-in} tokens,
\tdim{semin} is kept in full because the Deuteronomy chain is already
compact, the report-level \tdim{VERB-in} \glossquote{converb} label is
only a comparison term, \tdim{ahih ciangin}
\glossquote{when / when it was} is support only, and ordinary
\tdim{ciangin} \glossquote{when}, \tdim{dingin}
\glossquote{in order to / clause-bound irrealis}, \tdim{ngenin}
\glossquote{pray-CVB}, \tdim{a bawl mi} \glossquote{person who},
\tdim{omna} \glossquote{place / being / existence}, and \tdim{muhna-ah}
\glossquote{in seeing / in the sight} stay boundary-controlled. No
equally clean Gospel example is currently used for this construction.

\subsubsection{Overview of the current
evidence}\label{overview-of-the-current-evidence}

The report calls this domain switch reference, but the safer
grammar-facing label is same-subject and different-subject clause
linkage. The secure core is same-subject converb-like linkage;
\tdim{ahih ciangin} remains support only; ordinary \tdim{ciangin},
purposive \tdim{dingin}, blocked \tdim{ngenin}, and relative /
nominalized material stay boundary-controlled. No full switch-reference
system is claimed.

\paragraph{Promoted same-subject
evidence}\label{promoted-same-subject-evidence}

\tdim{bawlin} \glossquote{make-CVB} is the clearest same-subject anchor
because the same \tdim{eite} \glossquote{we.INCL} controller continues
into the following \tdim{bawl ni} clause. The verse is trimmed for
display so the target \tdim{bawl-in} is not buried under other boundary
\tdim{-in} tokens.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:sr-bawlin-gen11p4}
\reviewwarninginline{[REVIEW PREVIEW WARNING: ex:sr-bawlin-gen11p4: Bible reference mapped, but slice example is not a direct verse span; analyzed slice wording]}
\gll \tdimword{.} \tdimword{khùa-pì} \tdimword{khàt} \tdimword{bāwl-ìn.} \tdimword{eite'} \tdimword{minthan} \tdimword{nàdìng} \tdimword{khàt} \tdimword{bāwl} \tdimword{nì.} \\
      village-big one make-\textsc{cvb} \textsc{1pl}.\textsc{pro}.\textsc{poss} bless \textsc{purp} one make day \\
\glt \glossquote{... let us build us a city... and let us make us a name...} (Genesis 11:4)
\end{exe}

The full verse contains nearby boundary \tdim{-in} tokens
(\tdim{amaute in}, \tdim{nadingin}, \tdim{a dawn in}, \tdim{lamin}), but
they are not the target converb.

\tdim{semin} \glossquote{serve-CVB} is the other secure same-subject
anchor. The current verse keeps the same \tdim{note} controller across
the clause chain, and \tdim{beel-in} is boundary material rather than a
second converb anchor.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:sr-semin-deut10p20}
\gll \tdimword{Topa} \tdimword{nòtè'} \tdimword{Pasian} \tdimword{nà} \tdimword{zah-tàk} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{à,} \tdimword{àmà} \tdimword{nà} \tdimword{sèm-ìn,} \tdimword{àmàh} \tdimword{beel-ìn,} \tdimword{àmà} \tdimword{mīn} \tdimword{tàwh} \tdimword{nà} \tdimword{ki-ciam} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     Lord \textsc{2pl}.\textsc{pro}.\textsc{poss} God \textsc{2sg} fear-true \textsc{irr} 2/3PL \textsc{3sg} \textsc{3sg}.\textsc{poss} \textsc{2sg} serve-\textsc{cvb} \textsc{3sg}.\textsc{pro} pan-\textsc{erg} \textsc{3sg}.\textsc{poss} name \textsc{com} \textsc{2sg} covenant \textsc{irr} 2/3PL \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{Thou shalt fear the LORD thy God; him shalt thou serve, and to him shalt thou cleave, and swear by his name.} (Deuteronomy 10:20)
\end{exe}

The person glossing stays \tdim{2SG} for \tdim{na} to match the
prefix/agreement slice, and the verse is kept in full because the
same-subject chain is already compact enough to read cleanly.

No equally clean Gospel example is currently used for this construction.

\paragraph{Support-only and boundary
material}\label{support-only-and-boundary-material}

\tdim{ahih ciangin} stays support only. It is source-resolved, but the
construction is temporally framed and does not independently prove a
dedicated different-subject marker.

Ordinary \tdim{ciangin} remains the temporal subordinator; \tdim{dingin}
remains purposive / clause-bound irrealis material; \tdim{ngenin} stays
blocked because the analyzer export splits \tdim{net-IN} and
\tdim{pray-CVB}. Relative-clause and nominalization material
(\tdim{a bawl mi}, \tdim{omna}, \tdim{muhna-ah}) stays outside the core
subject-tracking claim.

\paragraph{Deferred material}\label{deferred-material-7}

This section does not claim a full switch-reference system, full
subordination chapter, full relative-clause chapter, full converb
chapter, or full discourse system.

\reviewchapterbreak

\section{Clause type, discourse-facing material, and expressive
morphology}\label{clause-type-discourse-facing-material-and-expressive-morphology}

\subsection{Negation}\label{negation}

The current negation evidence supports a three-way core around \tdim{lo}
\glossquote{NEG} for ordinary clause negation, \tdim{loh}
\glossquote{dependent / derived NEG} in dependent environments, and
\tdim{kei} \glossquote{NEG / prohibitive NEG} in prohibitives and
irrealis-heavy negative contexts.

\subsubsection{Overview of negation in
Tedim}\label{overview-of-negation-in-tedim}

Tedim negation is not reducible to \tdim{lo} \glossquote{NEG} alone. The
current controlled evidence supports at least three major zones:
ordinary clause-level negation with \tdim{lo} \glossquote{NEG},
dependent or derived negation with \tdim{loh}
\glossquote{dependent / derived NEG}, and \tdim{kei}
\glossquote{NEG / prohibitive NEG} in prohibitives and other
irrealis-heavy negative environments
\citep{henderson1965, zamngaihcing2017}.

\subsubsection{Negation inventory}\label{negation-inventory}

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{Clause-level negation with
\tdim{lo}}{Clause-level negation with }}\label{clause-level-negation-with}

\tdim{Lo} \glossquote{NEG} is the safest ordinary clause-level negator,
and \tdim{lo hi} \glossquote{NEG DECL} is common in straightforward
predicate negation.

Genesis 4:5 gives a compact clause-level OT anchor.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:neg-lo}
\gll \tdimword{Kain} \tdimword{lè} \tdimword{àmà} \tdimword{pìak-ná} \tdimword{thusim} \tdimword{lò} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{kain} and \textsc{3sg}.\textsc{poss} give.to-\textsc{nmlz} parable \textsc{neg} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{he had not respect for Cain and his offering.} (Genesis 4:5)
\end{exe}

Matthew 2:10 gives a Gospel comparandum in the same clause-level zone.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:neg-lo-matt2-10}
\gll \tdimword{Túa} \tdimword{aksi} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{mùh} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{cìang-ìn} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{lungdam-ná} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{ki-gēn} \tdimword{théi} \tdimword{lò} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{dist} star \textsc{3sg} see.\textsc{ii} 2/3PL then-\textsc{erg} \textsc{3sg} rejoice-\textsc{nmlz} 2/3PL conversation know \textsc{neg} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{their joy could not be told.} (Matthew 2:10)
\end{exe}

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{Dependent and derived negation with
\tdim{loh}}{Dependent and derived negation with }}\label{dependent-and-derived-negation-with}

\tdim{Loh} \glossquote{dependent / derived NEG} is distinct from
ordinary \tdim{lo}, and the pattern \tdim{loh ding-a}
\glossquote{NEG.DEP IRR-LOC/FUNC} and related purposive shapes are
central in dependent negation.

Genesis 3:11 gives the OT dependent-negation anchor.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:neg-loh}
\reviewwarninginline{[REVIEW PREVIEW WARNING: ex:neg-loh: Bible reference mapped, but slice example is not a direct verse span; analyzed slice wording]}
\gll \tdimword{Nà} \tdimword{nèk} \tdimword{lòh} \tdimword{dìng-à} \tdimword{kòng} \tdimword{thupiak} \tdimword{singgah} \tdimword{né} \tdimword{khá} \tdimword{nà} \tdimword{hì} \tdimword{hìam?} \\
     \textsc{2sg} eat.\textsc{ii} \textsc{neg}-\textsc{nom} \textsc{irr}-\textsc{loc} \textsc{1sg→3} commandment hang eat \textsc{neg}.\textsc{perf} \textsc{2sg} \textsc{decl} \textsc{q} \\
\glt \glossquote{that thou shouldest not eat?} (Genesis 3:11)
\end{exe}

Matthew 5:19 gives a Gospel dependent-negation comparandum.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:neg-loh-matt5-19}
\reviewwarninginline{[REVIEW PREVIEW WARNING: ex:neg-loh-matt5-19: Bible reference mapped, but slice example is not a direct verse span; analyzed slice wording]}
\gll \tdimword{Midang-tè} \tdimword{ìn} \tdimword{zòng} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{zùih} \tdimword{lòh} \tdimword{nàdìngìn} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{gēn} \tdimword{mì-tè} \tdimword{pèn} \tdimword{mì} \tdimword{néu-pèn} \tdimword{hì} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     other.person-\textsc{pl} \textsc{erg} also \textsc{3sg} follow-\textsc{nom} \textsc{neg}-\textsc{nom} \textsc{purp}.\textsc{erg} \textsc{3sg} speak person-\textsc{pl} \textsc{top} person small-\textsc{top} \textsc{decl} \textsc{irr} 2/3PL \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{those who teach others not to keep them will be called least.} (Matthew 5:19)
\end{exe}

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\tdim{kei} in prohibitives and
irrealis-heavy
negation}{ in prohibitives and irrealis-heavy negation}}\label{in-prohibitives-and-irrealis-heavy-negation}

\tdim{Kei} \glossquote{NEG / prohibitive NEG} is central in
prohibitives, especially \tdim{kei in} \glossquote{NEG IMP.SG} and
\tdim{kei un} \glossquote{NEG IMP.PL}, and it also appears in broader
irrealis-heavy negative environments.

Genesis 22:12 gives the core OT prohibitive anchor.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:neg-kei}
\gll \tdimword{Tangval-pà} \tdimword{sù} \tdimword{kèi} \tdimword{ìn.} \tdimword{Àmà} \tdimword{tùngàh} \tdimword{bangmah} \tdimword{híh} \tdimword{kèi} \tdimword{ìn.} \\
     youth-father tear \textsc{neg} \textsc{erg} \textsc{3sg}.\textsc{poss} on-\textsc{loc} nothing \textsc{prox} \textsc{neg} \textsc{erg} \\
\glt \glossquote{Do not strike the boy; do not do anything to him.} (Genesis 22:12)
\end{exe}

Mark 1:25 gives a compact Gospel prohibitive comparandum.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:neg-kei-mark1-25}
\gll \tdimword{Pau} \tdimword{kèi} \tdimword{ìn;} \tdimword{híh} \tdimword{mipa} \tdimword{sùng} \tdimword{pàn-ìn} \tdimword{pái-khìa} \tdimword{ìn.} \\
     \textsc{pau} \textsc{neg} \textsc{erg} \textsc{prox} man inside \textsc{abl}-\textsc{erg} go-exit \textsc{erg} \\
\glt \glossquote{Be silent, and come out of him.} (Mark 1:25)
\end{exe}

\subsubsection{Ordinary plural negative predicates are not automatically
prohibitive}\label{ordinary-plural-negative-predicates-are-not-automatically-prohibitive}

The pattern \tdim{V lo uh} is not automatically a prohibitive. Genesis
2:25 \tdim{maizum lo uh hi} \glossquote{they were not ashamed} is an
ordinary declarative plural negative predicate, not a directive, and
Gospel clauses such as Matthew 13:17 \tdim{mu lo uh} show the same
non-prohibitive negative profile.

\subsubsection{Negative existence and
absence}\label{negative-existence-and-absence}

Negative existence and absence are commonly expressed with
\tdim{om lo hi} \glossquote{not exist / be absent} and \tdim{nei lo hi}
\glossquote{not have}.

Genesis 37:24 gives an OT existential-negation anchor.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:neg-om-lo-gen37-24}
\reviewwarninginline{[REVIEW PREVIEW WARNING: ex:neg-om-lo-gen37-24: Bible reference mapped, but slice example is not a direct verse span; analyzed slice wording]}
\gll \tdimword{Khu} \tdimword{sùng-àh} \tdimword{tùi} \tdimword{ōm} \tdimword{lò} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     smoke inside-\textsc{loc} water exist \textsc{neg} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{there was no water in the pit.} (Genesis 37:24)
\end{exe}

John 14:30 gives a Gospel possession-negation comparandum.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:neg-nei-lo-john14-30}
\gll \tdimword{Àmàh} \tdimword{ìn} \tdimword{kèima} \tdimword{tùngàh} \tdimword{à-ana} \tdimword{néi} \tdimword{lò} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{3sg}.\textsc{pro} \textsc{erg} \textsc{1sg}.self on-\textsc{loc} \textsc{3sg}-child.\textsc{pl} have \textsc{neg} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{he has no claim on me.} (John 14:30)
\end{exe}

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{Cessative
\tdim{nawn lo}}{Cessative }}\label{cessative}

\tdim{Nawn lo} \glossquote{no longer / not again} is best treated as a
constructional negation pattern, not as a separate basic negator.

Genesis 8:12 gives the OT cessative anchor.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:neg-nawn-lo-gen8-12}
\reviewwarninginline{[REVIEW PREVIEW WARNING: ex:neg-nawn-lo-gen8-12: Bible reference mapped, but slice example is not a direct verse span; analyzed slice wording]}
\gll \tdimword{Túa} \tdimword{vachuang} \tdimword{hòng} \tdimword{ciah-kík} \tdimword{nàwn} \tdimword{lò} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{dist} ? \textsc{3→1} return-\textsc{iter} \textsc{cont} \textsc{neg} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{the dove did not return again.} (Genesis 8:12)
\end{exe}

Matthew 28:6 gives a Gospel cessative comparandum.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:neg-nawn-lo-matt28-6}
\gll \tdimword{Àmàh} \tdimword{ōm} \tdimword{nàwn} \tdimword{lò} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{3sg}.\textsc{pro} exist \textsc{cont} \textsc{neg} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{he is no longer here.} (Matthew 28:6)
\end{exe}

\subsubsection{Ability and inability}\label{ability-and-inability}

The ability pair \tdim{thei lo} \glossquote{cannot / not know} and
\tdim{theih loh} \glossquote{not being able / dependent inability} is a
productive part of negation, and the dependent form should not be
collapsed into ordinary clause-level \tdim{thei lo}.

Genesis 27:23 gives a compact OT ability-negation anchor.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:neg-thei-lo}
\gll \tdimword{Àmàh} \tdimword{ìn} \tdimword{théi} \tdimword{lò} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     \textsc{3sg}.\textsc{pro} \textsc{erg} know \textsc{neg} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{he did not recognize him.} (Genesis 27:23)
\end{exe}

John 9:4 gives a Gospel dependent-ability comparandum.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:neg-theih-loh-john9-4}
\reviewwarninginline{[REVIEW PREVIEW WARNING: ex:neg-theih-loh-john9-4: Bible reference mapped, but slice example is not a direct verse span; analyzed slice wording]}
\gll \tdimword{Kuamah} \tdimword{ìn} \tdimword{nà} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{sep} \tdimword{thèih} \tdimword{lòh} \tdimword{zān} \tdimword{hùn} \tdimword{hòng} \tdimword{tùng} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     nobody \textsc{erg} \textsc{2sg} \textsc{3sg} work know.\textsc{ii} \textsc{neg}-\textsc{nom} break time \textsc{3→1} on \textsc{irr} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{night is coming when no one can work.} (John 9:4)
\end{exe}

\subsubsection{Negative polarity items}\label{negative-polarity-items}

The forms \tdim{kuamah} \glossquote{nobody} and \tdim{bangmah}
\glossquote{nothing} are included here only with filtered, manually
checked negative-licensing contexts; raw exact-string counts
overgenerate, and \tdim{bangmah} has non-NPI uses such as
\tdim{tua bangmah hi-in} \glossquote{likewise / in that way}.

Exodus 2:12 gives a filtered OT \tdim{kuamah} example.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:neg-kuamah}
\gll \tdimword{Kuamah} \tdimword{mú} \tdimword{lò} \tdimword{àhìh} \tdimword{màn-ìn} \tdimword{Egypt} \tdimword{mipa} \tdimword{thàt-ìn} \tdimword{sehnel} \tdimword{sùng-àh} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{seel} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     nobody see.\textsc{i} \textsc{neg} be.\textsc{3sg}.\textsc{rel} reason-\textsc{erg} \textsc{egypt} man kill-\textsc{cvb} wilderness inside-\textsc{loc} \textsc{3sg} hide \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{he saw that there was no man, and hid the Egyptian in the sand.} (Exodus 2:12)
\end{exe}

Matthew 22:46 gives a filtered Gospel \tdim{kuamah} comparandum.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:neg-kuamah-matt22-46}
\gll \tdimword{Kuamah} \tdimword{ìn} \tdimword{àmà} \tdimword{thù-dot-ná} \tdimword{kam-khàt} \tdimword{beek} \tdimword{dawng} \tdimword{théi} \tdimword{lò} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{hì.} \\
     nobody \textsc{erg} \textsc{3sg}.\textsc{poss} word-ask-\textsc{nmlz} word-one only receive know \textsc{neg} 2/3PL \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{no one could answer him a word.} (Matthew 22:46)
\end{exe}

\subsubsection{Deferred material}\label{deferred-material-8}

Several issues remain outside the present account. The present section
does not reopen a full TAM chapter, a full clause-linkage chapter, a
full imperative-mood chapter, or a full polarity-licensing typology; it
only marks the boundaries needed to keep \tdim{lo}, \tdim{loh},
\tdim{kei}, negative existence, ability-negation, and filtered NPIs in a
coherent grammar-facing description.

\subsection{Interrogatives}\label{interrogatives}

The current interrogative evidence is strongest for clause-final
\tdim{hiam} \glossquote{question particle} and WH + \tdim{hiam} content
questions, with core \tdim{bang} \glossquote{what}, \tdim{kua}
\glossquote{who}, \tdim{bangci} \glossquote{how}, and \tdim{banghangin}
\glossquote{why}, while \tdim{bang hiam cih}
\glossquote{what ... saying}, \tdim{bangmah} \glossquote{nothing},
\tdim{bangin} \glossquote{as / how}, and the comparison-particle forms
\tdim{maw} \glossquote{question/comparison particle}, \tdim{ham}
\glossquote{question/comparison particle}, and \tdim{em}
\glossquote{question/comparison particle} stay at the boundary.

\subsubsection{Overview of interrogatives in
Tedim}\label{overview-of-interrogatives-in-tedim}

This section treats clause-final \tdim{hiam}
\glossquote{question particle} and selected WH + \tdim{hiam} content
questions. It keeps \tdim{bang} \glossquote{what}, \tdim{kua}
\glossquote{who}, \tdim{bangci} \glossquote{how}, and \tdim{banghangin}
\glossquote{why} at the core, while \tdim{bang hiam cih}
\glossquote{what... saying}, \tdim{bangmah} \glossquote{nothing},
\tdim{bangin} \glossquote{as / how}, and the comparison-particle forms
\tdim{maw} \glossquote{question/comparison particle}, \tdim{ham}
\glossquote{question/comparison particle}, and \tdim{em}
\glossquote{question/comparison particle} stay at the boundary.

The inventory below keeps the set compact. Raw counts are not treated as
grammar facts here.

\paragraph{Interrogative inventory}\label{interrogative-inventory}

\paragraph{\texorpdfstring{Clause-final \tdim{hiam}
\glossquote{question particle}}{Clause-final  }}\label{clause-final}

Clause-final \tdim{hiam} is the safest yes/no question marker in the
current evidence. It also appears with some WH questions, but the
independent-clause pattern is the clearest place to start.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:int-hiam-gen24-23}
\reviewwarninginline{[REVIEW PREVIEW WARNING: ex:int-hiam-gen24-23: Bible reference mapped, but slice example is not a direct verse span; analyzed slice wording]}
\gll \tdimword{Nà} \tdimword{pà} \tdimword{ínn-àh} \tdimword{kòtè} \tdimword{giah} \tdimword{nàdìng} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{áwng} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{hìam} \\
     \textsc{2sg} father house-\textsc{loc} \textsc{1pl}.\textsc{pro} camp \textsc{purp} \textsc{3sg} open \textsc{irr} \textsc{q} \\
\glt \glossquote{Is there room in your father's house for us to stay?} (Genesis 24:23)
\end{exe}

Matthew 6:25 gives a Gospel yes/no question with the same clause-final
\tdim{hiam} pattern, so the distribution is not confined to a single
narrative window.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:int-hiam-matt6-25}
\gll \tdimword{Nuntak-ná} \tdimword{pèn} \tdimword{àn} \tdimword{sàng-ìn} \tdimword{thù-pì-zàw} \tdimword{à,} \tdimword{pumpi} \tdimword{pèn} \tdimword{pùan} \tdimword{sàng-ìn} \tdimword{thù-pì-zàw} \tdimword{hì} \tdimword{lò} \tdimword{àhì} \tdimword{hìam?} \\
     live-\textsc{nmlz} \textsc{top} \textsc{3pl}.\textsc{poss} high-\textsc{erg} word-big-more \textsc{3sg} body \textsc{top} cloth high-\textsc{erg} word-big-more \textsc{decl} \textsc{neg} be.\textsc{3sg} \textsc{q} \\
\glt \glossquote{Is not life more important than food, and the body more important than clothing?} (Matthew 6:25)
\end{exe}

\paragraph{\texorpdfstring{WH + \tdim{hiam} content
questions}{WH +  content questions}}\label{wh-content-questions}

The content-question pattern is WH + \tdim{hiam}. The forms below are
the current core WH inventory in this section. \tdim{Bang}
\glossquote{what} and \tdim{kua} \glossquote{who} are the simplest
members of the set; \tdim{bangci} \glossquote{how} and \tdim{banghangin}
\glossquote{why} show that the same question marker can attach to
different semantic relations.

\subparagraph{\texorpdfstring{\tdim{bang}
\glossquote{what}}{ }}\label{section}

\tdim{Bang} is the clearest what-question word. It can appear in a very
short clause or inside a longer question frame.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:int-bang-exod16-15}
\gll \tdimword{Bàng} \tdimword{àhì} \tdimword{hìam?} \\
     like be.\textsc{3sg} \textsc{q} \\
\glt \glossquote{What is it?} (Exodus 16:15)
\end{exe}

Matthew 6:31 shows \tdim{bang} in a longer Gospel question frame.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:int-bang-matt6-31}
\gll \tdimword{Bàng} \tdimword{né} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{à,} \tdimword{bàng} \tdimword{dawn} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{à,} \tdimword{bàng} \tdimword{silh} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{ì-hí} \tdimword{hìam?} \\
     like eat \textsc{irr} \textsc{3sg} like top \textsc{irr} \textsc{3sg} like clothe \textsc{irr} \textsc{1pl}.\textsc{incl}-be \textsc{q} \\
\glt \glossquote{What shall we eat, what shall we drink, and what shall we wear?} (Matthew 6:31)
\end{exe}

\subparagraph{\texorpdfstring{\tdim{kua}
\glossquote{who}}{ }}\label{section-1}

\tdim{Kua} is the core who-question word. The analyzer sometimes tags it
as \tdim{NUM}, but that export label does not change the clause-level
reading.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:int-kua-gen48-8}
\gll \tdimword{Híh-tè} \tdimword{kua} \tdimword{àhì} \tdimword{hìam?} \\
     \textsc{prox}-\textsc{pl} who be.\textsc{3sg} \textsc{q} \\
\glt \glossquote{Who are these?} (Genesis 48:8)
\end{exe}

Matthew 16:15 gives a Gospel counterpart with the same question word.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:int-kua-matt16-15}
\gll \tdimword{Túa} \tdimword{ahihleh} \tdimword{nòtè} \tdimword{ìn} \tdimword{kèi} \tdimword{kua} \tdimword{hì} \tdimword{hòng} \tdimword{cí} \tdimword{nà} \tdimword{hì} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{hìam?} \\
     \textsc{dist} if \textsc{2pl}.\textsc{pro} \textsc{erg} \textsc{neg} who \textsc{decl} \textsc{3→1} say \textsc{2sg} \textsc{decl} 2/3PL \textsc{q} \\
\glt \glossquote{But who do you say that I am?} (Matthew 16:15)
\end{exe}

\subparagraph{\texorpdfstring{\tdim{bangci}
\glossquote{how}}{ }}\label{section-2}

\tdim{Bangci} stays visible as \tdim{bangci} rather than being flattened
into generic \tdim{bang}. It is the clearest manner-question item in the
current evidence.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:int-bangci-gen3-13}
\gll \tdimword{Bangci} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{hi-cí} \tdimword{gàm-tat} \tdimword{nà} \tdimword{hì} \tdimword{hìam?} \\
     how \textsc{3sg} \textsc{prox}-say land-rule \textsc{2sg} \textsc{decl} \textsc{q} \\
\glt \glossquote{How have you acted thus?} (Genesis 3:13)
\end{exe}

Matthew 12:26 keeps the same \tdim{bangci} pattern visible in Gospel
material.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:int-bangci-matt12-26}
\gll \tdimword{Túa} \tdimword{màh} \tdimword{bàng-ìn} \tdimword{Satan} \tdimword{lè} \tdimword{Satan} \tdimword{kī-hawlkhia} \tdimword{lè-ùh} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{ukna} \tdimword{bangci} \tdimword{kip} \tdimword{thèih} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{àhì} \tdimword{hìam?} \\
     \textsc{dist} \textsc{emph} like-\textsc{erg} \textsc{satan} and \textsc{satan} \textsc{refl}-drive.out and-\textsc{pl}.\textsc{imp} \textsc{3sg} rule.\textsc{nmlz} how firm know.\textsc{ii} \textsc{irr} be.\textsc{3sg} \textsc{q} \\
\glt \glossquote{How then can Satan cast out Satan?} (Matthew 12:26)
\end{exe}

\subparagraph{\texorpdfstring{\tdim{banghangin}
\glossquote{why}}{ }}\label{section-3}

\tdim{Banghangin} remains a reason-question item. The segmented form is
slightly noisy, but the clause-level reading is still clear.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:int-banghangin-gen4-6}
\gll \tdimword{bàng} \tdimword{hàng-ìn} \tdimword{nà} \tdimword{mái} \tdimword{sía} \tdimword{àhì} \tdimword{hìam} \\
     like because-\textsc{erg} \textsc{2sg} face evil be.\textsc{3sg} \textsc{q} \\
\glt \glossquote{Why is your countenance fallen?} (Genesis 4:6)
\end{exe}

Matthew 6:28 is a Gospel counterpart with the same reason-question
pattern.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:int-banghangin-matt6-28}
\gll \tdimword{Bàng} \tdimword{hàng-ìn} \tdimword{pùan-silh} \tdimword{dìng} \tdimword{lùng-himawh} \tdimword{nà} \tdimword{hì} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{hìam?} \\
     like because-\textsc{erg} cloth-wrap \textsc{irr} heart-fear \textsc{2sg} \textsc{decl} 2/3PL \textsc{q} \\
\glt \glossquote{Why are you anxious about clothing?} (Matthew 6:28)
\end{exe}

\paragraph{Embedded-question boundary}\label{embedded-question-boundary}

\tdim{Bang hiam cih} \glossquote{what... saying} belongs at the
embedded-question boundary, not in the independent-clause pattern. The
current evidence shows this most clearly where \tdim{thei lo}
\glossquote{do not know} governs the embedded question.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:int-embedded-exod16-15}
\gll \tdimword{bàng} \tdimword{hìam} \tdimword{cìh} \tdimword{théi} \tdimword{lò} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{hì} \\
     like \textsc{q} say.\textsc{nom} know \textsc{neg} 2/3PL \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{They did not know what it was.} (Exodus 16:15)
\end{exe}

Luke 23:34 shows the same boundary in a Gospel frame.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:int-embedded-luke23-34}
\gll \tdimword{Pà} \tdimword{àw,} \tdimword{àmaute} \tdimword{ìn} \tdimword{bàng} \tdimword{híh} \tdimword{ì-hí} \tdimword{hìam} \tdimword{cìh} \tdimword{théi} \tdimword{lò} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{àhìh} \tdimword{màn-ìn} \tdimword{à} \tdimword{màwh-ná} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{nà} \tdimword{mái-sàk} \tdimword{ìn} \\
     father voice \textsc{3pl}.\textsc{pro} \textsc{erg} like \textsc{prox} \textsc{1pl}.\textsc{incl}-be \textsc{q} say.\textsc{nom} know \textsc{neg} 2/3PL be.\textsc{3sg}.\textsc{rel} reason-\textsc{erg} \textsc{3sg} err-\textsc{nmlz} 2/3PL \textsc{2sg} face-\textsc{caus} \textsc{erg} \\
\glt \glossquote{Father, forgive them, for they know not what they are doing.} (Luke 23:34)
\end{exe}

\paragraph{\texorpdfstring{Blocked false friends and non-interrogative
\tdim{hiam}}{Blocked false friends and non-interrogative }}\label{blocked-false-friends-and-non-interrogative}

\tdim{Bang hang hiam cih leh} \glossquote{because / for this reason} is
a formulaic reason frame, not an ordinary question.
\tdim{Langnih a hiam namsau} \glossquote{two-edged sword} (Revelation
1:16) and \tdim{a hiam ciat uh} \glossquote{each of them} (2 Kings
11:11) are lexical \tdim{hiam} controls. \tdim{Bangmah}
\glossquote{nothing} (Genesis 9:21) and \tdim{bangin}
\glossquote{as / how} (Genesis 1:7; Exodus 14:30) are boundary forms
rather than core interrogatives.

\paragraph{Deferred comparison
particles}\label{deferred-comparison-particles}

\tdim{Maw} \glossquote{question/comparison particle}, \tdim{ham}
\glossquote{question/comparison particle}, and \tdim{em}
\glossquote{question/comparison particle} remain deferred. The current
evidence is enough to keep them visible, but not enough to build a
separate comparison-particle account.

\paragraph{Deferred material}\label{deferred-material-9}

Several issues remain outside the present account.

\subsection{Sentence-final particles}\label{sentence-final-particles}

The current sentence-final evidence is strongest for controlled
clause-final uses of \tdim{ahi hi} \glossquote{be.3SG DECL / it was},
\tdim{lo hi} \glossquote{NEG DECL}, \tdim{hen}
\glossquote{jussive / optative}, \tdim{in}
\glossquote{imperative / second-person singular imperative boundary},
and \tdim{un} \glossquote{plural imperative}, while \tdim{hiam}
\glossquote{question particle}, \tdim{aw}
\glossquote{vocative / exclamative boundary}, \tdim{tahen}
\glossquote{deferred jussive-looking form}, \tdim{ta}
\glossquote{perfective / change-of-state boundary}, and \tdim{zo}
\glossquote{completive boundary} remain overlap or deferred material.

\subsubsection{Overview of sentence-final particles in
Tedim}\label{overview-of-sentence-final-particles-in-tedim}

This section treats a small controlled set of sentence-final or
clause-final material: \tdim{hi} \glossquote{declarative}, \tdim{ahi hi}
\glossquote{be.3SG DECL / it was}, \tdim{lo hi} \glossquote{NEG DECL},
\tdim{hen} \glossquote{jussive / optative}, \tdim{in}
\glossquote{imperative / second-person singular imperative boundary},
and \tdim{un} \glossquote{plural imperative}. It keeps \tdim{hiam}
\glossquote{question particle}, \tdim{aw}
\glossquote{vocative / exclamative boundary}, \tdim{tahen}
\glossquote{deferred jussive-looking form}, \tdim{ta}
\glossquote{perfective / change-of-state boundary}, and \tdim{zo}
\glossquote{completive boundary} as overlap or deferred material. The
section does not present the whole mood, aspect, TAM, negation, or
interrogative system.

\subsubsection{Sentence-final particle
inventory}\label{sentence-final-particle-inventory}

  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}

  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}@{}}

\tdim{hi} & declarative & Genesis 1:13; Matthew 1:1 & supported with
caveat & discussed through \tdim{ahi hi}, not as unrestricted bare
\tdim{hi} \\
\tdim{ahi hi} & be.3SG DECL / it was & Genesis 1:13; Matthew 1:1 &
stable with caveat & bundles copular \tdim{ahi} and clause-final
\tdim{hi} \\
\tdim{lo hi} & NEG DECL & Genesis 4:5; Matthew 2:10 & stable with caveat
& overlaps with the negation system \\

caveat & not expanded into a full mood chapter \\

}

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{Declarative \tdim{hi} with copula
overlap}{Declarative  with copula overlap}}\label{declarative-with-copula-overlap}

Clause-final \tdim{hi} is safest here when discussed through
\tdim{ahi hi} \glossquote{be.3SG DECL / it was}. The section therefore
keeps copular overlap explicit and does not treat every \tdim{hi} token
as a settled sentence-final declarative marker.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:sfp-ahi-hi-gen1-13}
\gll \tdimword{àhì} \tdimword{hì} \\
     be.\textsc{3sg} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{it was} (Genesis 1:13)
\end{exe}

Matthew 1:1 gives a Gospel counterpart in the same
copula-plus-declarative frame.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:sfp-ahi-hi-matt1-1}
\gll \tdimword{híh} \tdimword{bàng} \tdimword{àhì} \tdimword{hì} \\
     \textsc{prox} like be.\textsc{3sg} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{it is thus / this is how it is} (Matthew 1:1)
\end{exe}

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{Negative-plus-declarative
\tdim{lo hi}}{Negative-plus-declarative }}\label{negative-plus-declarative}

\tdim{Lo hi} \glossquote{NEG DECL} is treated as a
negation-plus-declarative overlap point. This section keeps the overlap
visible and does not reopen the full negation account.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:sfp-lo-hi-gen4-5}
\gll \tdimword{thusim} \tdimword{lò} \tdimword{hì} \\
     parable \textsc{neg} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{it was not accepted} (Genesis 4:5)
\end{exe}

Matthew 2:10 gives a Gospel clause with the same \tdim{lo hi} pattern.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:sfp-lo-hi-matt2-10}
\gll \tdimword{ki-gēn} \tdimword{théi} \tdimword{lò} \tdimword{hì} \\
     conversation know \textsc{neg} \textsc{decl} \\
\glt \glossquote{it cannot be fully told} (Matthew 2:10)
\end{exe}

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{Optative or jussive
\tdim{hen}}{Optative or jussive }}\label{optative-or-jussive}

\tdim{Hen} \glossquote{jussive / optative} is included for controlled
optative-like clause-final usage. The section keeps this narrow and does
not widen it into a full mood system.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:sfp-hen-gen1-3}
\gll \tdimword{Khuavak} \tdimword{ōm} \tdimword{hèn} \\
     light exist \textsc{juss} \\
\glt \glossquote{let there be light} (Genesis 1:3)
\end{exe}

Matthew 6:9 gives a Gospel jussive prayer frame with the same
clause-final \tdim{hen}.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:sfp-hen-matt6-9}
\gll \tdimword{nà} \tdimword{mīn} \tdimword{sìangtho} \tdimword{kī-zahtak} \tdimword{hèn} \\
     \textsc{2sg} name holy \textsc{refl}-honor \textsc{juss} \\
\glt \glossquote{hallowed be your name} (Matthew 6:9)
\end{exe}

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{Imperative \tdim{in} and
\tdim{un}}{Imperative  and }}\label{imperative-and}

\tdim{In}
\glossquote{imperative / second-person singular imperative boundary} and
\tdim{un} \glossquote{plural imperative} are both visible in controlled
clauses. For \tdim{in}, the case-like analyzer/export overlap remains
explicit. For \tdim{un}, the clause-final imperative plural use is
cleaner.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:sfp-in-gen6-14}
\gll \tdimword{teembaw} \tdimword{khàt} \tdimword{bāwl} \tdimword{ìn} \\
     ark one make \textsc{erg} \\
\glt \glossquote{make an ark} (Genesis 6:14)
\end{exe}

Luke 23:34 keeps the same imperative boundary with \tdim{in} in Gospel
speech.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:sfp-in-luke23-34}
\gll \tdimword{à} \tdimword{màwh-ná} \tdimword{ùh} \tdimword{nà} \tdimword{mái-sàk} \tdimword{ìn} \\
     \textsc{3sg} err-\textsc{nmlz} 2/3PL \textsc{2sg} face-\textsc{caus} \textsc{erg} \\
\glt \glossquote{forgive their sin} (Luke 23:34)
\end{exe}

Psalms 100:1 provides the current controlled Old Testament
plural-imperative \tdim{un} anchor.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:sfp-un-ps100-1}
\gll \tdimword{ging-sàk} \tdimword{ùn} \\
     sound-\textsc{caus} \textsc{imp}.\textsc{pl} \\
\glt \glossquote{make a joyful noise} (Psalms 100:1)
\end{exe}

Matthew 3:3 gives a Gospel plural-imperative counterpart with
clause-final \tdim{un}.

\begin{exe}
\ex \label{ex:sfp-un-matt3-3}
\gll \tdimword{lam-pì} \tdimword{tāng-sàk} \tdimword{ùn} \\
     road give-\textsc{caus} \textsc{imp}.\textsc{pl} \\
\glt \glossquote{make his paths straight} (Matthew 3:3)
\end{exe}

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\tdim{Hiam} as interrogatives
overlap}{ as interrogatives overlap}}\label{as-interrogatives-overlap}

\tdim{Hiam} \glossquote{question particle} remains a cross-reference
boundary with interrogatives. The clause \tdim{Hihte kua ahi hiam?}
(Genesis 48:8) stays useful as overlap control, but this section does
not reopen independent \tdim{hiam} analysis.

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\tdim{Aw} as vocative or exclamative
boundary}{ as vocative or exclamative boundary}}\label{as-vocative-or-exclamative-boundary}

\tdim{Aw} \glossquote{vocative / exclamative} remains boundary material
here. \tdim{Gam khempeuh aw} \glossquote{all lands!} (Psalms 100:1) and
\tdim{David suan Josef aw} \glossquote{Joseph, son of David!} (Matthew
1:20) show vocative force, but they do not yet justify a broader
sentence-final mood claim.

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\tdim{Tahen}, \tdim{ta}, and \tdim{zo} as
deferred TAM-overlap
material}{, , and  as deferred TAM-overlap material}}\label{and-as-deferred-tam-overlap-material}

\tdim{Tahen} \glossquote{deferred jussive-looking form} remains
unresolved because fused \tdim{tahen} and split \tdim{ta hen} material
are still noisy in the current evidence. \tdim{Ta}
\glossquote{perfective / change-of-state boundary} remains boundary
material where clause-final \tdim{ta hi} overlaps TAM. \tdim{Zo}
\glossquote{completive boundary} remains deferred where lexical/export
ambiguity is still visible.

\subsubsection{Deferred material}\label{deferred-material-10}

Several issues remain outside the present account.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Broader interactions among sentence-final particles, clause type, and
  discourse structure remain outside this section.
\item
  The present section does not settle the full relation between
  sentence-final material and TAM or negation.
\item
  Further treatment of rare or noisy clause-final forms remains deferred
  until cleaner controlled evidence is available.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Coordinators}\label{coordinators}

The current coordinators evidence is strongest for NP coordination with
\tdim{le} \glossquote{and (NP coordinator)}, while \tdim{leh}
\glossquote{if / when}, sequential \tdim{a}
\glossquote{sequential linker with 3SG/FUNC overlap}, and \tdim{mawh}
\glossquote{deferred disjunction material} remain boundary or deferred
material, and \tdim{Ahih hangin} \glossquote{but / however} plus
\tdim{ahih kei leh} \glossquote{otherwise / if not} remain caveated
connector patterns.

\subsubsection{Overview of coordinators in
Tedim}\label{overview-of-coordinators-in-tedim}

This section keeps coordination narrowly constructional. \tdim{le}
\glossquote{and (NP coordinator)} is the clean noun-phrase anchor, while
\tdim{leh} \glossquote{if / when} and sequential \tdim{a}
\glossquote{sequential linker with 3SG/FUNC overlap} stay
boundary-controlled. \tdim{mawh}
\glossquote{deferred disjunction material} remains deferred.
\tdim{Ahih hangin} \glossquote{but / however} and \tdim{ahih kei leh}
\glossquote{otherwise / if not} are treated as caveated connector
material \citep{henderson1965, zamngaihcing2017}. This section does not
present a full coordination, subordination, converb, or discourse
system.

\subsubsection{Coordinator inventory}\label{coordinator-inventory}

{\def\LTcaptype{none} % do not increment counter

  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}

  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}

\midrule\noalign{}

\endlastfoot
\tdim{le} & NP coordination & \tdim{vantung le leitung};
\tdim{Van le lei} & core & limited to clean NP coordination claims \\
\tdim{leh} & conditional / boundary linker &
\tdim{veilam na lak leh kei taklamah ka pai ding hi} & boundary & do not
flatten conditional \tdim{leh} into simple clause conjunction \\
\tdim{a} & sequential linker &
\tdim{luang a tua mun panin gun hong kikhenin} & boundary & export
overlap with agreement/function \tdim{a} \\
\tdim{mawh} & disjunction / alternative-question row & \tdim{mawh} &
